Obstructions to Erdős-Pósa Dualities for Minors Thanks: All authors were supported by the French-German Collaboration ANR/DFG Project UTMA (ANR-20-CE92-0027). The third author was also supported by the Franco-Norwegian project PHC AURORA 2024 (Projet n° 51260WL). The last author was also supported by the Institute for Basic Science (IBS-R029-C1).
Christophe Paul, Evangelos Protopapas, Dimitrios M. Thilikos, Sebastian Wiederrecht

Abstract
Let 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and ℋ \mathcal{H} be minor-closed graph classes. We say that the pair ( ℋ , 𝒢 ) (\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}) is an Erdős-Pósa pair (EP-pair) if there exists a function f f such that for every k k and every graph G ∈ 𝒢 , G\in\mathcal{G}, either G G has k k pairwise vertex-disjoint subgraphs which do not belong to ℋ , \mathcal{H}, or there exists a set S ⊆ V ⁡ ( G ) S\subseteq V(G) of size at most f ⁡ ( k ) f(k) for which G − S ∈ ℋ . G-S\in\mathcal{H}. The classic result of Erdős and Pósa says that if ℱ \mathcal{F} is the class of forests, then ( ℱ , 𝒢 ) (\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}) is an EP-pair for all graph classes 𝒢 . \mathcal{G}. A minor-closed graph class 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is an EP-counterexample for ℋ \mathcal{H} if 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is minimal with the property that ( ℋ , 𝒢 ) (\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}) is not an EP-pair. In this paper, we prove that for every minor-closed graph class ℋ \mathcal{H} the set ℭ ℋ \mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}} of all EP-counterexamples for ℋ \mathcal{H} is finite . In particular, we provide a complete characterization of ℭ ℋ \mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}} for every ℋ \mathcal{H} and give a constructive upper bound on its size. We show that each class 𝒢 \mathcal{G} in ℭ ℋ \mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}} can be described as the set of all minors of some, suitably defined, sequence of grid-like graphs ⟨ 𝒲 k ⟩ k ∈ ℕ . \langle\mathscr{W}_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}. Moreover, each 𝒲 k \mathscr{W}_{k} admits a half-integral packing, i.e., k k copies of some H ∉ ℋ H\not\in\mathcal{H} where no vertex is used more than twice. This implies a complete delineation of the half-integrality threshold of the Erdős-Pósa property for minors and as a corollary, we obtain a constructive proof of Thomas’ conjecture on the half-integral Erdős-Pósa property for minors which was recently confirmed by Liu. Our results are algorithmic. Let h = h ⁡ ( ℋ ) h=h(\mathcal{H}) denote the maximum size of an obstruction to ℋ . \mathcal{H}. For every minor-closed graph class ℋ , \mathcal{H}, we construct an algorithm that, given a graph G G and an integer k , k, either outputs a half-integral packing of k k copies of some H ∉ ℋ H\not\in\mathcal{H} or outputs a set of at most 2 k 𝒪 h ​ ( 1 ) {2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}} vertices whose deletion creates a graph in ℋ \mathcal{H} in time 2 2 k 𝒪 h ​ ( 1 ) ⋅ | G | 4 ​ log ⁡ | G | . 2^{2^{{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}}}\cdot|G|^{4}\log|G|. Moreover, as a consequence of our results, for every minor-closed class ℋ , \mathcal{H}, we obtain min-max-dualities, which may be seen as analogues of the celebrated Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour, for the recently introduced parameters ℋ \mathcal{H} -treewidth and elimination distance to ℋ . \mathcal{H}.



Keywords:
Erdős-Pósa dualities; Graph parameters; Graph minors; Treewidth, Elimination distance; Universal Obstructions; Parametric graphs; Decomposition theorems

Mathematics Subject Classification:
05C83; 05C85; 05C10; 05C75, 68R10

1
Introduction

Min-max dualities like Menger’s Theorem or the related Max-Flow/Min-Cut Theorem are among the most powerful results in discrete mathematics, usually having strong algorithmic consequences.
In many cases, exact dualities like the ones above do not exist, however, allowing for some gap may yield approximate results.
Two emblematic theorems of this type in structural graph theory are the Erdős-Pósa Theorem for hitting and packing cycles
[
19
]
and the Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour which ties treewidth to the existence of large and highly connected planar minors
[
62
]
.
In this paper we generalize both results above, maintaining their deep connection, to arbitrary minor-closed graph classes
\mathcal{H}.

Regarding the former, we determine
all
minor-closed classes
\mathcal{G}
where, for each of its members, the absence of a small hitting set for the obstructions of
\mathcal{H}
implies the presence of many disjoint such obstructions.
Moreover, regarding the second, we prove that any graph where the parameter “
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth” is large must contain a certificate for this in the form of a highly regular grid-like minor containing many “highly entangled” obstructions to
\mathcal{H}.

1.1
Erdős-Pósa dualities for graph minors

We begin with a general introduction to Erdős-Pósa-type dualities together with some basic notation for the presentation of our results.

Minors.

A graph
Z
is a
minor
of a graph
G
if
Z
can be obtained from a subgraph of
G
by contracting edges.
This induces a partial order on the class of all graphs which we denote by
\leq.

A graph class
\mathcal{G}
is
minor-closed
if it contains all minors of its elements and it is
proper
if it does not contain all graphs.
The
obstruction set

{\sf obs}(\mathcal{G})
of a minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{G}
is the set of
\leq
-minimal graphs not in
\mathcal{G}.

We refer to the graphs in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{G})
as the
obstructions
of
\mathcal{G}.

Due to the main result of the Graph Minors series by Robertson and Seymour
[
66
]
we know that
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{G})
is a
finite
set for all minor-closed graph classes
\mathcal{G}.

Apices and barriers.

Given a graph class
\mathcal{H},
an
\mathcal{H}
-modulator
of a graph
G
is a subset of vertices of
G
whose deletion yields a graph in
\mathcal{H}.

The
\mathcal{H}
-apex
number of
G,
denoted by
{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(G),
is defined as the minimum size of an
\mathcal{H}
-modulator in
G.

When
\mathcal{H}
is a minor-closed graph class, the study of this parameter includes the proof of constructive bounds, as a function in
k,
for the number of obstructions of the graph class
\{G\mid{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k\}

[
2
,
21
]
as well as fixed-parameter algorithms for deciding whether a graph belongs to
\{G\mid{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k\},
parameterized by
k

[
50
,
41
,
33
,
36
,
71
,
72
,
73
]
.
Instances of this problem include the classic case where
\mathcal{H}
is the class of all forests
[
15
,
8
,
30
,
49
]
as well as other classes with “good” algorithmic properties such as planar graphs
[
36
,
33
]
and graphs of bounded genus
[
41
]
, that have attracted particular interest.

As an attempt to introduce an “obstructing measure” for a graph
G
to belong to some graph class
\mathcal{H},
we consider a collection
\mathcal{B}
of subgraphs of
G,
each “obstructing” membership to
\mathcal{H}.

When
\mathcal{H}
is minor-closed, every member of
\mathcal{B}
should contain a common
1
1

1

One might alternatively ask for minor-containment of different elements of
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{H})
; however this would not define a parameter that is functionally different, as
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
is finite by the Robertson-Seymour Theorem.
element of
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{H})
as a minor.
If the subgraphs in
\mathcal{B}
are pairwise vertex disjoint we call
\mathcal{B}
an
integral
\mathcal{H}
-barrier
(or
\mathcal{H}
-barrier for short).
If every vertex of
G
belongs to at most two members of
\mathcal{B},
then
\mathcal{B}
is a
half-integral
\mathcal{H}
-barrier
.
We may turn this notion into a maximization parameter
{\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
(resp.
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\text{-}{\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
) by defining it as the maximum size of an
\mathcal{H}
-barrier (resp. half-integral
\mathcal{H}\text{-}{\sf barrier}
) of
G.

For example, in the case where
\mathcal{H}
is the class of forests, an integral
\mathcal{H}
-barrier of
G
is a set of pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles in
G
(notice that each cycle contains
K_{3}\in\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})=\{K_{3}\}
as a minor).

Erdős-Pósa dualities.

If
G
contains an
\mathcal{H}
-barrier
\mathcal{B},
then every
\mathcal{H}
-modulator is forced to contain at least one vertex from each member of
\mathcal{B}.

Therefore,
{\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(G).

This holds for all possible choices of
\mathcal{H}
and
G.

Similarly, one may observe that
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\text{-}{\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(G).

The question of duality is whether the reverse inequalities hold approximately:

Under which assumptions does the absence of a large (half-integral)
\mathcal{H}
-barrier imply the existence of a small
\mathcal{H}
-modulator?

Providing a precise and complete answer to the “
when
” but also to the “
why
” of the question above is the objective of this paper.

In order to discuss this objective formally, let us present a unified way to express Erdős-Pósa type questions and results.
Let
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
be two minor-closed graph classes.
We refer to
\mathcal{H}
as the
target class
and
\mathcal{G}
as the
environment class
.
The pair
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is an
Erdős-Pósa pair
, or
EP-pair
for short, if there is a function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},
called the
gap function
, such that, for every graph
G\in\mathcal{G},
it holds that
{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq f({\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)).

The concept of
EP-pair
allows us to express
all
possible Erdős-Pósa dualities for the minor relation and thereby formalize the “when”-part of the question above as follows

Which pairs
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
are EP-pairs and what are their gap functions?

(1)

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{all}}
denote the class of all graphs and let
\mathcal{F}
be the class of all forests.
The seminal theorem of Erdős and Pósa
[
19
]
initiated research for Erdős-Pósa dualities and motivated their name. It can be reformulated as:

(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}})
is an EP-pair whose gap function belongs to
\mathcal{O}(k\log k).

(2)

Robertson and Seymour, as a consequence of their celebrated Grid Theorem, proved the following:
[
62
]

(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}})
is an EP-pair if and only if
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{H})
contains a planar graph.

(3)

The proof of (
3
) identifies candidates for graph families that indicate an answer to the “why”-part of (
1
): they are wall-like structures representing the graphs embeddable in non-spherical surfaces such as the projective plane or the torus (see
Figure 1
).
Cames van Batenburg, Huynh, Joret, and Raymond
[
11
]
proved that for every EP-pair
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{all}})
the gap function
2
2

2

Given two functions
\chi,\psi\colon\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N},
we write
\chi(n)=\mathcal{O}_{x}(\psi(n))
to denote that there exists a computable function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that
\chi(n)=\mathcal{O}(f(x)\cdot\psi(n)).

Moreover, we write
\chi(n)=\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}_{x}(\psi(n))
to denote that there exists a function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\chi(n)=f(x)n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}.
is
\mathcal{O}_{h}(k\log k)
where
h\coloneqq h(\mathcal{H})
denotes the maximum size of a member of
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H}),
thereby matching the bound of (
2
).

Using
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\text{-}{\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
instead of
{\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
in the discussion above yields the concept of an
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\text{-}
Erdős-Pósa pair
, or a
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\text{-}
EP-pair
for short.
In a major breakthrough result, Liu
[
47
]
proved
3
3

3

A proof of Thomas’ Conjecture was announced by Norin in 2010 at the SIAM Conference on Discrete Mathematics but it was never published
[
54
]
. The proof we present in this paper is independent of both Liu’s and Norin’s work.
the conjecture of Thomas (see
[
35
]
) stating the following:

(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}})
is a
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
-EP-pair for all proper minor-closed graph classes
\mathcal{H}.

(4)

A major drawback of Liu’s result is that it does neither provide explicit bounds on the gap function for (
4
), nor does it provide a constructive way to obtain either the
\mathcal{H}
-modulator, or the half-integral
\mathcal{H}
-barrier.
For this reason, algorithmic applications of (
4
) are not immediate.
Pursuing a constructive version of Liu’s result appears to be a crucial step in the further development of the algorithmic aspects of Graph Minors series and its applications.

The Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour
[
62
]
implies that the target graph classes
\mathcal{H}
which together with
\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}
form EP-pairs are exactly those of bounded treewidth
4
4

4

A definition of treewidth can be found in
Section 11
.
.
This leads to the following variant of (
1
):

Given a minor-closed class
\mathcal{H}
of unbounded treewidth, what is the most general environment class
\mathcal{G}
such that
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is an EP-pair?

Counterexamples to the Erdős-Pósa property.

To answer the question above, we define
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
as the collection of all graph classes
\mathcal{G}
for which
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is not an EP-pair, while
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
is an EP-pair for every minor-closed class
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
properly contained in
\mathcal{G}.

In other words,
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
contains all environment classes that are subset-minimal counterexamples for being an EP-pair with
\mathcal{H}
as the target class.
Observe that, by (
3
),
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}=\emptyset
if and only if
\mathcal{H}
has bounded treewidth.

We wish to stress the “second order” nature of the collection of graph classes
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}.

An order-theoretic perspective.

With respect to set containment, we may think of
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
as an
obstruction
for a minor-closed environment class
\mathcal{G}
to form an EP-pair with
\mathcal{H}.
Indeed, if
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}

is the collection of all environment minor-closed graph classes that form EP-pairs with the target minor-closed class
\mathcal{H},
then

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
contains the
\subseteq
-minimal minor-closed graph classes that do not belong in
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}.
In this way,
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
can be seen as a
second-order obstruction
for the collection of classes
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}}.

Therefore, providing a characterization of
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
is a way to describe
all
possible Erdős-Pósa duality theorems for minor-closed graph classes.
The fact that the collection
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
exists and is countable for every
\mathcal{H}
is a consequence of the fact that
(\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}},\leq)
is a WQO, as proven by Robertson and Seymour
[
66
]
.
Indeed, this follows by the fact that every graph class in
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
is minor-closed and thereby is characterized by a finite set of obstructions.
However, since
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
is a
collection
of graph classes, WQO for the minor relation does not imply that
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
is finite.
In fact, the finiteness of
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}},
for all
\mathcal{H},
would follow immediately from the stronger conjecture that
(\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}},\leq)
is an “
\omega^{2}
-WQO”
[
55
]
.
This conjecture, as well as the more general one that
(\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}},\leq)
is a Better Quasi Ordering (BQO) remain central open problem in order theory
[
57
]
.
Unfortunately, proving these conjectures seems to be a “hopelessly difficult problem”, as mentioned by Robertson, Seymour, and Thomas in
[
69
]
.
Moreover, it is unlikely that if such a proof ever appears, it is a constructive one.
Even a constructive proof that
(\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}},\leq)
is a WQO appears to be out of reach
[
24
,
45
,
20
]
.

An alternative way to obtain explicit bounds for the gap function would be a constructive proof for Erdős-Pósa-type dualities.
This would constitute the basis of an algorithmic theory.
For these reasons, to tackle such theorems, it seems imperative to study the collections
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
explicitly.

Our results.

In this paper we fully characterize the collection
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
and prove that it is
finite
.

A
parametric graph
is a minor-monotone sequence
\mathscr{G}=\langle\mathscr{G}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
of graphs, i.e.for every
t\in\mathbb{N},

\mathscr{G}_{t}\leq\mathscr{G}_{t+1}.

As we shall see, the concept of parametric graph is central to the characterization of the collection
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}},
for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}.

Indeed, for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},
we construct a
finite
collection of parametric graphs
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}=\{\mathscr{F}^{\mathcal{H},1},\ldots,\mathscr{F}^{\mathcal{H},r}\},

r\coloneqq r(\mathcal{H}),
consisting of “wall-like” parametric graphs, hereafter called
walloids
,
and prove that, every class in
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
is the set of all minors of some parametric graph from
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}.

In particular, we prove the following.

Theorem 1.1

(Main result)
.

For every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
and every minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{G},

(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is an EP-pair if and only if the class
\mathcal{G}
excludes a member of
\mathscr{F},
for every
\mathscr{F}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}.

Notice that
Theorem 1.1
indeed provides an answer to the “when” part of our leading question (
1
): the absence of a large
\mathcal{H}
-barrier in a graph
G\in\mathcal{G}
implies the existence of a small
\mathcal{H}
-modulator if and only if
\mathcal{G}
excludes a graph for each of the classes generated by the parametric graphs in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}.

An important feature of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
(see the definition in (
9
)) is the following.

Observation 1.2
.

For every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},
every
\langle\mathscr{F}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}},
and every
t\in\mathbb{N}
we have that
{\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathscr{F}_{t})=1.

In light of
Observation 1.2
, the “why” part is answered precisely because the parametric graphs in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
are those where it is impossible to pack two copies of some member of
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H}),
while, with increasing
t,
they require larger and larger
\mathcal{H}
-modulators.
In this sense, they indeed act as proper counterexamples to the Erdős-Pósa property in the exact same way as the surface-based examples of Robertson and Seymour
[
62
]
.

For each proper minor-closed class
\mathcal{H},
the set
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
is constructed using the obstructions of
\mathcal{H}
as a starting point.
We postpone the description of this construction to
Subsection 1.5
.
We stress however that for every class
\mathcal{H}
of bounded treewidth, as
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}=\emptyset,
we have that
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}=\emptyset.

As a first non-trivial example, let us consider
\mathcal{H}
to be a minor-closed class such that
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
contains no planar graphs and at least one single-crossing minor
5
5

5

A graph is a
single-crossing minor
if it is a minor of some graph that can be drawn in the plane with at most one crossing.
.
It turns out that
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
is composed of the two parametric graphs that are depicted in
Figure 1
6
6

6

In
[
56
]
, we have proven, among others, that the parametric graphs depicted in
Figure 1
are the two parametric graphs in
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
for every minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
whose all obstructions are non-planar and at least one of them is single-crossing minor. The result of the first paper only applies to a restricted collection of classes
\mathcal{H}
whose obstruction set contains at least one graph that belongs to a
specific
proper subclass of
K_{8}
-minor-free graphs. We explain this in more detail in
Section 13
.
.

Figure 1
:
Two wall-like graphs in
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
when
\mathcal{H}
is the class of
K_{3,3}
-minor free graphs.

This implies that
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
is composed of the class of all toroidal graphs and the class of all projective planar graphs.
This generalizes (
3
), by replacing the condition that the target graph class has a planar obstruction, to the condition that the target graph class
\mathcal{H}
has obstructions that are all non-planar and that at least one of them is a single-crossing minor, obtaining that, in this case,
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is an EP-pair if and only if
\mathcal{G}
excludes some toroidal and some projective planar graph.

In fact, for every class
\mathcal{H}
whose obstruction set does not contain a planar graph,
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
contains a parametric graph representing some orientable surface other than the sphere and a parametric graph representing a non-orientable surface.
However, in general not all members of
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
correspond to surfaces.
As we shall see in
Subsection 1.5
, the members of
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
may be arbitrarily far away from being surface-embeddable. A wide family of
\mathcal{H}
’s where

\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
indeed corresponds to surfaces is studied in
[
56
]
.

Finally, we wish to stress that our results yield two additional insights.
The first is that

|\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}|=|\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}|\in 2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell(h^{2}))}}
for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},

(5)

where
\ell(\cdot)
is the so-called
unique linkage function
(see
[
67
,
40
,
51
,
3
,
26
]
and
Proposition 3.3
) and
h\coloneqq h(\mathcal{H})
is the maximum size of a member of
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H}).

The second resides on following important feature of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
: each parametric graph
\mathscr{F}=\langle\mathscr{F}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
consists of wall-like graphs that contain a half-integral
\mathcal{H}\text{-}{\sf barrier}
whose size increases as a function in their index
t
(see
Lemma 11.7
).
Likewise, the wall-like objects that “obstruct” the integral Erdős-Pósa duality (see
Theorem 1.1
), do serve as certificates of the
half-integral
Erdős-Pósa duality.
As a corollary of this, we obtain a constructive proof of Thomas’ conjecture which implies that the gap function of (
4
) is bounded by
{2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}}.

1.2
Min-max dualities and a high-level overview of our approach

At its core, our approach is very close to the spirit of the Graph Minor series of Robertson and Seymour.
That is, we do not immediately obtain
Theorem 1.1
.
Instead, we take a detour and first describe the structure of graphs that “locally” exclude a large (half-integral)
\mathcal{H}
-barrier.

The Grid Theorem
[
62
]
states that there exists a function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every graph
G
and every integer
k,
if
G
has treewidth at least
f(k)
then
G
contains a
k
-wall
7
7

7

See
Subsection 2.4
for the definition of walls.
as a subgraph.
For this we need two ingredients.
The first is a description of what “local” with respect to a wall means.
Let
k
be a positive integer,
W
be a
k
-wall in a graph
G
and
S\subseteq V(G)
be a set of
strictly less
than
k
vertices.
Then there exists at least one pair of a horizontal and a vertical path of
W
which avoids
S.

Notice that all possible choices of such pairs belong to the same connected component of
G-S.

We say that this component
contains the majority of
G-S
8
8

8

For the reader familiar with the theory of graph minors it is probably apparent that this notion does indeed define a tangle. However, for our purposes it suffices to deal with tangles only in this implicit fashion.
.
The second ingredient is another collection of parametric graphs whose explicit definition we postpone to
Subsection 1.5
.
For every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},
we will describe a finite collection of parametric graphs
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}=\{\mathscr{W}^{\mathcal{H},1},\dots,\mathscr{W}^{\mathcal{H},w}\},
where
w=w(\mathcal{H})
is a constant depending only on the class
\mathcal{H},
consisting of “wall-like” parametric graphs which have, among others, the following three important properties:

1.

if, for some
\mathscr{W}=\langle\mathscr{W}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}},
there does not exist
q\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathscr{W}_{q}
contains two disjoint copies of some member of
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
as a minor, then
\mathscr{W}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}},

2.

there exists
f^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
\mathscr{W}=\langle W_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}\setminus\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and every
k\in\mathbb{N},

\mathscr{W}_{{f^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}}(k)}
contains an
\mathcal{H}
-barrier of size
k,
and

3.

there exists a function
f^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
i\in[w]
and every positive integer
k,

\mathscr{W}^{\mathcal{H},i}_{f^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}(k)}
contains a half-integral
\mathcal{H}
-barrier of size
k
and an
f^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}(k)
-wall such that the wall and the barrier cannot be separated by fewer than
k
vertices.

At the heart of our proof sits the following “local structure theorem”.
We present here an equivalent but slightly simplified version of
Theorem 9.2
which avoids a lot of the heavier notations and technical definitions.

Theorem 1.3

(Local structure theorem (simplified))
.

For every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
there exist functions
f^{1},f^{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every positive integer
k
and every graph
G,
if
W
is a
f^{1}(k)
-wall in
G,
then

•

there exists a parametric graph
\mathscr{W}=\langle\mathscr{W}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
G
contains a subdivision
W^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
and
W^{\prime}
cannot be separated from
W
by fewer than
k
vertices, or

•

there exists a set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
f^{2}(k)<f^{1}(k)
such that the component of
G-S
which contains the majority of the degree-
3
-vertices of
W-S
belong to
\mathcal{H}.

With
Theorem 1.3
we have a powerful tool at our disposal which allows us to describe any graph
G
which excludes a member of every parametric graph in
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
in terms of a
tree decomposition
into pieces that are either small or belong to
\mathcal{H}.

This type of behavior is very reminiscent of the Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour
[
62
]
, but also of their general structure theorem for
H
-minor-free graphs
[
65
]
, and it is similar to other, more recent results such as the one in
[
76
]
.
To provide a description of this intermediate result and how it leads to
Theorem 1.1
, we require some additional definitions.
In the following we describe, roughly, how all of our results reduce to
Theorem 1.3
.

A (very) brief outline of the proof of
Theorem 1.3
is given in
Subsection 1.4
, for a slightly more in-depth discussion and overview of the proof, including several of the more technical definitions we postponed so far, we refer the reader to
Subsection 1.5
and
Subsection 1.6
.

Parametric families.

Let
\mathscr{G}=\langle\mathscr{G}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}=\langle\mathscr{G}_{t}^{\prime}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
be two parametric graphs.
We write
\mathscr{G}\lesssim\mathscr{G}^{\prime},
if there is some function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathscr{G}_{t}\leq\mathscr{G}_{f(t)}^{\prime}
for all
t\in\mathbb{N}.

If
\mathscr{G}\lesssim\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\lesssim\mathscr{G}
we say that
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
are
equivalent
, denoted by
\mathscr{G}\equiv\mathscr{G}^{\prime}.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
be parametric families.
We say that
\mathfrak{G}\lesssim^{*}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
if for every
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
there is some
\mathscr{G}\in\mathfrak{G}
such that
\mathscr{G}\lesssim\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
9
9

9

This “lifting” of the relation
\lesssim
on parametric graphs to families of parametric graphs is called the
Smyth extension
of
\lesssim
in the theory of WQOs.
.
Moreover,
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
are said to be
equivalent
if
\mathfrak{G}\lesssim^{*}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\lesssim^{*}\mathfrak{G},
this is denoted by
\mathfrak{G}\equiv^{*}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}.

A parametric family
\mathfrak{G}
is an
antichain
if for every pair
\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{G}
with
\mathscr{G}\neq\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
it holds that
\mathscr{G}\not\lesssim\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\not\lesssim\mathscr{G}.

If
\mathfrak{G}
is a parametric family, we say that a subset
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{G}
is a
minimization
of
\mathfrak{G}
if
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\equiv^{*}\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is an antichain.
Notice that all minimizations of
\mathfrak{G}
are pairwise equivalent.
This permits us to use
\mathsf{min}(\mathfrak{G})
to denote an arbitrarily chosen minimization of
\mathfrak{G}.

Graph parameters and universal obstructions.

A graph parameter is a function
{\sf p}\colon\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}\to\mathbb{N}
mapping graphs to non-negative integers.
Given two graph parameters
{\sf p}
and
\mathsf{q},
we write
{\sf p}\preceq\mathsf{q}
if there is a function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
{\sf p}(G)\leq f(\mathsf{q}(G))
for every graph
G.

We refer to
f
as the
gap function
of this relation.
It is not hard to observe that
\preceq
is a quasi-ordering on the set of graph parameters.
We also say that
{\sf p}
and
\mathsf{q}
are
equivalent
with gap function
f,
denoted by
{\sf p}\sim\mathsf{q},
if
{\sf p}\preceq\mathsf{q}
and
\mathsf{q}\preceq{\sf p}
where
f
is a gap function witnessing both relations.
Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a finite set of graph parameters.
We define
\max(\mathfrak{P})
as the parameter
{\sf p}(G)\coloneqq\max\{\mathsf{q}(G)\mid\mathsf{q}\in\mathfrak{P}\}.

Given a parametric graph
\mathscr{G}=\langle\mathscr{G}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}},
we define the graph parameter
{\sf p}_{\mathscr{G}}\colon\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}\to\mathbb{N}

to be

\displaystyle{\sf p}_{\mathscr{G}}(G)\coloneqq\max\{t\mid\mathscr{G}_{t}\leq G\}.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a parametric family.
We define
{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{G}}(G)\coloneqq\max(\{{\sf p}_{\mathscr{G}}\mid\mathscr{G}\in\mathfrak{G}\}).

One may now observe that
\mathfrak{G}^{1}\lesssim^{*}\mathfrak{G}^{2}
if and only if
{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{G}^{1}}\preceq{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{G}^{2}}.

We say that a parametric family
\mathfrak{G}
is an
obstructing set
for a graph parameter
{\sf p}
if
{\sf p}\sim{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{G}}.

Moreover,
\mathfrak{G}
is a
universal obstruction
for
{\sf p}
if it is an obstructing set for
{\sf p}
and a
\lesssim
-antichain.
Observe that
\mathfrak{G}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
are both obstructing sets for the same graph parameter if and only if
\mathfrak{G}_{1}\equiv^{*}\mathfrak{G}_{2}.

With these notions at hand, we are now ready to describe how our approach yields a far-reaching generalization of the Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour.
Indeed, as we will see, the parameter
\mathsf{p}_{\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}}
is equivalent to a generalization of treewidth which has received considerable attention in recent years, specifically by the algorithmic community.

\mathcal{H}
-treewidth.

Given a graph
G
and a set
X\subseteq V(G),
we define
\mathsf{torso}(G,X)
as the graph obtained from
G[X]
by turning, for each component
K
of
G-X,
the set
N_{G}(V(K))
(i.e., the neighbors of the component in
X
) into a clique.
Given a class
\mathcal{H},
the parameter
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth
, denoted by
\mathcal{H}
-
{\sf tw},
is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G)\coloneqq\min\big\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mid

\displaystyle\text{\ there exists }X\subseteq V(G)\text{ such that }\mathsf{tw}(\mathsf{torso}(G,X))\leq k\text{ and}

(6)

\displaystyle\text{ every component of }G-X\text{ belongs to }\mathcal{H}\big\}

Here
\mathsf{tw}(G)
denotes the
treewidth
of the graph
G
(see
Section 11
for a definition).

The above definition was proposed by Eiben, Ganian, Hamm, and Kwon
[
18
]
as a natural extension of treewidth that measures the tree decomposability of a graph into pieces belonging to some class
\mathcal{H}
and has interesting algorithmic applications (see
[
18
,
31
,
4
,
5
,
32
,
29
,
53
]
).
A direct consequence of the Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour is that
\mathcal{H}
-
\mathsf{tw}\sim{\sf tw}
if and only if
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
contains a planar graph (see
Observation 11.2
).

Our first core result is that, for every
\mathcal{H},

\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
is an obstructing set for
\mathcal{H}
-
{\sf tw}.

For this, we need two results.
The first is the following.

Theorem 1.4
.

For every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},
every parametric graph
\mathscr{W}=\langle\mathscr{W}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}},
and every
t\in\mathbb{N}
it holds that
\mathcal{H}\text{-}{\sf tw}(\mathscr{W}_{t})\in\Omega_{h}(t).

The proof of
Theorem 1.4
is based on an appropriate adaptation of the notion of
brambles
to the concept of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth.
Brambles are known to act as lower bounds to treewidth
[
59
]
.
For the notion of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth we adopt the following definition.
A
strict
10
10

10

Notice that the original notion of brambles (see for example
[
61
]
) allows for two bramble elements to be disjoint if they are joined by an edge.
While we believe that such a (more relaxed) definition of
\mathcal{H}
-brambles could yield even tighter duality results, strict
\mathcal{H}
-brambles occur more naturally within our proofs.

\mathcal{H}
-bramble in a graph
G
is a collection
\mathcal{B}
of connected subgraphs of
G
which pairwise intersect such that
\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{H}=\emptyset.

The
order
of a strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble
\mathcal{H}
is the minimum size of a hitting set for all elements of
\mathcal{B}.

In
Figure 2
we provide a sketch of the construction of a strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble of large order in a member of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}.

The fact that the members of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
contain large order strict
\mathcal{H}
-brambles stems from their construction.
We show that for each
\langle\mathscr{W}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
there exists some constant
c=c(\mathcal{H})
such that
\mathscr{W}_{c}
contains a member of
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
as a minor (see
Theorem 10.1
).
In fact, for increasing
t,

\mathscr{W}_{t}
contains a large half-integral
\mathcal{H}
-barrier consisting of such minors and this barrier also acts as the strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble.

Figure 2
:
A strict bramble of subgraphs, all obstructing membership to
\mathcal{H},
in a member
\mathscr{W}_{k}
of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}.

The blue strip at the bottom represents the large wall that constitutes the basis of
\mathscr{W}_{k}.

At the top of this wall, we attach gadgets with different properties.
These gadgets are, from left to right, two “handle segments” and two “crosscap segments” which model a surface.
What follows are several “flower segments”. These segments model parts of an obstruction
Z\in\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
which attach to the rest of the graph through few (that is less than three) vertices.
Each “flower segment” hosts
k
disjoint copies of the same part.
Finally, on the far right, there are additional “flower segments”.
These flowers still model parts of
Z,
but these parts are now allowed to attach to the rest of the graph via boundaries of size larger than three (although we will show that the boundaries are still of bounded size).

The gray area serves two purposes.
First, it illustrates one possible way
Z
might fit into
\mathscr{W}_{k}
as a minor.
Second, it indicates how to draw several copies of
Z
in a way that no vertex of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
is contained in more than two copies and that any two copies intersect. Such a family of copies of
Z,
embedded as minors into
\mathscr{W}_{k}
forms the strict bramble.
See
Figure 9
and
Lemma 11.7
for a more detailed description.

The proof that strict
\mathcal{H}
-brambles of large order act as lower bounds to
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth follows the line of fairly standard arguments (see
Lemma 11.3
).
The most technical part of this paper is the upper bound towards the parametric equivalence between
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}
and
\mathsf{p}_{\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}}.

This proof spans
Section 4
to
Section 10
.
The key step, which is the proof of
Theorem 1.3
, can be found in
Section 9
while the most involved and novel technical parts are confined to
Section 7
and
Section 8
.

Due to the wall-like nature of the parametric graphs in
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}},

Theorem 1.5
and
Theorem 1.4
provide a
grid theorem
for
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth for
every
proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}.

The final proof of
Theorem 1.5
utilizes
Theorem 1.3
together with known balanced separator arguments.

Theorem 1.5

(General grid theorem)
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{main_grid_general}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},
where
h=h(\mathcal{H}),
every graph
G,
and every
k\in\mathbb{N},
if
\mathcal{H}\text{-}{\sf tw}(G)\geq f(h,k),
then there exists
\langle\mathscr{W}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
G
contains a subdivision of
\mathscr{W}_{k}.

Moreover,
f_{\ref{main_grid_general}}(h,k)={2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}}.

As a direct corollary, we also obtain a similar statement for the set
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathsf{min}}
of universal obstructions.

Corollary 1.6
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{cor_grid_general}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},
where
h=h(\mathcal{H}),
every graph
G,
and every
k\in\mathbb{N},
if
\mathcal{H}\text{-}{\sf tw}(G)\geq f(h,k),
then there exists
\langle\mathscr{W}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{W}^{\mathsf{min}}_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
G
contains a subdivision of
\mathscr{W}_{k}.

From
\mathcal{H}\text{-}{\sf tw}
to
{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}.

To express the structural behavior of
\mathsf{apex}_{\mathcal{H}}
in the form of an equivalence between parameters we must consider the family
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and all possible
\mathcal{H}
-barriers.
We define (see (
10
) and the surrounding definitions)
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}
to be the union of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
and the parametric family
\{\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}_{Z}}\mid Z\in\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})\}
where
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}_{Z}}\coloneqq\langle t\cdot Z\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}.

With this, we obtain the following lower bound towards our desired parametric equivalence between the parameters
\mathsf{apex}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathsf{p}_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}}.

Observation 1.7
.

For every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
and every parametric graph
\langle\mathscr{W}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}
it holds that
{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathscr{W}_{t})=\Omega_{h}(t)
where
h\coloneqq h(\mathcal{H})
is the maximum size of a member of
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H}).

The less trivial direction of the equivalence between
{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}}
and, in particular, obtaining its single exponential gap function are mostly a matter of carefully combining our results on
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth with established techniques from the study of the Erdős-Pósa property for minors.

Theorem 1.8
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{thm_apex_upper_bound}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},
where
h=h(\mathcal{H}),
every graph
G,
and every
k\in\mathbb{N},
if
{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\geq f_{\ref{thm_apex_upper_bound}}(h,k),
then there exists
\langle\mathscr{F}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
G
contains
\mathscr{F}_{k}
as a minor.
Moreover,
f_{\ref{thm_apex_upper_bound}}(h,k)={2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}}.

The proof of
Theorem 1.8
makes use of the three core properties of the family
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
which allow, by
Theorem 1.5
to reduce to the case of bounded
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}.

This reduction goes as follows:
If we find a large enough member of one of the parametric graphs in
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
we are done.
Moreover, by combining the first and the second core property of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
we know that, whenever we find a large enough member of one of the parametric graphs in
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}\setminus\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}},
we have found a member of one of the parametric graphs in
\{\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}_{Z}}\mid Z\in\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})\}.

Hence, whenever
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth of some graph
G
is large enough, we immediately obtain that
\mathsf{p}_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}}(G)
is also large by
Theorem 1.5
.
It follows that the only instances we have to check are those where
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth is bounded.

From here, we are able to use standard arguments in two steps.
First, we show the theorem for every class, minor-excluding some
connected
graph
Z
(
Subsection 12.1
).
Roughly speaking, we employ the same recursive method as described by Diestel in his proof of (
3
) (see
[
13
]
).
This leaves open only cases where
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
contains disconnected graphs.
These cases need some additional arguments using the connected case as a basis (
Subsection 12.2
).

It is worthwhile to mention that the
mechanism
that allows us to deduce a finite obstructing set for
\mathsf{apex}_{\mathcal{H}}
appears far more universal.
In
Section 13
we discuss this approach in a more general setting and conjecture that our results are the basis for a whole family of similar results based on the knowledge of obstructing sets for certain parameters.
As a proof of concept, we apply this mechanism to the parameter
\mathcal{H}
-treedepth
(also known as
elimination distance to
\mathcal{H}
) in
Subsection 12.3
and obtain a finite obstructing set for every choice of a proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}.

This parameter was originally introduced by Bulian and Dawar
[
9
,
10
]
and has inspired its own avenue of research
[
18
,
31
,
4
,
5
,
32
,
29
,
53
]
.

A sketch of the proof of
Theorem 1.1
.

By the definition,
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}
is the union of two parametric families, namely
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\{\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}_{Z}}\mid Z\in\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})\}
where the latter is consisting exclusively of
\mathcal{H}
-barriers.
Now let
h=h(\mathcal{H})
be the maximum size of a member of
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
and let
\mathcal{G}
be a minor-closed graph class.
If there exists
\mathscr{F}=\langle\mathscr{F}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
\mathscr{F}_{t}\in\mathcal{G}
for every
t\in\mathbb{N},
then, by
Observation 1.2
and
Observation 1.7
, it follows that
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is not an Erdős-Pósa pair.
For the reverse direction, we may assume that for each
\mathscr{F}=\langle\mathscr{F}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}},
there exists some
t_{\mathscr{F}}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathscr{F}_{t}\notin\mathcal{G}
for all
t\geq t_{\mathscr{F}}.

Let
t^{\star}\coloneqq\max\{t_{\mathscr{F}}\mid\mathscr{F}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}\}.

It follows that, for every
k\in\mathbb{N},
if for any graph
G\in\mathcal{G}
it holds that
\mathsf{apex}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\geq f_{\ref{thm_apex_upper_bound}}(h,t^{\star}+1+k),
then, by
Theorem 1.8
,
G
must contain an
\mathcal{H}
-barrier of size
k.

Therefore,
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is indeed an Erdős-Pósa pair.

1.3
Algorithmic consequences

Let us address some important algorithmic consequences of our results along with subsequent open problems.

A constructive (approximation)
FPT
-algorithm for half-integral packing.

Our results imply, for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H},
the existence of an algorithm
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
that, given a graph
G
and an integer
k,
either outputs a half-integral
\mathcal{H}
-barrier of size
k
or an
\mathcal{H}
-modulator of size at most
2^{k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}}
in time
2^{2^{{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}}}\cdot|G|^{4}\log|G|.

Observe that
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
can be used as an
FPT
-approximation algorithm for the following problem, defined for every graph
Z.

Given a graph
Z,
we define
\mathsf{excl}(Z)
to be the class of all graphs that exclude
Z
as a minor.

Half-Integral
Z
-Packing

Input:

A graph
G
and a non-negative integer
k.

Question:

Does
G
contain a half-integral
\mathsf{excl}(Z)
-barrier of size
k
?

Due to the algorithmic consequences of the Robertson and Seymour theorem, there
exists
an algorithm that resolves the problem above, for every
\mathcal{H},
in time
f(k)\cdot|G|^{2}
(see
[
20
]
), however it is non-constructive as it is based on the knowledge of the set
{\sf obs}(\{G\mid\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\text{-}{\sf barrier}_{\mathcal{H}}(G)\leq k\}).

Due to the lack of an explicit upper bound on the size of this set and of its members, so far no constructive algorithm is known for any non-planar graph
Z.

We observe that for the class
\mathsf{excl}({Z}),
the algorithm
\mathbf{A}_{\mathsf{excl}(Z)}
discussed above, either decides that
(G,k)
is a
yes
-instance of
Half-Integral
Z
-Packing
or that
(G,f(k))
is a
no
-instance of
Half-Integral
Z
-Packing
, where
f(k)={2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}}.

This constitutes the first constructive (approximation)
FPT
-algorithm for the
Half-Integral
Z
-Packing
problem.
In
Section 13
we discuss further algorithmic consequences of our results.

Deciding when an enviroment class has the Erdős-Pósa property.

Given a minor antichain
\mathcal{R},
we denote by
\mathsf{excl}(\mathcal{R})
the class of graphs that excludes the graphs in
\mathcal{R}
as minors, i.e., the class
\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}\cap\bigcap_{R\in\mathcal{R}}\mathsf{excl}(R).

Consider the following problem for any given non-empty minor antichain
\mathcal{R}.

Checking EP-pairs for
\mathcal{R}
-minor free target

Input:

An antichain
\mathcal{Z}
for the minor relation.

Output:

Is
\big(\mathsf{excl}(\mathcal{R}),{\sf excl}(\mathcal{Z})\big)
an EP-pair?

The antichain
\mathcal{R}
(resp.
\mathcal{Z}
) provides a finite representation of a target (resp. environment) class and the question is whether the fixed target class
{\sf excl}(\mathcal{R})
makes an EP-pair with the input environment class
{\sf excl}(\mathcal{Z}).

For every
\mathscr{F}=\langle\mathscr{F}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{excl}(\mathcal{R})},
we define
\mathscr{F}\!\!\downarrow\coloneqq\{G\mid\text{$G$ is a minor of $\mathscr{F}_{t}$ for some $t\in\mathbb{N}$}\}.

Notice that, by definition,
\mathscr{F}\!\!\downarrow
is a minor-closed class.
Let now

\mathbb{O}_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq\{{\sf obs}(\mathscr{F}\!\!\downarrow)\mid\mathscr{F}\in\mathfrak{F}_{{\sf excl}(\mathcal{R})}\}.

Observe that our results imply that the set above is
finite
. Moreover, by Robertson and Seymour theorem, it consists of
finite
sets of graphs.
In light of the definitions above, a possible restatement of our main result (
Theorem 1.1
), is the following:

For any two antichains
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{Z}
for the minor relation, where
\mathcal{R}\neq\emptyset,
the tuple
\big(\mathsf{excl}(\mathcal{R}),{\sf excl}(\mathcal{Z})\big)
is an EP-pair if and only if for every
\mathcal{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{\mathcal{R}},
there exists some graph
Z\in\mathcal{Z}
such that no graph in
\mathcal{O}
is a minor of
Z.

The description above is given in terms of finite obstruction sets to emphasize that the entire problem
has a finite description.
The formulation above reflects that for each
\mathscr{F}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{excl}(\mathcal{R})},

\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathscr{F}\!\!\downarrow\neq\emptyset,
i.e., none of the
\mathscr{F}\!\!\downarrow
is fully contained in
\mathsf{excl}(\mathcal{Z}).

This implies that the
Checking EP-pairs for
\mathcal{R}
-minor free target
problem can be solved by performing
\sum_{\mathcal{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{\mathcal{R}}}\sum_{Z\in\mathcal{Z}}|\mathcal{O}|
calls to the almost-linear minor-checking algorithm of Korhonen, Pilipczuk, and Stamoulis
[
42
]
(see also
[
64
,
37
]
for previous algorithms, dating back to the Graph Minor series).
Therefore, for every non-empty antichain
\mathcal{R}
of the minor relation,
Checking EP-pairs for
\mathcal{R}
-minor free target
is solvable in almost-linear time.
Making this algorithm constructive, assuming the knowledge of
\mathcal{R},
is an interesting open question.

Erdős-Pósa dualities for topological minors.

A relevant question on Erdős-Pósa dualities is, to what extent an analogous theory of half-integrality, based on obstructing sets, can be build for other partial-orders one might define on graphs.
A particularly natural cousin of the minor relation that we may consider is the topological minor relation.
Here, we already have the half-integrality result of Liu
[
47
]
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
be graph classes that are closed under topological minors.
We say that
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is an
Erdős-Pósa pair for topological minors
, in short is a
tEP-pair
, if there exists a function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that every graph
G\in\mathcal{G},
without
k
disjoint subdivisions of graphs that do not belong to
\mathcal{H},
contains a set
S
of at most
f(k)
vertices such that
G-S\in\mathcal{H}.

An immediate question is: When is
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}})
a tEP-pair?
Is there a characterization analogous to the one of (
3
) for the topological minor relation?
This question was already asked by Robertson and Seymour in
[
62
]
and, as mentioned in
[
47
]
, Liu, Postle, and Wollan have indeed found a complete (but complicated) characterization.
Moreover, this characterization seems to be qualitatively more complex than the planarity criterion in (
3
) for the case of minors.

Let
\mathsf{texcl}(R)
be the class of graphs excluding the graph
R
as a topological minor.
According to Liu
[
48
]
, the problem that, given a graph
R,
asks whether
\big(\mathsf{texcl}(R),\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}\big)
is a tEP-pair is
NP
-complete even if we restrict its inputs to trees.
We wish to stress the contrast to the fact that, by (
3
), the question “is
\big(\mathsf{excl}(R),\mathcal{G}_{{\text{\rm{all}}}}\big)
an EP-pair?
” is just planarity testing and thus, can be answered in polynomial time.
This suggests that a theory of obstructing sets such as the one for minors presented in this paper is either impossible or, at least, extremely complex for the case of topological minors.
A possible source of this complexity gap seems to be the fact that graphs ordered by the topological minor relation do not form a WQO.
However, it is an interesting question whether our approach may help in the direction of finding a constructive proof of Liu’s theorem with explicit bounds.
In
Figure 3
we illustrate the current landscape of Erdős-Pósa dualities.

Figure 3
:
The iceberg provides a visual metaphor for the landscape on Erdős-Pósa dualities for (topological) minor-closed graph classes.

Improving the gap function.

All parametric equivalences that we prove in this paper, namely
{\sf p}_{\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sim\mathcal{H}\text{-}{\sf tw},

p_{\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sim\mathcal{H}\text{-}{\sf td},
and
p_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sim{\sf apex}_{\mathcal{H}},
have exponential gap functions, i.e.,
f(h,k)\in{2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}}.

A natural question is whether this gap can be improved to a polynomial one, that is, one of the form
k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}.

This would also imply an improvement of the approximation gap of the
FPT
-approximation algorithm for the
Half-Integral
Z
-Packing
problem (mentioned on page
1.3
).
One of the two sources of exponential explosion can be found in
Lemma 4.2
whose proof is given in
[
77
]
which, in turn uses
[
39
, Theorem 11.4]
as a departure point where the parametric dependencies are already exponential.
This indicates that any improvement on this might require a general improvement of the dependencies of the Graph minors Structural Theorem (GMST) in
[
65
,
39
]
.
The second level of exponentiation comes from
Section 6
where we need thickets with nests of exponential size around their vortices in order to split those vortices.
This is because each split consumes a part of the nest to maintain the large radial linkage which leads back to the wall, and to extract new flowers.
Interestingly the reason for the exponentiality in the proof of
[
39
, Theorem 11.4]
is also the vortex splitting operation.
It seems reasonable to conjecture that finding a way to bound the dependencies of the GMST by polynomials would also provide a way to avoid the first source of exponentiation.
Such a result would be a major advancement.

Finally, the degree of the polynomial in
k,
even in the exponent of
2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}},
is highly dependent on the “meta parameter”
h=h(\mathcal{H}).

In fact, this dependency is at least double exponential in
h.

Therefore, another avenue of improvement could be to lower this dependency.
A bound on the gap function of the form
2^{\mathsf{poly}_{h}(k)},
or, if the improvements to polynomial dependency we mention above can indeed be achieved, even
\mathsf{poly}_{h}(k)
cannot be ruled out.

1.4
A short description of the proof of
Theorem 1.3

Since this proof is by far the most technical and involved part of the entire paper, to properly explain it requires a large number of deeply nested definitions.
Several of these definitions are given in
Subsection 1.5
and the majority of the remaining definitions can be found in
Section 2
.
Here we provide a very brief and informal overview of the different steps and our approach.
A second, more in-depth but also more technical overview of our proof can be found in
Subsection 1.6
.

To avoid dealing with graph classes
\mathcal{H}
directly in our proof, we aim for the following variant of
Theorem 1.3
.

Theorem 1.9
.

There exist functions
f^{1},f^{2}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all positive integers
h
and
k
and every graph
G,
if
W
is an
f^{1}(h,k)
-wall in
G,
then there exists a set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
f^{2}(h,k)<f^{1}(h,k)
such that for every graph
Z
on at most
h
vertices, either

•

there exists a parametric graph
\mathscr{W}=\langle\mathscr{W}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathsf{excl}(Z)}
such that
G
contains a subdivision
W^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{W}_{k}
and
W^{\prime}
cannot be separated from
W
by fewer than
k
vertices, or

•

the component of
G-S
which contains the majority of
W-S
is
Z
-minor-free.

The Graph Minor Structure Theorem of Robertson and Seymour.

To explain our approach, it is necessary to introduce the Graph Minor Structure Theorem (GMST) of Robertson and Seymour
[
65
,
39
]
.
We present here an abbreviated version of the local version of their theorem which avoids most of the technical statements.
Here local means again “with respect to a large wall”.

Let
\alpha,b,d
be non-negative integers.
We say that a graph
G
is
\alpha
-almost embeddable
with
breadth

b
and
depth

d
in some surface
\Sigma
if there exists a set
A\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
\alpha
and there are graphs
G^{(i)}
such that
G-A=G^{(0)}\cup G^{(1)}\cup\dots\cup G^{(b)}
where
G^{(0)}
is a graph embedded in
\Sigma
and each
vortex

G^{(i)},

i\in[b],
is a graph of pathwidth at most
d
which is ‘‘attached along a path decomposition
11
11

11

Please see
Subsection 2.2
for a formal definition.
” to the vertices of some face of
G^{(0)}.

The local version of the GMST of Robertson and Seymour now reads as follows.

There exist functions
f^{1},f^{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every positive integer
k
and every graph
G
with an
f^{1}(k)
-wall
W,
there either exists (1) a
K_{k}
-minor which cannot be separated from
W
by deleting fewer than
k
vertices, or (2) there exists a subgraph
G^{\prime}
of
G
which cannot be separated from
W
by deleting fewer than
k
vertices and whose torso is
f^{2}(k)
-almost embeddable with breadth
k^{2}
and depth
f^{2}(k)
in a surface where
K_{k}
does not embed.

(7)

Instead of starting at (
7
) we make use of a recent refinement by Thilikos and Wiederrecht
[
77
]
which allows us to obtain one of three things.
We either find: (1) a large clique minor; (2) a walloid of large order that represents a surface
\Sigma
of large enough Euler-genus such that every graph on at most
h
vertices can be drawn on
\Sigma;
or (3) a subgraph
G^{\prime}
with the properties from the second outcome of (
7
) together with a walloid
W^{*}
of large order which is fully contained in the graph
G^{(0)}
of the almost embedding of the torso of
G^{\prime}
in this outcome.

From here on our proof follows along three crucial steps.

Step 1: Almost embeddings of a graph
Z.

Let us fix
G^{(0)}
to be the part of the torso of
G^{\prime}
as above which is drawn in the surface.
Moreover, let
M
be a minimal connected subgraph of
G
such that
M
contains some graph
Z
on at most
h
vertices as a minor and
M
contains a vertex of the wall
W.

Now some part of
M
belongs to
G^{(0)},
but other parts of
M
may belong to the vortices
G^{(i)}
or to the so-called “flaps”.
Flaps are those parts of
G
which are separated from
G^{\prime}-A
by at most three vertices.
Since these separators are so small, they do not have to be caught within the vortices.
Instead, they form facial triangles in
G^{(0)}
(see
Proposition 2.1
and the definitions surrounding it).
This induces a specific
0
-almost embedding of bounded breadth and depth for the graph
M.

A
flower
of
M
is any maximal subgraph of
M
which is fully contained in either a vortex or a flap
12
12

12

We refer to
Subsection 1.5
for a formal definition.
.
Our proof introduces a procedure that either finds many duplicates of every single flower of
M,
or finds a small hitting set for at least one of those flowers.

In
Section 7
and
Section 8
we show, among others, that it suffices to repeat this procedure for only a bounded number of such graphs
M
for every graph
Z
on at most
h
vertices.
Moreover, we show that we may assume that
M
has only a bounded number, in
|Z|,
of vertices of degree different than two.

For any such
M,
where each individual flower appears in abundance, we will then be able to construct a corresponding member of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathsf{excl}(Z)}
of large order.
This is made precise in
Lemma 9.1
.
A formal definition of flowers and the way we obtain these graphs
M
and their
0
-almost embeddings, together with a full definition of the family
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathsf{excl}(Z)}
is given in
Subsection 1.5
.

Step 2: Refining an almost embedding.

The “gathering” and “hitting” of the flowers of the graphs
M
as above happens in two stages.
In the first stage we gather all possible flowers which can easily be found to be abundant.
Meanwhile all flowers where this is impossible, or success is at least “not as apparent”, are confined within a small number of disks described with reference to the embedding of
G^{(0)}.

Dealing with these disks, we call them
thickets
, is the second stage and the only part where we actually find the hitting set.

The first stage is exclusively concerned with flowers of
M
that are produced via the flaps of
G^{(0)}.

Recall that we have the walloid
W^{*}
within
G^{(0)}
which fully represents our surface
\Sigma.

With the help of
W^{*}
we now produce a tiling of
\Sigma
using only hexagons.
We may assume that there are only a bounded number of
M
’s we have to consider and each
M
can only produce a bounded number of different flowers.
This allows us to classify, using the pigeonhole principle, those flowers that appear (a) in many different and far apart hexagons of our tiling and those (b) that appear either clustered closely together or a bounded number of times.
This substep is done in
Section 4
and
Section 5
.

Let us mark all hexagons which contain flowers of type (b).
By imitating a technique developed by Kawarabayashi, Thomas, and Wollan
[
39
]
in their new proof of (
7
) we now gather all marked hexagons in a small number of disks within the drawing of
G^{(0)}
and then further refine our distinction between abundant and scarce flowers until each of the disks that still contains marked hexagons can be seen as a vortex of bounded depth itself.
This step is done in
Section 6
.

Step 3: Harvesting the flowers of a “thicket”.

The vortices created in the previous steps are all equipped with additional infrastructure.
This allows us to route anything “of value” we might find inside, back to
W^{*}.

We call these objects
thickets
.
The second purpose of
Section 7
and
Section 8
is to apply a
vast generalization of the “vortex killing” argument of Thilikos and Wiederrecht
[
76
]
.
The outcome of this argument is, for any possible flower
F
of any
M
we have to consider, to either find many disjoint copies of
F
pasted along a single face of
G^{(0)}
and highly connected to
W^{*},
or find a small hitting set for all occurrences of
F
within this thicket.
Let
S
be the union of all of these hitting sets for all thickets.
Finally, we prove that whenever some
M
avoids the set
S,
then this must mean that there is an equivalent
M^{\prime}
whose flowers have been found in abundance in
Step 2
and this one, and thus we are able to produce the corresponding member of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathsf{excl(Z)}}
(see
Lemma 9.1
).

The proof of
Theorem 1.9
, or its more technical incarnation
Theorem 9.2
, is now only a matter of carefully combining the previous steps.

1.5
Defining the families
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
: A constructive description of
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}

In order to state
Theorem 1.1
in full formality, we first need to make our definitions of the graphs
M
that appear in
Step 1
of
Subsection 1.4
and then describe how the families
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
and ultimately
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
are defined.
Once this is done, the collection
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}
of counterexamples is fully described, in accordance with
Theorem 1.1
, as the collection of every graph class generated through the minor closure of a single member of
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}.

Some definitions presented in this section still hide further technicalities which are expanded upon in
Section 2
.

As a first step, we make the
0
-almost embeddings of our ‘‘minor models’’
13
13

13

Later we will call these graphs the
extensions
of
Z.
explicit.

Hypergraphs from graph partitions.

A
subgraph partition
of a graph
Z
is a collection
\mathcal{P}
of subgraphs of
Z
such that
14
14

14

Given a set
\mathcal{S}

of objects where
\cup
has been defined, we use
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{S}
as a shorthand for
\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}S.
Here the union of two graphs
G_{1}=(V_{1},E_{1})
and
G_{2}=(V_{2},E_{2})
is defined as
G_{1}\cup G_{2}=(V_{1}\cup V_{2},E_{1}\cup E_{2}).

{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{P}=Z
and
\{E(P)\mid P\in\mathcal{P}\}
is a partition of
E(Z).

Given a subgraph
P\in\mathcal{P},
we define the
boundary
of
P
in
Z
to be the set
\partial_{Z}(P)
containing every vertex of
V(P)
that is also a vertex of some other member of
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P\}.

We define
\mathcal{P}(Z)
to be the set containing all subgraph partitions
\mathcal{P}
of
Z
such that for every
P\in\mathcal{P},

\partial_{Z}(P)\neq\emptyset.

Given a subgraph partition
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}(Z),
we define the hypergraph
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}=(V_{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}})
where
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}=\{\partial_{Z}(P)\mid P\in\mathcal{P}\}
and
V_{\mathcal{P}}={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}.

It follows that each hyperedge
e
of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
is the boundary of some member, say
G_{e},
of the graph partition (notice that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
may be a multi-set, as in the case of the example of
Figure 5
).

In
Subsection 1.4
we spoke a lot about “pasting graphs along the vertices of a face” of “confining” parts of a graph within a disk.
The following provides a formal framework to deal with notions like those in a way that is, at first, independent of topology.

Linear societies and their decompositions.

A
linear society
is a pair
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
where
H
is a non-empty graph and
\Lambda
is a linear ordering of some non-empty subset of vertices of
H.

We use
V(\Lambda)
to denote the set of vertices of
\Lambda
and we refer to
V(\Lambda)
as the
boundary
of the linear society
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle.

Moreover, we
always
demand that every connected component of
H
contains some vertex of
V(\Lambda).

Let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be a linear society where
\Lambda=\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}\rangle.

Consider a drawing, possibly with crossings, of
H
in a closed disk
\Delta
such that the vertices drawn on the boundary, denoted by
{\sf bd}(\Delta),
are exactly the vertices of
\Lambda
drawn with respect to the order
\Lambda.

We also demand that all edges of
H
are drawn in the interior of
\Delta.

For simplicity, we do not distinguish between the vertices of
V(\Lambda)
and the points of
{\sf bd}(\Delta)
where these vertices are drawn.

Whenever different components of
H
are drawn within
\Delta
in the way above such that there exists a curve
\gamma
in
\Delta
with exactly its endpoints in
{\sf bd}(\Delta)
where
\gamma
does not intersect the drawing of
H
in any way, we are able to “split”
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
into smaller linear societies using
\gamma
while also respecting the original linear ordering
\Lambda
up to shifts.
We formalize this intuition with the definition below to obtain a notion of “components” of a linear society.

We see the set
{\sf bd}(\Delta)\setminus V(\Lambda)
as a set of open lines between consecutive vertices in
\Lambda.

For each
i\in[r],
let
p_{i}
be a point of the line with endpoints
y_{i-1}
and
y_{i}
(where
y_{0}=y_{r}
).
A closed subdisk
\Delta^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta
is
splitting
for
\Delta
if

•

H
can be drawn (possibly with crossings) inside
\Delta\setminus\Delta^{\prime},

•

\{p_{1}\}\subseteq{\sf bd}(\Delta)\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta^{\prime})\subseteq\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\},
and

•

the cardinality of
|{\sf bd}(\Delta)\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta^{\prime})|
is maximized.

Let
{\sf bd}(\Delta)\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta^{\prime})=\{p_{i}\mid i\in I\}
for some
[1]\subseteq I\subseteq[r]
where
I=\{i_{1}=1,i_{2},\ldots,i_{q}\}
and observe that
I
is the same for every choice of a splitting subdisk
\Delta^{\prime}
for
\Delta.

For each
j\in[q],
let
\Lambda_{j}\coloneqq\langle y_{i_{j}},\ldots,y_{i_{j+1}-1}\rangle
and let
H_{j}
be the union of all connected components of
H
that contain vertices of
\Lambda_{j}.

We denote the
decomposition
of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
into the linear societies
\langle H_{i},\Lambda_{i}\rangle
as above by
\mathsf{dec}({H,\Lambda})\coloneqq\langle\langle H_{1},\Lambda_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H_{q},\Lambda_{q}\rangle\rangle.

Next, we reintroduce the topology to fully understand how linear societies may be used to encode the “almost” part of our embeddings.

Surfaces and linear societies.

By
surface
we mean a two-dimensional manifold (possibly with boundary).
We say that a surface
\Sigma
is
contained
in some surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
if
\Sigma^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\Sigma
by adding handles and crosscaps.
Given a pair
(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c})\in\mathbb{N}\times[0,2]
we define
\Sigma^{(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c})}
to be the surface without boundary created from the sphere by adding
\mathsf{h}
handles and
\mathsf{c}
crosscaps.
If
\mathsf{c}=0,
the surface
\Sigma^{(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c})}
is
orientable
, otherwise it is
non-orientable
.
By Dyck’s theorem
[
17
,
23
]
, two crosscaps are equivalent (via homeomorphisms) to a handle in the presence of a (third) crosscap.
This implies that the notation
\Sigma^{(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c})}
is sufficient to capture all surfaces without boundary.

Given a graph
Z
we denote by
\mathbb{S}^{Z}
the set containing all surfaces without boundary where
Z
cannot be embedded.
We define
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}^{Z})
to be the set of all containment-wise minimal surfaces not in
\mathbb{S}^{Z}.

It follows that
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}^{Z})
contains one or two surfaces, see
[
77
]
.
We define
\mathbb{S}^{(Z)}
as the set containing every surface that is contained in some surface of
{\sf sobs}(\mathbb{S}^{Z}).

Let now
\Sigma
be some surface and let
M
be a graph, let
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}(M)
be a subgraph partition of
M,
and assume that
\Gamma
is a
\Sigma
-embedding.
In such an embedding all vertices of a hyperedge
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
are drawn on the boundary of some closed disk
\Delta_{e}
of
\Sigma
and two disks
\Delta_{e}
and
\Delta_{e^{\prime}}
intersect only on the common vertices of
e
and
e^{\prime}.

Given a choice of starting vertex
y\in e
and some rotation of the boundary of
\Delta_{e},
we obtain a linear ordering
\Lambda_{e,y}
of the vertices of
e
starting from
y.

The rotation of each hyperedge is chosen to be counterclockwise (that is, it has
\Delta_{e}
on the left) in case
\Sigma
orientable, and it is chosen to be an arbitrary rotation if
\Sigma
is non-orientable.
Each
\langle G_{e},\Lambda_{e,y}\rangle,
for
y\in e,
can now be seen as a linear society.
We say that the linear society
\langle G_{e},\Lambda_{e,y}\rangle
is
disk embeddable
if the graph
G_{e}
can be embedded in a closed disk
\Delta
such that exactly the vertices of
\Lambda_{e,y}
are drawn on its boundary.

We are finally ready to give a definition of those
0
-almost embeddings of graphs that sit at the core of our description of
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathcal{H}}.

Embedding pairs.

Let
Z
be a graph.
An
embedding pair
of
Z
is a pair
(\Sigma,\mathbf{B}),
where
\Sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{(Z)}
is a surface, and
\mathbf{B}
is the set of all linear societies which are generated, as described below, by a fixed triple of the form
(M,\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
where

1.

M
is a connected graph containing
Z
as a minor such that
|M|\leq\mathsf{ext}(|Z|),

2.

\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}(M),
and

3.

\Gamma
is a
\Sigma
-embedding of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}},

where
\mathsf{ext}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a function of order
2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell(|Z|^{2}))}
that will be determined in the proof of
Lemma 9.1
in
Section 9
and
\ell(\cdot)
is the
unique linkage function
(see
[
67
,
40
,
3
,
51
,
26
]
).

Given a triple as above, we further consider some function
\eta\colon\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}\to V(M)
that assigns a starting vertex
\eta(e)\in e
to every
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}.

Thereby,
\eta
generates the following set of linear societies

\{\langle G_{e},\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)}\rangle\mid e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}\text{\penalty\ and $\langle G_{e},\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)}\rangle$ is not disk-embeddable}\}.

Notice that
{\bf B}
may be empty.
We denote by
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathsf{excl}(Z)}
the set containing all embedding pairs of
Z.

Notice that a graph on
h
vertices has at most
2^{\mathcal{O}((\mathsf{ext}(|Z|))^{2})}
many subgraph partitions.
Moreover, there exist at most
2|Z|^{2}+1
many different surfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{(Z)}
and for each choice of
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}(Z)
we have
|\mathcal{P}|\leq\mathsf{ext}(|Z|)^{2}
and
|P|\leq\mathsf{ext}(|Z|)
for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
which implies that there are at most
\mathsf{ext}(|Z|)^{3}
many choices for
\eta.

It follows that
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathsf{excl}(Z)}
has
2^{(\mathsf{ext}(|Z|))^{2}}
elements.
Given a proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
we define
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{H}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{Z\in\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})}\mathfrak{P}_{\mathsf{excl}(Z)}.

We now obtain, in particular, that
|\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{H}}|
is finite for every choice of
\mathcal{H}.

The walloids that make up our families
\mathfrak{F}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
are constructed from several smaller pieces that can be circularly arranged.
Such a modular definition allows for a nicer presentation, but it also facilitates our proofs as it allows us in many places to reduce to the analysis of single pieces of the walloid rather than its entire global structure.

t
-segments.

Each member
\mathscr{W}
of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
is a wall-like parametric graph to which, from now on, we refer as an
elementary

guest walloid
.
These parametric graphs
\mathscr{W}
are built using the embedding pairs from
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{H}}.

The main building components of elementary guest walloids are called
elementary

t
-segments
.
While the formal definitions are explained in detail in
Subsection 2.4
.
Here, we provide an intuitive description which will be sufficient to understand the family
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
and the more detailed summary of the proof of
Theorem 1.3
.

The three basic components are the elementary
(t^{\prime},t)
-wall segment
, the
elementary
t
-handle segment
, and the

elementary
t
-crosscap segment
.
The
elementary
(t^{\prime},t)
-wall segment
is an elementary
(t^{\prime},t)
-wall and acts as the starting point of our construction of all other elementary
t
-segments.
The leftmost drawing of
Figure 4
depicts an elementary
(12,3)
-wall segment.
We depict the
t
“leftmost” vertices of an elementary
(t^{\prime},t)
-wall segment in red and its
t
“rightmost” vertices in blue. Later, we will use these vertices in order to glue together distinct
t
-segments, using new edges depicted in orange.
The
t^{\prime}
“top” vertices of the elementary
(t^{\prime},t)
-wall segment are drawn with white interiors.

The
elementary
t
-handle segment
is an elementary
(4t,t)
-wall segment where we added two collections of
t
edges, each between the top vertices of the
(4t,t)
-wall, as indicated in the second drawing of
Figure 4
.
The
elementary
t
-crosscap segment
is an elementary
(4t,t)
-wall segment obtained by adding a collection of
2t
edges between the top vertices of the elementary
(4t,t)
-wall in a pairwise crossing manner, as indicated in the third drawing of
Figure 4
.

:

3
-wall segment, an elementary

The fourth and most complicated, and somehow central, elementary
t
-segment is parametrized by an integer
b\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and a linear society
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
where
\mathsf{dec}(H,\Lambda)=\langle\langle H_{1},\Lambda_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H_{q},\Lambda_{q}\rangle\rangle.

An
elementary
(t,b,H,\Lambda)
-flower segment
is constructed by starting from an elementary
(t+b|\Lambda|+t,t)
-wall segment.
As a first step, we add a collection of
t
edges joining its
t
leftmost top and its
t
rightmost top vertices, as indicated by the brown edges of the rightmost drawing of
Figure 4
.
The construction is completed by taking, for every
j\in[q],

b
copies
\langle H^{1}_{j},\Lambda^{1}_{j}\rangle,
…,
\langle H^{b}_{j},\Lambda^{b}_{j}\rangle
of
\langle H_{j},\Lambda_{j}\rangle
and connecting the
p
-th vertex of
\Lambda^{i}_{j}
with the
(t+(\Sigma_{j^{\prime}\in[j-1]}|\Lambda_{j-1}|)b+(i-1)|\Lambda_{j}|+p)
-th top vertex for every
j\in[q],
every
i\in[b],
and every
p\in|\Lambda_{j}|.

The rightmost drawing in
Figure 4
depicts an elementary
(3,3,K_{3,3}^{-},\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle)
-flower segment, where
K_{3,3}^{-}
is the graph obtained from
K_{3,3}
by deleting the edge
y_{1}y_{2}.

The 3 copies of
K_{3,3}^{-}
are depicted in orange (see also

).

Cylindrical concatenations and the construction of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}.

Given a sequence
\langle W_{1},\ldots,W_{q^{\prime}}\rangle
of
t
-segments, we define their
cylindrical concatenation
as the graph obtained by adding edges (depicted in orange) between same-height rightmost (depicted in blue) vertices of
W_{i}
and leftmost (depicted in red) vertices of
W_{i+1}
for every
i\in[q^{\prime}-1].

The construction is then completed by adding edges between same height rightmost vertices of
W_{1}
and leftmost vertices of
W_{q^{\prime}}
(see
Figure 5
for an example).

Figure 5
:
Left: A graph
M=Z
and a subgraph partition
\mathcal{P}
of
M
containing two graphs
H_{1}
and
H_{2}.

H_{1}
is drawn in the green disk while
H_{2}
is drawn in the violet disk. The hypergraph
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
contains the vertices
a,b,c,
and
d
and two hyper-edges
e_{1}=e_{2}=\{a,b,c,d\}.
The figure shows an embedding of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
in the sphere.
The choices for
Z,

M,

\mathcal{P},
and
\eta,
generate the embedding pair
\mathbf{p}=(\Sigma,\mathbf{B})
for the class
\mathsf{excl}(Z)
where
\mathbf{B}=\{\langle H_{1},\langle d,b,a,c\rangle\rangle,\langle H_{2},\langle c,a,b,d\rangle\rangle\}.
Right: The guest walloid
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{3}.

Let now
\mathbf{p}=(\Sigma,\mathbf{B})
be some embedding pair of
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathcal{H}},
where
\Sigma=\Sigma^{(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c})}
for some
\mathsf{h}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathsf{c}\in[0,2].

Let also
\mathbf{B}=\{(H_{1},\Lambda_{1}),\ldots,(H_{q},\Lambda_{q})\}.

We define the parametric graph
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}=\langle\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}},
where
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t}
is the cylindric concatenation of
\langle W_{1},\ldots,W_{q^{\prime}}\rangle,
where
q^{\prime}=1+\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+q
and such that
W_{1}
is the elementary
(4t,t)
-wall segment,
W_{2},\ldots,W_{1+\mathsf{h}}
are elementary
t
-handle segments,
W_{1+\mathsf{h}+1},\ldots,W_{1+\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}}
are elementary
t
-crosscap segments, and for
i\in[q],

W_{1+\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+i}
is the elementary
(t,t,H_{i},\Lambda_{i})
-flower segment.

Notice that the above construction of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}
depends on the ordering of the elements of
\mathbf{B}
considered.
This is not a problem for our definition as all alternative parametric graphs defined by different orderings are pairwise equivalent (see for example the proof of
Lemma 5.4
and
Subsection 6.4
).
So we may choose an arbitrary ordering for for defining
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}.

For the purposes of our construction, for every graph
Z,
we also set up a special symbol
{\bf p}_{Z}
(treated as a separate “embedding pair”) and we set
\mathscr{W}^{{\bf p}_{Z}}=\langle t\cdot Z\mid t\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{P}}^{(Z)}\coloneqq\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)}\cup\{{\bf p}_{Z}\}.
Let now
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed class.
We define
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\varnothing}\coloneqq\{\mathscr{W}^{{\bf p}_{Z}}\mid Z\in{\sf obs}(\mathcal{H})\}.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r},
be the connected components of our graph
Z.

We define the following four parametric families.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{(Z)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\big\{\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}_{1}}+\cdots+{\mathscr{W}}^{\mathbf{p}_{r}}\mid(\mathbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{r})\in\widehat{\mathfrak{P}}^{(Z_{1})}\times\cdots\times\widehat{\mathfrak{P}}^{(Z_{r})}\big\},

(8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq
