corresponding to the nodes contained in the trace of the outer cycle
C_{s+1}
of a
(t,s)
-thicket segment of
W
and
H
is the subgraph of
G
drawn in the
\mathsf{trace}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}(C_{s+1}).

If
\delta
has a single vortex then we say that
(G,\delta,W)
is a
single
-
thicket

(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard.

A
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta
of
G
with vortices
c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell}
is
(x,y)
-
fertile
if there are positive integers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}
where
\sum_{i\in[\ell]}x_{i}=x
and such that any vortex society of
\delta,

has a rendition with
x_{i}
vortices, each of depth at most
y.

We also say that
\delta
is
(z,p)
-
ripe
if it has at most
z
vortices, each of depth at most
p.

Notice that if
\delta
is
(z,p)
-ripe, then it is also
(z,p)
-fertile.

The
\delta
-
influence
of a fence
C,
denoted by
\delta\text{-}\mathsf{influence}(C),
is the union of all simple cells of
\delta
that contain at least one edge of
C
and the cells of
\delta
whose disk is a subset of the
\mathsf{trace}(C^{\mathsf{si}})\cap N(\delta)
-avoiding disk of the trace of
C.

Note that a cell can belong to the influence of at most three of the fences of
W.

Moreover, disjoint fences have disjoint influences.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}.

The
d
-folio of a fence
C
of
W
is the union of the
d
-folios of all
c
-societies, where
c
is a cell in the
\delta
-influence of
C,
i.e.,

d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(C)\coloneqq\{d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(G_{c},\Lambda_{c,x})\mid\text{$c\in\delta\text{-}\mathsf{influence}(C)$ and $x\in B_{\Delta_{c}}$}\}.

A fence of
W
is
d
-
homogeneous
if all its bricks have the same (in terms of society isomorphism)
d
-folio.
We moreover say that the triple
(G,\delta,W)
is

•

d
-
plowed
if all its
2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+1
enclosures are
d
-homogeneous,

•

(x,y)
-
fertile
if
\delta
is
(x,y)
-fertile,

•

(z,p)
-
ripe
if
\delta
is
(z,p)
-ripe and every thicket society of
(G,\delta,W)
has depth at most
2s+p,
and

•

d
-
blooming
if every linear society
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
in
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)
is the linear society of a
(t,b)
-parterre segment of
W.

3
Preliminary results

In this section, we present two independent results that will be of use for proofs in the following sections.
In the first subsection, we prove a homogeneity lemma for walls and a mono-dimensional variant for ladders.
In the second subsection, we prove a result that allows us to control to some extent the behavior of a “minimal” linkage traversing multiple nests.

3.1
Finding a homogeneous subwall

In this subsection we show how given a large enough wall whose bricks come equipped with a subset from a fixed set of colors, we can always find an arbitrarily large subwall of it where all bricks are accompanied by the same color set.
We moreover observe how the same arguments give us a mono-dimensional variant applied to an object we call a ladder.

Let
\zeta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

z,t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},
and
W
be a
(z,t)
-wall.
Assume that every brick
B
of
W
is assigned a subset of
[\zeta]
which we call the
\zeta
-
palette
of
B,
denoted by
\zeta\text{-}\mathsf{palette}(B).
We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given
\zeta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

z,t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},
and an
(f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(z,\zeta),f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(t,\zeta))
-wall
W,
outputs a
(z,t)
-wall
W^{\prime}
that is a subwall of
W
and such that every brick of
W^{\prime}
has the same
\zeta
-palette in time

{\mathcal{O}(z^{2^{\zeta}}\cdot t^{2^{\zeta}})}.

Moreover

f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(x,\zeta)=x^{2^{\zeta}}.

Proof.

Let
\alpha:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be such that
\alpha(\zeta)\coloneqq 2^{\zeta}.

We define
f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(z,\zeta)\coloneqq z^{\alpha(\zeta)}.

Let
\zeta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

z,t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},
and
W
be a
(z^{\alpha(\zeta)},t^{\alpha(\zeta)})
-wall.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}}
be a grouping of the horizontal paths of
W
in consecutive bundles of
t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}
paths ordered from left to right, where the last path in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is the first path of
\mathcal{P}_{i+1}.

Also let
\mathcal{Q}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Q}_{z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}}
be a grouping of the vertical paths of
W
in consecutive bundles of
z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}
paths ordered from top to bottom, where the last path in
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is the first path of
\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}.

For every
i\in[t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}]
and
j\in[z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}],
let
W_{i,j}
be the graph
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i}\cup\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{j}
that is a
(z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)},t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)})
-wall that is a subwall of
W,
where
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{j}
are defined as follows.
Let
P_{i}
(resp.
P^{\prime}_{i}
) be the topmost (resp. bottommost) path of
W
in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
and
Q_{j}
(resp.
Q^{\prime}_{j}
) be the leftmost (resp. rightmost) path of
W
in
\mathcal{Q}_{j}.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i}
(resp.
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{j}
) contain the paths in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
(resp.
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
) cropped to contain only vertices of vertical (resp. horizontal) paths between
Q_{i}
(resp.
P_{i}
) and
Q^{\prime}_{i}
(resp.
P^{\prime}_{i}
).
There are
z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}\cdot t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}
such subwalls depending on the choices of
i
and
j.

We also consider
P
(resp.
P^{\prime}
) to be the top (resp. bottom) horizontal path in
W,
and
Q
(resp.
Q^{\prime}
) to be the leftmost (resp. rightmost) vertical path in
W.

Observe that the graph
P\cup(\bigcap_{i\in[t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}]}\mathcal{P}_{i})\cup P^{\prime}\cup Q\cup(\bigcap_{j\in[z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}]}\mathcal{Q}_{j})\cup Q^{\prime}
also forms a
(z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)},t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)})
-wall
W^{\prime}
that is a subwall of
W.

Now, we assume inductively that for every
(z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)},t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)})
-wall
W^{\prime\prime}
that is a subwall of
W,
there exists a
(z,t)
-wall that is a subwall of
W^{\prime\prime}
such that every brick of that subwall has the same
(\zeta-1)
-palette.
Next, we show that there is
W^{\prime\prime}\in\{W_{i,j}\mid i\in[t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}],j\in[z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}]\}\cup\{W^{\prime}\}
such that, either the
\zeta
-palette of every brick of
W^{\prime\prime}
contains
\zeta
or no brick contains
\zeta.

Notice that from the induction hypothesis this suffices to prove our claim.
To conclude, observe that by definition, either the
\zeta
-palette of every brick of
W^{\prime}
contains
\zeta
or there is a
W^{\prime\prime}\in\{W_{i,j}\mid i\in[t^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}],j\in[z^{\alpha(\zeta-1)}]\}
such that no brick of
W^{\prime\prime}
contains
\zeta.

∎

Let
L
be a subdivision of the
(2\times t)
-grid, where
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq}2.

We call
L
a
t
-
ladder
.
Notice that
L
can be seen as the union of
t
horizontal paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{t}
and two vertical paths
Q_{1},Q_{2}
which correspond to the subdivided rows and columns respectively of the original
(2\times t)
-grid.
We say that a ladder
L^{\prime}
is a
subladder
of
L
if
L^{\prime}
is obtained from
L
by removing the edges of some (maybe none) of its horizontal paths.
Consider a plane-embedding of
L
obtained from the natural plane-embedding of the
(2\times t)
-grid.
We call all facial cycles of
L,
except the cycle that bounds the outer face, the
bricks
of
L.

For
\zeta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
we define the
\zeta
-palette of a brick of
L
as before.

We get the following mono-dimensional corollary of
Lemma 3.1
, by applying the same inductive argument only in the dimension of the horizontal paths.

Corollary 3.2
.

There is an algorithm that, given
\zeta\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2},
and an
f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(t,\zeta)
-ladder
L,
outputs a
t
-ladder
L^{\prime}
that is a subladder of
L
and such that every brick of
L^{\prime}
has the same
\zeta
-palette in time
\mathcal{O}(t^{2^{\zeta}}).

3.2
Taming a linkage crossing large nests

In this subsection we prove that for every linkage in a graph
G
that “invades” a number of large enough nests in a
\Sigma
-decomposition of
G
we can appropriately define a “minimal” linkage equivalent to the first with a number of additional information that will be useful to bound the complexity of the extensions of models we have to consider.
We begin with a few necessary definitions related to the unique linkage function
\ell\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
which we already discuss in the introduction.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a linkage in a graph
G.

The endpoints of the paths of
\mathcal{L}
are called
terminals
.
The
pattern
of
\mathcal{L}
is the set of pairs of endpoints of the paths of
\mathcal{L}.

\mathcal{L}
is
equivalent
to a linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
in
G
if they have the same pattern.

An
LB-pair
of
G
is a pair
(\mathcal{L},B)
where
B
is a subgraph of
G
with maximum degree two and
\mathcal{L}
is a linkage in
G.

We define
\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{L},B)\coloneqq|E(\mathcal{L})\setminus E(B)|
to be the number of edges of the paths of
\mathcal{L}
that are not edges of
B.

The following proposition, restated in the terminology of this paper, is proved in
[
26
]
.
It essentially states that if a linkage in a graph
G
crosses through a subgraph of
G
of maximum degree two in a complicated way, i.e., creating large treewidth, then there is a way to simplify the linkage.

Proposition 3.3

(Lemma 1,
[
26
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph and

(\mathcal{L},B)
be an LB-pair of
G.

Then, if
{\sf tw}(\mathcal{L}\cup B)>\ell(|\mathcal{L}|),

G
contains a linkage
\mathcal{R}
such that

1.

\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{R},B)<\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{L},B),

2.

\mathcal{R}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{L},
and

3.

\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{L}\cup B.

Given a
\delta
-grounded cycle
C
of some
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta
of some graph, we denote by
G^{-}_{C}
the graph obtained by the union of the graphs
G_{c},
where
c\subseteq\Delta_{C}
and
G_{c}\cap C=\emptyset.

We proceed with the main result of this subsection which gives us our desired minimal linkage with all of the necessary additional properties that we discussed.
The running time follows from standard irrelevant vertex arguments used in order to compute the minimal linkage implied by the lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

There exist functions
f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{1}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},

f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{2}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},

f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{3}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},
and an algorithm that,

•

given
r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

s\geq f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{1}(r),

•

a graph
G,
a linkage
\mathcal{L}
of order
r
in
G,
a surface
\Sigma,

•

a sequence
(\mathcal{C}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}^{q})
where, for every
i\in[q],

\mathcal{C}^{i}
is a set of at least
s
many cycles of
G,

•

a sequence
(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{q})
of pairwise disjoint disks in
\Sigma,

•

a
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta
of
G
such that

–

for every
i\in[q],

(\delta[\Delta_{i}],c_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})
is a
q
-nested cylindrical rendition in
\Delta_{i}
around a cell
c_{i}\in C(\delta),

–

C(\delta)\setminus\{c_{i}\mid i\in[p]\}
contains only flap cells, and

–

for every
i\in[p],
the terminals of
\mathcal{L}
are either vertices of
G^{-}_{C^{i}_{1}},
for some
i\in[q],
or vertices of
G\setminus\bigcup_{i\in[q]}G\cap\Delta_{i},
and

•

a sequence
(\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{\prime}_{q})
of arc-wise connected subsets of
(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{q})
respectively such that, for every
i\in[q],
the graph
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}\cap\Delta_{i}
is drawn in
\Delta^{\prime}_{i},

outputs a linkage
\mathcal{R}
such that

1.

\mathcal{R}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{L},

2.

\mathcal{R}
is a subgraph of the graph obtained from
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}\cup(\bigcup_{i\in[q]}{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}^{i})
after removing, for every
i\in[q],
every vertex and edge of
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}^{i}
not drawn in
\Delta^{\prime}_{i},

3.

for every
i\in[q],
the number of disjoint subpaths of paths in
\mathcal{R}
with at least one endpoint in
B_{\Delta_{i}}
that contain an edge of
G^{-}_{C^{i}_{1}}
is at most
f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{2}(r),
and

4.

E(\mathcal{R})\cap E(G^{-}_{C^{i}_{1}})\neq\emptyset
for at most
f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{3}(r)
distinct indices
i\in[p]

in time

2^{\mathsf{poly}(r)}\cdot|G|^{2}.

Moreover

f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{1}(r)=\mathcal{O}(\ell(r)),

f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{2}(r)=\ell(r),
and
f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{3}(r)=2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell(r))}.

Proof.

We define
f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{1}(r)=3\ell(r)+2,

f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{2}(r)=\ell(r),
and
f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{3}(x)=(2r)^{2\ell(r)}\cdot\ell(r)+2r.

Let
\mathbf{\Delta}\coloneqq(\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{\prime}_{q}).

For brevity, we call
\mathcal{L}
a
\mathbf{\Delta}
-avoiding linkage.
For every
i\in[q],
let
\mathbf{C}_{i}
be the graph obtained from
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}_{i}
after removing every vertex and edge not drawn in
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}.

First, notice that the pair
(\mathcal{L},\mathbf{C}_{i})
is an LB-pair of
G,
for every
i\in[q].

Then, consider
\mathcal{R}
to be a
\mathbf{\Delta}
-avoiding linkage of order
r
in
G,
that is equivalent to
\mathcal{L},
is a subgraph of
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}\cup\bigcup_{i\in[q]}\mathbf{C}_{i},
and is such that for every
i\in[q],
the quantity
\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{L},\mathbf{C}_{i})
is minimized.
Clearly, by definition
\mathcal{R}
satisfies properties
1
and
2
.
We argue why
\mathcal{R}
also satisfies properties
3
and
4
.

Classifying subpaths.

Consider an index
i\in[q].

Let
\mathcal{R}_{i}
be the set containing all
B_{\Delta_{i}}
-
B_{\Delta_{i}}
-paths that are subpaths of the paths in
\mathcal{R}
and whose vertices and edges belong to
G\cap\Delta_{i}.

Also, let
\mathcal{T}_{i}
be the set containing all paths that are subpaths of the paths in
\mathcal{R}
and whose vertices and edges belong to
G\cap\Delta_{i},
with one endpoint in
B_{\Delta_{i}}
and the other being a terminal of
\mathcal{R}
that is vertex of
G^{-}_{C^{i}_{1}}.

Notice that the set
\mathcal{R}_{i}\cup\mathcal{T}_{i}
is a linkage in
G.

Now, partition
\mathcal{R}_{i}
into the set
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}
that contains all paths in
\mathcal{R}_{i}
that contain an edge of
G^{-}_{C^{i}_{1}}
and the set
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{m}}
that contains those that do not.
Also, let
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}
be the linkage containing all
V(C^{i}_{1})
-
V(C^{i}_{1})
-paths that are subpaths of the paths in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\cup\mathcal{T}_{i}
that do not intersect
G^{-}_{C^{i}_{1}}.

Finally, let
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{r}}
be the linkage containing all minimal
B_{\Delta_{i}}
-
V(C^{i}_{1})
-paths that are subpaths of the paths in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\cup\mathcal{T}_{i}.

Bounds from combing.

Consider an index
i\in[q].

Let
t_{i}\coloneqq 3{\sf tw}(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathbf{C}_{i})/2.

Let
(C^{i}_{1},\ldots,C^{i}_{s_{i}}),
where
s_{i}\geq s,
be the sequence of cycles of the nest
\mathcal{C}^{i}.

First by
Proposition 3.3
, we have that for every
i\in[q],

{\sf tw}(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathbf{C}_{i})\leq\ell(r).

Moreover, in the terminology used in
[
26
]
, the linkage
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{r}}
contains all
\mathbf{C}_{i}
-
rivers
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}
contains
\mathbf{C}_{i}
-
valleys
, while the linkage
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{m}}
contains
\mathbf{C}_{i}
-
mountains
of
\mathcal{R}.

Moreover, by an appropriate application of Lemmas 4 and 7, from
[
26
]
, we have that
|\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}|\leq{\sf tw}(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathbf{C}_{i})\leq\ell(r),
that
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}
may intersect only the first
t_{i}\leq 3\ell(r)/2
cycles of
\mathcal{C}^{i},
while
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{m}}
may intersect only the last
t_{i}\leq 3\ell(r)/2
cycles of
\mathcal{C}^{i}.

Moreover, notice that for each path in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\cup\mathcal{T}_{i},
there is at least one path in
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{r}}
that is a subpath of it.
This implies that
|\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\cup\mathcal{T}_{i}|\leq\ell(r).

Then
\mathcal{R}
also satisfies property
3
.

Bound on the number of vortices.

If
q\leq f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{3}(r)
then we can immediately conclude.
Hence assume that
q>f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{3}(r).

First, we can easily observe that the number of distinct indices
i\in[q]
for which
\mathcal{T}_{i}\neq\emptyset
is at most
2r
since
\mathcal{R}
hast at most
2r
many terminals.
Let
Q\subseteq[q]
be the set of indices for which
\mathcal{T}_{i}\neq\emptyset,

i\in Q.

Now, consider an index
i\in[q]\setminus Q.

Let
\Lambda_{i}
be the sub-sequence of
\Lambda_{\Delta_{i},y},
for a choice of
y\in V_{\Delta_{i}},
such that the set of maximal subpaths, say
\mathcal{P}_{i},
of the cycles in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
that connect vertices of the paths in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}
and that also contain at least one internal vertex that is a vertex of a path in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i},
are subgraphs of
\mathbf{C}_{i},
and such that
B_{\Lambda_{i}}
contains exactly the endpoints of the paths in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}.

Notice that, by the assumptions for
\Delta^{\prime}_{i},
such a
\Lambda_{i}
must exist.
Hence
\langle\mathcal{Q}_{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}\cup\mathcal{P}_{i},\Lambda_{i}\rangle
is a linear society where
b_{i}\coloneqq|\Lambda_{i}|\leq 2\ell(r),

r_{i}\coloneqq|\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}|=\ell(r),
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}\leq|\mathcal{C}_{i}|.

Now, fix a linear ordering
\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}}\coloneqq\langle(s_{1},t_{1}),\ldots,(s_{r},t_{r})\rangle
of the terminals of the paths in
\mathcal{R}.

We proceed to define for every
i\in[q]\setminus Q,
the signature of
\langle\mathcal{Q}_{i},\Lambda_{i}\rangle
with respect to
\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}},
which we denote by
\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{i},\Lambda_{i}),
as follows.
We define

\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{i},\Lambda_{i})\coloneqq\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{b_{i}}\rangle,

where for every
j\in[b_{i}],

x_{j}
equals
s_{n}
(resp.
t_{n}
) if the
j
-th vertex in
\Lambda_{i},
say
u,
is a vertex of the
n
-th path of
\mathcal{R}
with respect to
\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}},
say
R,
and an endpoint of the subpath
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}
of
R,
and moreover if the vertex
u
appears before (resp. after) on
R
ordered from
s_{n}
to
t_{n}
than the other endpoint of
R^{\prime}.

Observe that the above definition for
\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{i},\Lambda_{i}),
implies that the size of the set
\{\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{i},\Lambda_{i})\mid i\in[q]\}
is upper bounded by
(2r)^{2\ell(r)}.

To conclude, assume towards a contradiction, that there are more than
f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{3}(r)
distinct indices
i\in[q]
such that
E(\mathcal{R})\cap E(G^{-}_{C^{i}_{1}})\neq\emptyset.

This means that there exist
i\neq j\in[q]\setminus Q
for which
\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{i},\Lambda_{i})=\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{j},\Lambda_{j}).

W.l.o.g. observe that by a simple parity argument, there exists an index
n\in[b_{i}]
such that
x_{n}=s_{w}
and
x_{n+1}=t_{w^{\prime}},
for some
w,w^{\prime}\in[r_{i}].

Let
R_{w}
(resp.
R_{w^{\prime}}
) be the
s_{w}
-
t_{w}
-(resp.
s_{w^{\prime}}
-
t_{w^{\prime}}
)-path of
\mathcal{R}.

Let
I\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}
(resp.
O\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i}
) be the subpath of
R_{w}
(resp.
R_{w^{\prime}}
) such that
x_{n}
(resp.
x_{n+1}
) is an endpoint of
I
(resp.
O
).
Let
y
(resp.
z
) be the other endpoint of
I
(resp.
O
).
Then, let
I_{\mathsf{s}}
(resp.
I_{\mathsf{t}}
) be the minimal subpath of
I
whose endpoints are
x_{n}
(resp.
y
) and a vertex of
V(C^{i}_{1}).

Symmetrically, let
O_{\mathsf{t}}
(resp.
O_{\mathsf{s}}
) be the minimal subpath of
O
whose endpoints are
x_{n+1}
(resp.
z
) and a vertex of
V_{C^{i}_{1}}.

Also let
(P_{1},\ldots,P_{s_{i}})
be the sequence of the paths in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
ordered from innermost to outermost, and let
(P^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,P^{\prime}_{s_{i}})
be the
x_{i}
-
y_{i}
-paths that are minimal subpaths of
P_{i},
where
u_{i}\in V(I_{\mathsf{s}})
and
v_{i}\in V(O_{\mathsf{t}}).

Observe that, by the previous arguments and since the endpoints
x_{n}
and
x_{n+1}
are consecutive in
\Lambda_{i},

P^{\prime}_{t_{i}+1}
as well as
P^{\prime}_{s_{i}-t_{i}-1}
is not intersected by any other path in
\mathcal{R}_{i}.

Hence we can obtain a new linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{R}
by swapping the pairs
(s_{w},t_{w})
and
(s_{w^{\prime}},t_{w})
of the pattern of
\mathcal{R}
as follows.
We define
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{R}\setminus\{R_{w},R_{w^{\prime}}\}\cup\{S,T\},
where

S\coloneqq s_{w}R_{w}x_{n}\cup x_{n}I_{\mathsf{s}}u_{s_{i}-t_{i}-1}\cup P^{\prime}_{s_{i}-t_{i}-1}\cup v_{s_{i}-t_{i}-1}I_{\mathsf{t}}\cup yR_{w^{\prime}}t_{w^{\prime}}

and

T\coloneqq s_{w^{\prime}}R_{w^{\prime}}z\cup zOv_{t_{i}+1}\cup P^{\prime}_{t_{i}+1}\cup v_{t_{i}+1}Ox_{n+1}\cup x_{n+1}R_{w^{\prime}}t_{w^{\prime}}.

Since,
\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{i},\Lambda_{i})=\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{j},\Lambda_{j})
we can perform the exact same change within
\mathsf{sig}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathcal{Q}_{j},\Lambda_{j})
and obtain a linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}
that is equivalent to
\mathcal{R}
and we can moreover observe that
\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{i},\mathbf{C}_{i})<\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{R}_{i},\mathbf{C}_{i})
and
\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{j},\mathbf{C}_{j})<\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{R}_{j},\mathbf{C}_{j}),
which is a contradiction.
Hence
\mathcal{R}
also satisfies property
4
.
∎

4
Plowing a meadow

In this section, we obtain a preliminary version of our
\Sigma
-decomposition in the form of a meadow and our main goal is to prove
Lemma 4.3
.
That is, we show that any meadow with large enough infrastructure can be refined to a meadow that is
d
-plowed.
The first subsection deals with the creation of our preliminary meadow.
The second subsection deals with the proof of
Lemma 4.3
.

4.1
Creating a meadow

In this subsection we use the results of
[
77
]
to create our preliminary meadow.
This preliminary step makes an important case distinction for us as it will have two outcomes, either we find a large clique minor, or we obtain the preliminary meadow which will then be further refined in the following subsections.
Due to this, the only other case where we have to deal with the clique minor is in
Section 9
where we combine all of the intermediate steps into one central theorem, namely
Theorem 9.2
.

We need a definition to describe what it means for a clique-minor to be “local” with respect to some wall.
For this notice that, if a graph
G
contains
K_{t}
as a minor, there exist
t
pairwise vertex disjoint connected subgraphs
X_{1},\dots,X_{t}
such that for every pair
i,j\in[t]
there exists an edge with one end in
X_{i}
and the other in
X_{j}.

Indeed, every
K_{t}
-minor in
G
is witnessed by such a family.
Now let
W
be a
k
-wall where
k\geq t+1.

We say that a clique minor is
grasped
by
W
if there exist sets
\langle X_{i}|i\in t\rangle
as above such that for every
i\in[t]
there exists a pair
(h^{i}_{1},h^{i}_{2})\in[k]\times[k]
such that the intersection of the
h_{1}
st row and the
h_{2}
nd column of
W
is contained in
X_{i}.

Moreover, for
i\neq j
we have that
h^{i}_{1}\neq h^{j}_{1}
and
h^{i}_{2}\neq h^{j}_{2}.

Observation 4.1

(Meadow creation)
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}},f^{2}_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given positive integers
g,

k,

t
and
h,
a graph
G,
and an
f^{1}_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}}(h,k,t)
-wall
W^{\prime}
as input, finds, in time
\mathcal{O}_{h,t}(|G|^{3})
either

1.

a
K_{3k^{2}+k\cdot h}
-minor grasped by
W^{\prime},

2.

there exist non-negative integers
h^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}\leq 2
such that
2h^{\prime}+c^{\prime}=g
and a subdivision
W^{\prime}
of a walloid which is a cylindrical concatenation of
h^{\prime}

t
-handle segments and
c^{\prime}

t
-crosscap segments in
G-A
such that the base cylinder of
W^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
W,
or

3.

a set
A\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
f_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}}^{2}(h,k,t)
and an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,\Sigma)
-meadow
(G-A,\delta,W)
where
\Sigma
is of Euler genus less than
g.

Moreover, we have
x\in\mathcal{O}(k^{4}\cdot h),

y\in 2^{\mathsf{poly}_{g}(k)}\cdot t,

f^{1}_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}}(h,k,t)\in\mathsf{poly}_{g}(k,t),
and
f^{2}_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}}(h,k,t)\in 2^{\mathsf{poly}_{g}(k)}\cdot t.

For this let us first state the technical key result of
[
77
]
which acts as our point of departure.
Please notice that the following statement is an abbreviated version of Lemma 5.5 from
[
77
]
where we only state those parts of the conclusion that are relevant to our case.
The main difference to the way the Lemma is stated in
[
77
]
is that it produces a walloid representing a surface instead of a clique-minor (this walloid is actually found within the clique-minor in the proof of
[
77
]
).
For our purposes, however, the clique-minor is much more convenient.
Moreover, notice that the first and the second outcome are merged together in the original statement.
For our purposes, it is slightly more convenient to state them separately.

Lemma 4.2

(an abbreviated version of Lemma 5.5 from
[
77
]
)
.

Let
g,

k,
and
q
be non-negative integers, where
r\geq 16\cdot 12(162^{2g}+4)k
and
q\geq 6k,
and set

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle 12(162^{2g}+4)k,

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle 2(162^{2g}+4)q,

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\left\lfloor\frac{1}{10^{5}t^{26}}t^{10^{7}t^{26}}\cdot M\right\rfloor,\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle 49152\cdot t^{24}(3+4M)+\mu.

Let
W
be an
R
-wall in a graph
G.

Then either,

1.

G
contains a
K_{t}
-minor grasped by
W,

2.

there exist

•

a surface
\Sigma
of Euler-genus
g^{\prime}<g,

•

a set
A\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
\mu,
and

•

a
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D})
of
G-A
with the following properties:

(a)

\delta
is of breadth at most
2t^{2}
and depth at most
\mu,

(b)

there exist non-negative integers
h^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}\leq 2
such that
2h^{\prime}+c^{\prime}=g^{\prime}
and a subdivision
W_{\Sigma}
of a walloid which is a cylindrical concatenation of
h

q
-handle segments and
c

q
-crosscap segments in
G-A
such that
W_{\Sigma}
is grounded in
\delta,

(c)

the base cylinder of
W_{\Sigma}
is a subgraph of
W,
and

(d)

let
X
be the set of all vertices of
G
drawn in the interior of cells of
\delta
and let
G_{\delta}
be the torso of the set
V(G)\setminus(X\cup A)
in
G-A,
then
G_{\delta}
is a quasi-
4
-connected component of
G-A.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that returns one of the outcomes above in time
\mathcal{O}_{g,h,k,q}(|G|^{3})

Proof sketch of
Observation 4.1
.

To see how
Observation 4.1
follows from
Lemma 4.2
for an appropriate choice of numbers first notice that the first outcome of
Lemma 4.2
gives the first outcome of
Observation 4.1
.
So we only have to deal with the second outcome of
Lemma 4.2
.
In this outcome, we are already given the walloid
W_{\Sigma}.

In case the Euler-genus of
\Sigma
is at least
g
we find the desired walloid for the second outcome of
Observation 4.1
within
W_{\Sigma}.

Hence, we may assume that the Euler-genus of
\Sigma
is less than
g.

Let now
C
denote the exception cycle of
W_{\Sigma}
and
\Delta
be the disk bounded by its trace that avoids the simple face of
W_{\Sigma}.

Notice that
\Delta
induces a society that has a rendition in
\delta
with breadth at most
x
and depth at most
y.

By declaring this disk a new cell of
\delta
we obtain the desired meadow.
∎

4.2
The meadow plowing lemma

We are now ready to proceed with the proof of the main result of this section.
This lemma marks our first major refinement of our initial
\Sigma
-decomposition.
We prove that given a meadow with large enough infrastructure, we can maintain a still large infrastructure that has the property that we can divide into large territories (enclosures) that all have the property of being
d
-homogeneous, i.e., that small parts of the model (with respect to
d
) that invade such a territory can be represented anywhere in the area defined by the territory.

Lemma 4.3

(meadow plowing)
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},

d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

\gamma\in\mathbb{N},
and an
(x,y)
-fertile
(f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d,\gamma),\Sigma)
-meadow
(G,\delta,W),
where
\Sigma=\Sigma^{\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}}
and
\gamma=\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c},
outputs an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,\Sigma)
-meadow that is
d
-plowed in time

(t\gamma)^{2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}}}|G|^{2}.

Moreover

f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d,\gamma)\in(t\gamma)^{2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}}}.

Proof.

We define
f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d,\gamma)\coloneqq\max\{f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d))\cdot\gamma+2t,f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d))/\gamma,4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d))\cdot\gamma+f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d))\}.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface of Euler-genus
2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c},

\gamma\coloneqq\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c},

t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},

d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
and
(G,\delta,W)
be an
(x,y)
-fertile
(f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d^{\prime},\gamma),\Sigma)
-meadow, where
d^{\prime}\coloneqq|d\text{-}\textsf{folio}(\delta)|,
which by
Observation 2.2
is at most
f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d).

Our proof works as follows.
We first show how to define a
(t,\Sigma)
-walloid
W^{\prime}
that is the cylindrical concatenation of
\mathsf{h}
many
t
-handle segments and
\mathsf{c}
many
t
-crosscap segments, as a subgraph of
W,
with the property that all its
2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+1
enclosures are
d
-homogeneous in
\delta.

Then, we show how to define a
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta^{\prime}
from
\delta
such that
(G,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
is the required
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,\Sigma)
-meadow
(G,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
that is
d
-plowed.

Finding a homogeneous subwall.

Consider the graph
\widetilde{W}
which is the base cylinder of
W
obtained by the cylindrical concatenation of the base walls of its
\gamma
many
f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d,\gamma)
-handle and crosscap segments.
The base wall of each of these is by definition a
(4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d,\gamma),f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d,\gamma))
-wall.
As a result
\widetilde{W}
is a
(4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d,\gamma)\cdot\gamma,f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}(t,d,\gamma))
-cylindrical wall.
Then, by definition of
f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}},
there is a
(f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,d)\cdot\gamma+2t,d),f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,d))
-wall
W_{2}
that is a subwall of
\widetilde{W}
and that is disjoint from the first
4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,d)\cdot\gamma
cycles of
\widetilde{W}.

Now, we define the
d^{\prime}
-palette of every brick
B
of
W_{2}
as follows.
Observe that
B
is a fence of
W
and as such its
d
-folio is well-defined.
Consider any bijection
g:d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)\to[d^{\prime}]
and let
d^{\prime}\text{-}\mathsf{palette}(B)=g(d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(B)).

Next, we apply
Lemma 3.1
for
d^{\prime}
and
W_{2}
and we obtain a
(4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,d)\cdot\gamma+2t,2t)
-wall
W_{3}
that is a subwall of
W_{2}
and such that every brick of
W_{3}
has the same
d^{\prime}
-palette, i.e., every brick of
W_{3}
has the same
d
-folio.

Defining the base cylinder.

Let
b\coloneqq 4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,d)\cdot\gamma+2t
and
a\coloneqq 2t.

Let
P^{2}_{1},\ldots,P^{2}_{a}
be the horizontal paths of
W_{2}
and
Q^{2}_{1},\ldots,Q^{2}_{b}
be the vertical paths of
W_{2}.

We define
t
pairwise disjoint cycles
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{t}\}
as follows.
For every
i\in[t],
define
C_{i}\coloneqq P^{2}_{i}\cup P^{2}_{a-i+1}\cup Q^{2}_{i}\cup Q^{2}_{b-i+1}.

Next we define
b^{\prime}\coloneqq b-2t
pairwise disjoint paths
\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{b^{\prime}}\}
that are subpaths of vertical paths in
W_{2}
as follows.
For every
i\in[t],
define
P_{i}
to be the subpath of
Q^{2}_{t+i}
with one endpoint in
P^{2}_{1}
and the other in
P^{2}_{t}.

Observe that the graph
W_{3}\coloneqq{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\}
is a
(4\cdot f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,d)\cdot\gamma,t)
-cylindrical wall that is a subgraph of
W_{2}.

Moreover, notice that every brick of
W_{3}
has the same
d
-folio as it is a union of bricks of
W_{2},
and all such bricks have the same
d
-folio.

Attaching the handle and crosscap linkages.

Let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(2t,d).

Let
\mathcal{W}=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{\gamma})
be a sequence of
\mathsf{h}
many handle and
\mathsf{c}
-crosscap segments such that
W
is the cylindrical concatenation of
\mathcal{W}.

Additionally, let
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}=(W^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,W^{\prime}_{\gamma})
be a sequence of
(4t^{\prime},t)
-walls such that
W_{3}
is the cylindrical concatenation of
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}.

For each
i\in[\mathsf{h}],
let
\mathcal{L}_{i}\coloneqq\{L_{i,1},\ldots,L_{i,t^{\prime}}\}
be the first
t^{\prime}
paths in left to right order of the leftmost rainbow of
W_{i}.
Let
u_{i,j}
and
v_{i,j}
be the top boundary vertices of the base wall of
W_{i}
in left to right order that are endpoints of
L_{i,j},

j\in[t^{\prime}].

Also, let
\mathcal{R}_{i}\coloneqq\{R_{i,1},\ldots R_{i,t^{\prime}}\}
be the first
t^{\prime}
paths in left to right order of the rightmost rainbow of
W_{i}.
Let
w_{i,j}
and
z_{i,j}
be the top boundary vertices of the base wall of
W_{i}
in left to right order that are endpoints of
R_{i,j},

j\in[t^{\prime}].

Additionally, let
u^{\prime}_{i,1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{i,t^{\prime}},w^{\prime}_{i,1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{i,t^{\prime}},v^{\prime}_{i,1},\ldots v^{\prime}_{i,t^{\prime}},z^{\prime}_{i,1},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{i,t^{\prime}}
be the top boundary vertices of
W^{\prime}_{i}
in left to right order.

For each
i\in(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c}],
let
\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneqq\{T_{i,1},\ldots,T_{i,2t^{\prime}}\}
be the first
2t^{\prime}
paths in left to right order of the rainbow of
W_{i}.
Let
u_{i,j}
and
v_{i,j}
be the top boundary vertices of the base wall of
W_{i}
in left to right order that are endpoints of
T_{i,j},

j\in[t^{\prime}].

Additionally, let
u^{\prime}_{i,1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{i,2t^{\prime}},v^{\prime}_{i,1},\ldots v^{\prime}_{i,2t^{\prime}}
be the top boundary vertices of
W^{\prime}_{i}
in left to right order.

Our next target is to identify for every
i\in[\mathsf{h}],
a set
\mathcal{Y}_{i}=\mathcal{U}_{i}\cup\mathcal{V}_{i}\cup\mathcal{W}_{i}\cup\mathcal{Z}_{i}
of
4t^{\prime}
many paths such that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\{U_{i,1},\ldots,U_{i,t^{\prime}}\}
where, for
j\in[t^{\prime}],

U_{i,j}
is a
u_{i,j}
-
u^{\prime}_{i,j}
-path,
\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{V_{i,1},\ldots,V_{i,t^{\prime}}\}
where, for
j\in[t^{\prime}],

V_{i,j}
is a
v_{i,j}
-
v^{\prime}_{i,j}
-path,
\mathcal{W}_{i}=\{W_{i,1},\ldots,W_{i,t^{\prime}}\}
where, for
j\in[t^{\prime}],

W_{i,j}
is a
w_{i,j}
-
w^{\prime}_{i,j}
-path, and
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\{Z_{i,1},\ldots,Z_{i,t^{\prime}}\}
where, for
j\in[t^{\prime}],

Z_{i,j}
is a
z_{i,j}
-
z^{\prime}_{i,j}
-path.
Moreover, to identify for every
i\in(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c}],
a set
\mathcal{Y}_{i}=\mathcal{U}_{i}\cup\mathcal{V}_{i}
of
4t^{\prime}
many paths such that
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\{U_{i,1},U_{i,2t^{\prime}}\}
where, for
j\in[2t^{\prime}],

U_{i,j}
is a
u_{i,j}
-
u^{\prime}_{i,j}
-path and
\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{V_{i,1},\ldots,V_{i,2t^{\prime}}\}
where, for
j\in[2t^{\prime}],

V_{i,j}
is a
v_{i,j}
-
v^{\prime}_{i,j}
path.
We also want the paths
\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{4t^{\prime}\gamma}\}
in
\bigcup_{i\in[\gamma]}\{\mathcal{Y}_{i}\}
to be pairwise disjoint and also internally disjoint from
W_{3}.

We show how to define the
x
-
y
-path
Y_{i},

i\in[4t^{\prime}\gamma],
where
x
and
y
are the appropriate two vertices corresponding to the path
Y_{i}
as previously defined.
Consider
C_{j}
to be the
j
-th cycle of the base cylinder
\widetilde{W}
of
W
from top to bottom and
Q_{j}
to be the
j
-th vertical path of
\widetilde{W}
from left to right, where
Q_{1}
is the leftmost vertical path of
W_{1}.

Also let
\ell
be minimum such that
C_{\ell+1}
is not disjoint from
W_{3}.

Note that
\ell\geq 4t^{\prime}\gamma.

Assume w.l.o.g. that
x
is the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{x}}
in
C_{1}\cap Q_{i_{x}}
while
y
is the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{y}}
in
C_{\ell}\cap Q_{i_{y}}.

We distinguish three cases.

If
i_{x}=i_{y}
then
Y_{i}
is the subpath of
Q_{i_{x}}
whose endpoints are
x
and
y.

If
i_{x}<i_{y}
then
Y_{i}
is the union of the subpath of
Q_{i_{x}}
whose endpoints are
x
and the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{x}}
in

the subpath of
C_{\ell-i_{x}+1}
whose endpoints are the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{x}}
in
Q_{i_{x}}\cap C_{\ell-i_{x}+1}
and the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{y}}
in
Q_{i_{y}}\cap C_{\ell-i_{x}+1}
and whose internal vertices that belong to vertical paths are vertices of
Q_{j},

j\in(i_{x},i_{y}),
and the subpath of
Q_{i_{y}}
whose endpoints are
y
and the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{y}}
in
Q_{i_{y}}\cap C_{\ell-i_{x}+1}.

If
i_{x}>i_{y}
then
Y_{i}
is the union of the subpath of
Q_{i_{x}}
whose endpoints are
x
and the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{x}}
in
Q_{i_{x}}\cap C_{i_{x}},
the subpath of
C_{i_{x}}
whose endpoints are the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{x}}
in
Q_{i_{x}}\cap C_{i_{x}}
and the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{y}}
in
Q_{i_{y}}\cap C_{i_{x}}
and whose internal vertices that belong to vertical paths are vertices of
Q_{j},

j\in(i_{y},i_{x}),
and the subpath of
Q_{i_{y}}
whose endpoints are
y
and the topmost vertex of
Q_{i_{y}}
in
Q_{i_{y}}\cap C_{i_{x}}.

It is easy to see that the defined set of paths
\mathcal{Y}
are pairwise disjoint and also internally disjoint from
W_{3}.

Moreover by their definition, the graph
W_{4}\coloneqq{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{W_{2}\}\cup\mathcal{Y}
is a
(t^{\prime},\Sigma)
-walloid that is a subgraph of
W
and that is the cylindrical concatenation of
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\{W^{\prime\prime}_{i}\mid i\in[\gamma]\},
where
W^{\prime\prime}_{i},
for
i\in[\mathsf{h}],
is the
t^{\prime}
-handle segment obtained by the union of
W^{\prime}_{i},
the two rainbows of
W_{i},
and the linkage
\mathcal{Y}_{i},
and where
W^{\prime\prime}_{i},
for
i\in(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c}],
is a
t^{\prime}
-crosscap segment obtained by the union of
W^{\prime}_{i},
the rainbow of
W_{i},
and the linkage
\mathcal{Y}_{i}.

Moreover, the big enclosure of the base cylinder
\widetilde{W}_{4}
of
W_{4}
is
d
-homogeneous, since
\widetilde{W}_{4}
is the previously defined cylindrical wall
W_{3}
whose bricks have the same
d
-folio.

Homogenizing them.

For
i\in[\mathsf{h}],
we define the graph
L_{i}
as the union of the leftmost enclosure of
W^{\prime\prime}_{i},
the leftmost rainbow of
W_{i},
the linkage
\mathcal{U}_{i},
and the linkage
\mathcal{W}_{i}.

Symmetrically, we define the graph
R_{i}
as the union of the rightmost enclosure of
W^{\prime\prime}_{i},
the rightmost rainbow of
W_{i},
the linkage
\mathcal{V}_{i},
and the linkage
\mathcal{Z}_{i}.

Similarly, for
i\in(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c}],
we define the graph

as the union of the enclosure of

the rainbow of
W_{i},
the linkage
\mathcal{U}_{i},
and the linkage
\mathcal{V}_{i}.

Observe that the graphs

and

are

-ladders where each of their

many horizontal paths contains a common vertex with two vertical paths of

Also the graphs

are
2t^{\prime}
-ladders where each of their

many horizontal paths contains a common vertex with two vertical paths of

Observe that every brick of each of the graphs

is a fence of

and as such its

-folio is defined.
In the same way as in step 1 we can define the

-palette of each of these bricks and then apply

, to obtain

-ladders

and

-ladders

whose bricks individually all have the same

-folio.
Finally, we can obtain our desired
(t,\Sigma)
-walloid

by considering a sub-cylindrical wall of
W_{3}
that contains all cycles of
W_{3}
but only the vertical paths that contain an endpoint of some horizontal path of some
L^{\prime}_{i},R^{\prime}_{i},S^{\prime}_{i},
union all
L^{\prime}_{i},R^{\prime}_{i},S^{\prime}_{i}.

Notice that all
2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+1
enclosures of

are now

-homogeneous.

To conclude it remains to define the desired
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}).

We obtain
\delta^{\prime}
from
\delta
by considering the same drawing of
G
in
\Sigma,

\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,
and defining the cells
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
as follows.
Let
\Delta
be the
\mathsf{trace}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk

where

is the exceptional cycle of
W_{5}
while

is the simple cycle of

Define
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
to contain all cells of
\delta
not drawn in
\Delta,
as well as a new cell that is the closure of all cells of
\delta
drawn in
\Delta.

it is easy to observe that
\delta^{\prime}
is indeed as required.
∎

5
Sowing a garden

In this section, we obtain the second major refinement of our
\Sigma
-decomposition in the form of a
d
-blooming garden and our main goal is to prove
Lemma 5.4
.
We show how given a
d
-plowed meadow with enough infrastructure, we can refine it into a garden that is
d
-blooming.

5.1
Planting the seeds

In this subsection, we first introduce the notion of a fence at some distance from some other fence in a walloid, which will be useful for several arguments concerning the routing of paths in our walloid in the following proofs.
We then prove a couple of auxiliary results that will serve as intermediate steps towards the proof of
Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(G,\delta,W)
be a
(t,\Sigma)
-meadow of a graph
G
in a surface
\Sigma
for some
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}.

Let
F
be a fence of
W.

We say that
F_{0}\coloneqq F
is the fence
at distance

0

from

F
in
W.

Given
r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
we define recursively the fence at distance
r
from
F
in
W
as follows.
If
F_{r-1}
is disjoint from
C^{\mathsf{si}}
and
C^{\mathsf{ex}},
then
F_{r}
is the unique fence of
W
whose bricks are exactly the bricks of
F_{r-1}
union all bricks of
W
that share a vertex with some brick of
F_{r-1}.

We call a fence
F
of
W

r
-
internal
if the fence at distance
r
from
F
is well-defined.

Given an
1
-internal fence
F
of
W,
we define its
internal pegs
(resp.
external pegs
) as its vertices that are incident to edges of
W
that are not edges of
F
and that are edges of bricks of
F
(resp. of bricks that are not bricks of
F
).

It follows from the properties of
\delta
that for every flap cell
c
of
\delta
there are
|\widetilde{c}|
vertex-disjoint paths to any
|\widetilde{c}|
many distinct vertices not in
\sigma_{\delta}(c).

This observation allows us to use the following proposition which is a restatement of
[
7
, Lemma 22]
in the terminology used in this text.

Proposition 5.1

(Lemma 22,
[
7
]
)
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},

\Sigma
be a surface, and
(G,\delta,W)
be a
(t,\Sigma)
-meadow of
G.

For every
2
-internal brick
B
of
W
and every flap cell
c
in the
\delta
-influence of
B,

W
contains
|\widetilde{c}|
vertex-disjoint paths from
\widetilde{c}
to the external pegs of the fence
F
at distance
1
from
B
in
W.

Moreover these paths are drawn inside of the
(\mathsf{trace}(C^{\mathsf{si}})\cap N(\delta))
-avoiding disk of the trace of the fence at distance
2
from
B
in
W.

The following lemma will be useful in order to extract the flower segments needed to obtain the desired garden in
Lemma 5.4
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},

\Sigma
be a surface, and
(G,\delta,W)
be a
(t,\Sigma)
-meadow of
G.

For every
4
-internal brick
B
of
W
and every flap cell
c
in the
\delta
-influence of
B,
for every
c
-society
(G_{c},\Lambda_{c,y}),

W
contains
|\widetilde{c}|
vertex-disjoint paths from
\widetilde{c}
to the bottom boundary vertices of a
(10\times 10)
-wall
W^{\prime}
that is a subgraph of
W
and contains all bricks of the fence at distance
4
from
B,
such that the endpoints of these paths not in
\widetilde{c}
respect the linear ordering
\Lambda_{c,y}.

Proof.

Let
B
be a
4
-internal brick of
W,

c
be a flap cell in the
\delta
-influence of
B,
and fix a
c
-society
(G_{c},\Lambda_{c,y}).
Moreover, let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{4}
denote the fences at distances
1
up to
5
from
B
and
W^{\prime}
be a
(10\times 10)
-wall
W^{\prime}
that is a subgraph of
W
that is a wall and contains all bricks of
F_{4}.

We argue about how to find the desired paths when
|\widetilde{c}|=3.
Then, the other cases follow immediately.
W.l.o.g. we assume that the rotation tie-breaker gives
c
the counter-clockwise rotation.
Let
\Lambda_{c,y}=\langle x,y,z\rangle.

First apply
Proposition 5.1
for
B
and
c,
and let
P_{x},P_{y},P_{z}
be the implied paths where
x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}
are their respective endpoints in
F_{1}.

Observe that
F_{1}
has
12
external pegs which can be partitioned in four sets
T,L,B,R,
where
T
contains the
4
top external pegs,
L
contains the
2
leftmost external pegs,
B
contains the
4
bottom pegs, and
R
contains the
2
rightmost pegs.

We begin visiting the external pegs of
F
in counter-clockwise ordering starting from
z^{\prime}.

Respecting this ordering the next vertex to appear is
x^{\prime}
and then
y^{\prime}.

We proceed to distinguish cases depending on where the vertices
x^{\prime},

y^{\prime},
and
z^{\prime}
are on
F_{1},
and define three paths
P_{x^{\prime}},P_{y^{\prime}},P_{z^{\prime}}
as follows.

Case distinction for
z^{\prime}.

z^{\prime}\in T.

We define the path
P_{z^{\prime}}
starting from
z^{\prime},
moving upwards towards
F_{4}
following the vertical path of
W^{\prime}
that contains
z^{\prime},
following
F_{4}
in clockwise direction until we reach a vertex of
F_{4}
that belongs to the rightmost possible vertical path of
W^{\prime},
and then following this vertical path downwards to its bottom boundary vertex of
W^{\prime}.

Case distinction for
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime},
when
z^{\prime}\in T.

x^{\prime}\in T
(resp.
y^{\prime}\in T
).
We define the path
P_{x^{\prime}}
(resp.
P_{y^{\prime}}
) starting from
x^{\prime}
(resp.
y^{\prime}
), moving upwards towards
F_{3}
(resp.
F_{2}
) following the vertical path of
W^{\prime}
that contains
x^{\prime}
(resp.
y^{\prime}
), following
F_{3}
(resp.
F_{2}
) in counter-clockwise direction until we reach a vertex of
F_{3}
(resp.
F_{2}
) that belongs to the leftmost possible vertical path of
W^{\prime},
and then following this vertical path downwards to its bottom boundary vertex of
W^{\prime}.

x^{\prime}\in L
(resp.
y^{\prime}\in L
).
We define the path
P_{x^{\prime}}
(resp.
P_{y^{\prime}}
) starting from
x^{\prime}
(resp.
y^{\prime}
), moving to the left towards
F_{3}
(resp.
F_{2}
) following the horizontal path of
W^{\prime}
that contains
x^{\prime}
(resp.
y^{\prime}
), and then following the vertical path that contains the vertex of
F_{3}
(resp.
F_{2}
) we encountered downwards to its bottom boundary vertex of
W^{\prime}.

x^{\prime}\in B
(resp.
y^{\prime}\in B
).
We define the path
P_{x^{\prime}}
(resp.
P_{y^{\prime}}
) starting from
x^{\prime}
(resp.
y^{\prime}
), moving downwards towards
F_{3}
(resp.
F_{2}
) following the vertical path of
W^{\prime}
that contains
x^{\prime}
(resp.
y^{\prime}
), and then following the vertical path that contains the vertex of
F_{3}
(resp.
F_{2}
) we encountered downwards to its bottom boundary vertex of
W^{\prime}.

x^{\prime}\in R
(resp.
y^{\prime}\in R
).
We define the path
P_{x^{\prime}}
(resp.
P_{y^{\prime}}
) starting from
x^{\prime}
(resp.
y^{\prime}
), moving to the right towards
F_{2}
(resp.
F_{3}
) following the horizontal path of
W^{\prime}
that contains
x^{\prime}
(resp.
y^{\prime}
), and then following the vertical path that contains the vertex of
F_{2}
(resp.
F_{3}
) we encountered downwards to its bottom boundary vertex of
W^{\prime}.

We skip the remaining cases in favor of brevity and note that they are similar to the first.
Now, observe that, by the specifications of
Proposition 5.1
and by definition, the paths
P_{x}\cup P_{x^{\prime}},

P_{y}\cup P_{y^{\prime}},
and
P_{z}\cup P_{z^{\prime}}
are indeed vertex-disjoint with one endpoint in
\widetilde{c}
and the other being a bottom boundary vertex of
W^{\prime},
respecting the order of
\Lambda_{c,v},
ordered left to right.
∎

5.2
The garden creation lemma

We require one more auxiliary lemma that shows how to further refine our garden by extracting a parterre segment that represents a linear society of the
d
-folio of our
\Sigma
-decomposition which is not yet represented by any other parterre segment.

Lemma 5.3
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},

b,h,d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

•

an
(x,y)
-fertile
d
-plowed
(f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h),b,h,\Sigma)
-garden
(G,\delta,W),
and

•

a linear society
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle\in d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta),

outputs an
(x,y)
-fertile
d
-plowed
(t,b,h,\Sigma)
-garden
(G,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
such that

•

the set of linear societies of the parterre segments of
W^{\prime}
contains
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle,

•

is a strict superset of the set of linear societies of the parterre segments of
W,
and

•

the
d
-folio of
\delta^{\prime}
is the same as the
d
-folio of
\delta

in time

\mathcal{O}(h(t+b)|G|^{2}).

Moreover

f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h)\in\mathcal{O}(h(t+b)).

Proof.

We define
f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h)\coloneqq t+6+h\cdot(2t+10\cdot 3b).

Assume that the Euler-genus of
\Sigma
is
2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}.

Let
W
be the cylindrical contatenation of a sequence
\mathcal{W}=(W_{1},W^{\mathsf{h}}_{1},\ldots,W^{\mathsf{h}}_{\mathsf{h}},W^{\mathsf{c}}_{1},\ldots,W^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathsf{c}},W^{\mathsf{p}}_{1},\ldots,W^{\mathsf{p}}_{\ell}),
where
W_{1}
is a
(f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h),f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h))
-wall segment,
W^{\mathsf{h}}_{1},\ldots,W^{\mathsf{h}}_{\mathsf{h}}
are
f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h)
-handle segments,
W^{\mathsf{c}}_{1},\ldots,W^{\mathsf{c}}_{\mathsf{c}}
are
f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h)
-crosscap segments, and
W^{\mathsf{p}}_{1},\ldots,W^{\mathsf{p}}_{\ell}
are
(f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h),h)
-parterre segments.

Let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be the given linear society in
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta).

By definition
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle=\mathsf{dissolve}(\mu),
where
\mu
is a fiber of a
c
-society
\langle G_{c},\Lambda_{c,x}\rangle
of some cell
c\in C_{\mathsf{f}}(\delta)
of detail at most
d,
where
\Lambda
is a shift of
\Lambda_{c,x}.

First, notice that
c
belongs in the
\delta
-influence of at least one of the enclosures of
W.

Moreover, since
(G,\delta,W)
is
d
-plowed, i.e., all
2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+1
enclosures of
W
are
d
-homogeneous, the
\delta
-influence of every brick of an enclosure whose
\delta
-influence contains
c,
contains a cell
c^{\prime}\in C_{\mathsf{f}}(\delta)
such that a
c^{\prime}
-society of
\delta
has the same
d
-folio as
\langle G_{c},\Lambda_{c,x}\rangle.

The proof proceeds by demonstrating how to extract a
(t,b,h,H,\Lambda)
-parterre segment depending on which enclosure’s
\delta
-influence contains
c
and then showing how to obtain a new walloid
W^{\prime}
and a new
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta^{\prime}
from
W
and
\delta
respectively, that also contains this new parterre segment, in a way that maintains all the desired properties.

First, let us observe the following.
Let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq t+6+h\cdot(2t+10b),
and
F_{1}\coloneqq C^{\mathsf{ex}}.

For every fence
F_{r},

r\in[t^{\prime}],
at distance
r
from
C^{\mathsf{ex}},
we can naturally define a
(t^{\prime}-r,\Sigma)
-walloid
W_{r}
with the same number of handle and crosscap segments and with the same number of parterre segments whose linear societies are the same, where only their order changes.
Indeed, let
W_{r}
be the graph obtained by the union of the cycles of the base cylindrical wall of
W
that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
do not intersect, the paths of the rainbows all handles, crosscap, and parterre segments that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
do not intersect, the vertical paths of
W
that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
do not intersect, all linear societies of the parterre segments, and the maximal subpaths of all vertical paths of the walloid’s wall segment whose endpoints lie in cycles that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
do not intersect.

Observe that
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
intersect the cycles
C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}
of the base cylindrical wall of
W,
the first
r
paths of both rainbows of handle segments, the first
r
and the last
r
paths of rainbows of crosscap segments, the first
r
paths of rainbows of parterre segments, as well as all vertical paths that are not disjoint from the former rainbow paths.
Also, note that the linear society of each parterre segment is unaffected as we simply extend the boundary with paths towards the new top boundary vertices of the new base cylindrical wall of
W^{\prime}.

Finally, note that the number of vertical paths in the wall segment of
W_{r}
is the same as in
W.

Figure 11
:
Extracting a flower from a crosscap segment.

Extracting from a crosscap enclosure.

Let
p\coloneqq 2t+10b
and
q\coloneqq hp.

Consider the fences
\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\{F_{1}\ldots F_{hp+6}\}
at distance
0
up to
hp+5
from
F_{1}.

Let
W^{\mathsf{c}}\coloneqq W^{\mathsf{c}}_{i},
for a choice of

such that

is in the
\delta
-influence of the enclosure of
W^{\mathsf{c}}_{i}.

Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{t^{\prime}}
be the horizontal and
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{4t^{\prime}}
be the vertical paths of the base wall of
W^{\mathsf{c}}
in top to bottom and left to right order respectively.
Moreover, let
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{2t^{\prime}}\}
be the rainbow of
W^{\mathsf{c}}
in left to right order.
Observe that
P_{i},

i\in[hp+5]
is a subpath of
F_{i},
and that
R_{i}
and
R_{2t^{\prime}-i+1}
are also subpaths of
F_{i}.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{h}
be
h
many sets each containing a sequence of
b
many bricks, say
\mathcal{B}_{i}=(B^{i}_{1},\ldots,B^{i}_{b})
of
W
ordered from the leftmost to the rightmost (respecting the ordering of
\mathcal{R}
) obtained as follows.
For every
\mathcal{B}_{i},

i\in[h],
we reserve a bundle of
p
many consecutive cycles of
\mathcal{F}
namely
\mathcal{F}^{i}\coloneqq\{F_{6+(i-1)\cdot p+1},\ldots,F_{6+i\cdot p}\}.

We choose
B^{i}_{j},

j\in[b]
as the brick of the enclosure of
W^{\mathsf{c}}
such that
B^{i}_{j}\cap R_{6+(i-1)\cdot p+10\cdot(j-1)+5}\neq\emptyset
and
B^{i}_{j}\cap R_{6+(i-1)\cdot p+10\cdot(j-1)+6}\neq\emptyset
(see
Figure 11
).

Observe that by definition for every brick
B^{i}_{j},

i\in[h],

j\in[b],
there is a
(10\times 10)
-wall
W^{i}_{j}
that contains all bricks of the fence at distance
4
from
B^{i}_{j}
and such that it is disjoint from any

i\neq i^{\prime}\in[h]
and
j\neq j^{\prime}\in[b],
and it is disjoint from the first

and the last
t
cycles in
\mathcal{F}^{i}.

Moreover exactly
2
subpaths of each of the horizontal paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{5}
correspond to horizontal paths of
W^{i}_{j}.
We assume that the bottom horizontal path of
W^{i}_{j}
is the rightmost such subpath of
P_{5}.

Now for every
i\in[h],

j\in[b],
we are in the position to apply
Lemma 5.2
to the brick
B^{i}_{j},
for a
c^{i}_{j}
-society of a cell
c^{i}_{j}\in C_{\mathsf{f}}(\delta)
whose

-torso is the same as that of
\langle G_{c},\Lambda_{c,x}\rangle,
and obtain as a result a set
\mathcal{P}^{i}_{j}
of
|\widetilde{c}^{i}_{j}|
many vertex-disjoint paths from
\widetilde{c}^{i}_{j}
to the bottom boundary vertices of
W^{i}_{j},
such that the endpoints of these paths not in
\widetilde{c}^{i}_{j}
respect the linear ordering of the chosen
c^{i}_{j}
-society.
Let
\Lambda^{i}_{j}

If we replace
