Figure 13
:
Extracting a flower segment from a wall segment.

We are now in the position to prove the main result of this section, which is an iterative application of
Lemma 5.3
.
Our goal is to represent each linear society of the
d
-folio of our
\Sigma
-decomposition by a parterre segment containing many enough copies of the linear society it represents.

Lemma 5.4

(Garden creation)
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{lem_garden_creation}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3},

b,h,d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
and an
(x,y)
-fertile
(f_{\ref{lem_garden_creation}}(t,b,h,d),\Sigma)
-meadow
(G,\delta,W)
that is
d
-plowed, outputs an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,\Sigma)
-garden that is
d
-blooming in time

h(t+b)2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}\cdot|G|^{2}.

Moreover

f_{\ref{lem_garden_creation}}(t,b,h,d)\in h(t+b)2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}.

Proof.

We define
f_{\ref{lem_garden_creation}}(t,b,h,d)\coloneqq f_{\ref{lem_blooming_one_step}}(t,b,h)\cdot f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d).

The proof of the lemma is an iterative application of
Lemma 5.3
applied each time to a linear society of the
d
-folio of the corresponding
\Sigma
-decomposition that is not represented by the parterre segments of the corresponding walloid.
First observe that by definition
(G,\delta,W)
can be seen as an
(x,y)
-fertile
d
-plowed
(f_{\ref{lem_garden_creation}}(t,b,h,d),b,h,\Sigma)
-garden with no parterre segments.

Let
(G,\delta_{0},W_{0})=(G,\delta,W)
and
i=0.

Iteratively apply
Lemma 5.3
on
(G,\delta_{i},W_{i})
with a linear society
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle\in d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta_{i})
that is not the linear society of any parterre segment of
W_{i}.

Let
(G,\delta_{i+1},W_{i+1})
be the resulting garden. Set
i\coloneqq i+1,
and repeat.

Observe that the above procedure will repeat at most
d^{\prime}\coloneqq|d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)|
times, since the
d
-folio of any of the intermediate
\Sigma
-decompositions is the same.
Then, we conclude with the
(G,\delta_{d^{\prime}},W_{d^{\prime}})

(t,b,h,\Sigma)
-garden, which by the specifications of
Lemma 5.3
has all the desired properties.
∎

6
Growing an orchard

The goal of this subsection is to prove
Lemma 6.2
.
That is, we show that any
d
-blooming garden with big enough infrastructure can be transformed into an orchard that is both
d
-blooming and ripe.
This step marks the last refinement of our
\Sigma
-decomposition before the harvest can begin.

Observation 6.1

(Garden seen as a single thicket orchard)
.

There is an algorithm that, given an
s\in\mathbb{N}
and a
(x,y)
-fertile and
d
-blooming

(t+1+s,b,h,d,\Sigma)
-garden
(G,\delta,W),
outputs an
(x,y)
-fertile and
d
-blooming single-thicket
(t,b,h,s,d,\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W)
in time
\mathcal{O}(t\cdot s\cdot(b+h)|G|).

Lemma 6.2

(Bounding the depth of a orchard)
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}},f^{2}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}},f^{3}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}},f^{4}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{7}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given an
(x,y)
-fertile and
d
-blooming single-thicket
(t^{\prime},b,h,s^{\prime},\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W)
where

\displaystyle t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq f^{1}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},x,y,d)\text{ and}

\displaystyle s^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq f^{2}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},x,y,d),

outputs a
(q,p)
-ripe and
d
-blooming
(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},\Sigma)
-orchard in time
2^{{t^{*}}^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}|G|^{3}
where

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\leq f^{3}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},x,y,d)\text{ and}

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\leq f^{4}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},x,y,d).

The proof of
Lemma 6.2
will be an inductive one via a sequence of lemmas which we derive in the following subsections.

6.1
Developing the tools

Before we begin, we need some preliminary results on transactions in societies.

Strip societies.

Since our goal is to refine an
(x,y)
-fertile blooming garden, or better an
(x,y)
-fertile orchard with a single thicket, it suffices to deal with societies for which we already know that there exists a rendition in a disk with at most
x
vortices, each of depth at most
y.

This immediately allows us to deduce that any large enough transaction in such a society contains a still large subtransaction which is planar and satisfies a number of additional properties.

Let
G
be a graph and
H
be a subgraph of
G.

An
H
-bridge
is either an edge
e
with both endpoints in
H
such that
e\notin E(H),
or a subgraph of
G
formed by a component
K
of
G-V(H)
along with all edges of
G
with one endpoint in
V(K)
and the other endpoint in
V(H).

In the first case, we call the endpoints of
e
the
attachments
of the bridge, and in the second case the
attachments
of the bridge are those vertices that do not belong to
K.

Let
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
be a society and
\mathcal{P}
be a planar transaction of order at least two in
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle.

The
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
is defined as follows.
Let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{\ell}\}
be ordered
18
18

18

We assume here that neither the segment
a_{1}\Lambda a_{\ell}
nor the segment
b_{\ell}\Lambda b_{1}
contains both endpoints of
\Lambda.
If this is not the case we may slightly rotate
\Lambda
to obtain a new linear ordering
\Lambda^{\prime}
neither segment contains both endpoints and use
\Lambda^{\prime}
to derive the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle.
such that for each
i\in[\ell],

P_{i}
has the endpoints
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
and
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{\ell},b_{\ell},b_{\ell-1},\dots,b_{1}
appear in
\Lambda
in the order listed.
We denote by
H
the subgraph of
G
defined by the union of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
together with all vertices of
\Lambda
and by
H^{\prime}
the subgraph of
H
consisting of
\mathcal{P}
together with the vertices in
V(a_{1}\Lambda a_{\ell})\cup V(b_{\ell}\Lambda b_{1}).

We consider the set
\mathcal{B}
of all
H
-bridges of
G
with at least one vertex in
V(H^{\prime})\setminus V(P_{1}\cup P_{\ell}).

For each
B\in\mathcal{B}
let
B^{\prime}
be obtained from
B
by deleting all attachments that do not belong to
V(H^{\prime}).

Finally, let us denote by
G_{1}
the graph defined as the union of
H^{\prime}
and all
B^{\prime}
where
B\in\mathcal{B}
and let
\Lambda_{1}\coloneqq a_{1}\Lambda a_{\ell}\oplus b_{\ell}\Lambda b_{1}.

The resulting society
\langle G_{1},\Lambda_{1}\rangle
is the desired
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle.

The paths
P_{1}
and
P_{\ell}
are called the
boundary paths
of
\langle G_{1},\Lambda_{1}\rangle.

We say that the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society
\langle G_{1},\Lambda_{1}\rangle
of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
is
isolated
if no edge of
G
has one endpoint in
V(G_{1})\setminus V(P_{1}\cup P_{\ell})
and the other endpoint in
V(G)\setminus V(G_{1}).

Moreover, an isolated
\mathcal{P}
-strip society is separating if after deleting any non-boundary path of
\mathcal{P}
there does not exist a path from one of the two resulting segments of
\Lambda
to the other.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
t,x\geq 1,

p,s,y\geq 2,
and
b,h
be positive integers.
Moreover, let
(G,\delta,W)
be an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard and let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be a thicket society of
(G,\delta,W).

Then every transaction in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
of order at least
(x+1)(2xy+p)
contains a planar transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
such that the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is isolated, separating, and rural.

Proof.

Since
(G,\delta,W)
is
(x,y)
-fertile there exists a rendition
\rho
of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
in the disk with at most
x
vortices, each of depth at most
y.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a transaction of order at least
(x+1)(2y+p)
in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle.

Then there exist two disjoint segments, say
I
and
J
such that every path in
\mathcal{L}
has one endpoint in
I
and the other in
J.

Let us assume that
I
and
J
are chosen minimally with this property and let
\mathcal{L}=\{L_{1},L_{2},\dots,L_{\ell}\}
be ordered with respect to the occurrence of the endpoint
u_{i}
of
L_{i}
in
I.

Suppose there exists a transaction
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
such that there exists a path
P
in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
which crosses all other paths in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}

Suppose
|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}|>xy
then there must exist a transaction
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of order at least
y+1
and a vortex
c
of
\rho
such that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}
induces a transaction of order at least
y+1
in the vortex society of
c.

Since
c
is of depth at most
y
this is impossible and thus
|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}|\leq xy.

We partition
I
into
x+1
consecutive segments
I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{x+1}
each containing at least
2xy+p
endpoints of members of
\mathcal{L}.

For each
i\in[x+1]
there exists a family
\mathcal{L}_{i}
containing
p
consecutive paths such that
I_{i}
has at least
xy
other endpoints on either side.
Notice that, by the observation above, it is not possible that a path from
\mathcal{L}_{i}
crosses a path from
\mathcal{L}
which does not have an endpoint in
I_{i}.

In particular, this means that for any choice of
i\neq j\in[x+1]
there does not exist a vortex of
\rho
which contains an edge of a path in
\mathcal{L}_{i}
and an edge of a path of
\mathcal{L}_{j}.

Therefore, there must exist
i\in[x+1]
such that no path in
\mathcal{L}_{i}
has an edge or a vertex that belongs to a vortex of
\rho.

It follows that
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is isolated, separating, and rural as desired.
∎

Exposed transactions.

To ensure we are actually making progress with our constructions we require our transactions to run through the vortex region of our thicket.

Let
x,y,t,b,h,
and
s
be positive integers and
(G,\delta,W)
be an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard.
Moreover, let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be a thicket society of a
(t,s)
-thicket segment of
(G,\delta,W)
with nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{0},\ldots,C_{s+1}\}
and cylindrical rendition
\rho\coloneqq\delta\cap\Delta,
where
\Delta
is the
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C_{s+1}).

A transaction
\mathcal{P}
in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is said to be
exposed
if the
\mathsf{trace}{\rho}(C_{s+1})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho}(C_{0})
intersects every path in
\mathcal{P}.

Please note that our definition of exposed transactions differs slightly from the definition on
[
39
]
.
Indeed, we do not require the presence of a vortex at all for a transaction to be exposed, it suffices that the transaction crosses through the disk defined by the innermost cycle of the nest.

Pruning of an orchard.

In
[
39
]
it was ensured to always find an exposed transaction because Kawarabayashi et al. only considered crooked transactions which are forced to traverse the vortex in their setting.
For us, however, since our transactions are not necessarily crooked and we do not require our cylindrical renditions to be “around” a vortex, this is not the case.
We get around this by using a trick from
[
76
]
that allows us to either find, in every large enough transaction, either a big exposed transaction or a refinement of our orchard that reduced the part of
G
that belongs to our thicket society
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle.

Let
x,y,t,b,h,
and
s
be positive integers and
(G,\delta,W)
be an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard.
An
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W^{\prime})
is said to be a
pruning
of
(G,\delta,W)
if

•

all handle, crosscap, and parterre segments of
W
equal the handle, crosscap, and parterre segments of
W^{\prime},

•

if
W
has
\ell
thickets, then there exists
j\in[\ell]
such that
W^{\prime}
also has
\ell
thickets and all thickets of
W,
expect for the
j
th thicket, equal the thickets of
W^{\prime}
except for the
j
th thicket, and

•

if
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is a thicket society of the
j
-th thicket of
W
with nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{0},C_{1},\dots,C_{s+1}\}
and cylindrical rendition
\rho\coloneqq\delta\cap\Delta,
where
\Delta
is the
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C_{s+1}),
and
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
is a thicket society of the
j
-th thicket of
W^{\prime}
with nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C^{\prime}_{0},C^{\prime}_{1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{s+1}\}
and cylindrical rendition
\rho^{\prime}\coloneqq\delta\cap\Delta^{\prime},
where
\Delta^{\prime}
is the
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C^{\prime}_{s+1}),
then

–

either
\Delta^{\prime}
is properly contained in
\Delta
or

–

there exists
i\in[s]
such that

*

if
\Delta_{i}
is the
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho}(C_{s+1})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho}(C_{i}),

\langle H_{i},\Lambda_{i}\rangle
is a
\Delta_{i}
-society in
\rho,
and

*

\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
is the
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho^{\prime}}(C^{\prime}_{s+1})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho^{\prime}}(C^{\prime}_{i}),

\langle H^{\prime}_{i},\Lambda^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
is a
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
-society in
\rho^{\prime},

then either
H^{\prime}_{i}-V(C_{i}^{\prime})\subsetneq H_{i}-V(C_{i})
or
E(H^{\prime}_{i})\subsetneq E(H_{i}).

Hence, the pruning of an orchard is essentially achieved by “pushing” one of the cycles of the nest of one of the thickets either a bit closer to the untamed area in the center of the thicket or by extending the nest into the untamed area, thereby taming a part of the actual vortex inside the thicket.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
p,x,y,t,b,h,
and
s
be positive integers and
(G,\delta,W)
be an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard.
Let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be a thicket society of
(G,\delta,W)
and
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order at least
2s+p
in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle.

Then there exists

•

an exposed transaction
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p,
or

•

a pruning of
(G,\delta,W).

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathcal{O}(|G|).

Proof.

Let
\{C_{0},\ldots,C_{s+1}\}
be the nest paired with the
(t,s)
-thicket segment that corresponds to
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle,

\rho\coloneqq\delta\cap\Delta
be the cylindrical rendition of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle,
where
\Delta
is the
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C_{s+1}),
and
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}.

If
\mathcal{P}
contains an exposed transaction of order
p
we are immediately done.
Moreover, we can check in linear time for the existence of such a transaction by checking for each path in
\mathcal{P}
individually if it is exposed.

First, observe that we may assume that every path of
\mathcal{P}
is intersected by the
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho}(C_{s+1})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}{\rho}(C_{s}).

Indeed, if there is a path
P
which violates this condition then we can define a
\delta
-grounded cycle
C^{\prime}_{s+1}
by replacing a subpath of
C_{s+1}
whose endpoints are the same as the endpoints of a minimal
V(C_{s+1})
-
V(C_{s+1})
-subpath of
P
in a way that defines a cycle whose trace in
\delta
bounds a disk
\Delta^{\prime}
which contains
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C_{0}).

It is then easy to see that
\Delta^{\prime}
is properly contained in
\Delta
and by replacing our working thicket with a thicket whose thicket society corresponds to any
\Delta^{\prime}
-society in
\delta
yields a pruning of
(G,\delta,W).

Hence, we may assume that there exists a linkage
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
2s+1
such that
no
path of
\mathcal{Q}
is exposed and every path is intersected by the
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho}(C_{s+1})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho}(C_{s}).

Let
\Delta^{*}
denote the
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho}(C_{s+1})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\rho}(C_{0}).

It follows that
\mathcal{Q}
must be a planar transaction and no path in
\mathcal{Q}
intersects the
\Delta^{*}.

Notice that each member
Q
of
\mathcal{Q}
naturally separates
\Delta
into two disks, exactly one of which intersects
\Delta^{*}
(in fact this disk must contain
\Delta^{*}
).
Let us call the other disk the
small side of
Q
.
Given two members of
\mathcal{Q}
then either the small side of one is contained in the small side of the other, or their small sides are disjoint.
It is straightforward to see that if we say that two members are
equivalent
if their small sides intersect, this indeed defines an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{Q}.

Moreover, there are exactly two equivalence classes, one of which, call it
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},
must contain at least
s+1
members.
Since
|\mathcal{C}|=s
there must exist some
i\in[s]
and some subpath
L
of some path in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
such that

•

both endpoints of
L
belong to
V(C_{i}),

•

L
is internally disjoint from
\bigcup_{i\in[s]}V(C_{i}),

•

L
contains at least one edge that does not belong to
C_{i},
and

•

L
is drawn in the
\mathsf{trace}(C_{i})
-disk that is disjoint from the nodes corresponding to the vertices of
\Lambda.

Notice that
C_{i}\cup L
contains a unique cycle
C^{\prime}
different from
C_{i}
whose trace separates
\Delta^{*}
from the nodes corresponding to the vertices of
\Lambda.

Moreover, the
\Delta^{*}
-containing disk
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathsf{trace}(C^{\prime})
is properly contained in the
\Delta^{*}
-containing disk of
\mathsf{trace}(C_{i}).

In particular, there exists an edge of
C_{i}
which does not belong to
C_{i}^{\prime},
and there exists an edge that belongs to
C_{i}^{\prime}
but not to
C_{i}
and therefore, the graph drawn on
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
after deleting the vertices of
C_{i}^{\prime}
is properly contained in the graph drawn on
\Delta^{\prime}
after deleting the vertices of
C_{i}.

We would now like to define the new nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},C^{\prime},C_{i+1},\dots,C_{s}\}.

However, to fully obtain the desired pruning of
(G,\delta,W)
we need to use the radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
of the thicket in
W
to obtain a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of the same order which is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

First, we are going to change the cycle
C^{\prime}
once more.
Consider
C^{\prime\prime}
to be the cycle obtained from
C^{\prime}
after iteratively replacing a subpath of
C^{\prime},
in the same way as above, with any
V(C_{i})
-
V(C_{i})
subpath
R^{\prime}
of path in
\mathcal{R}
that satisfies the same four properties as
L
above.
It follows that there can be at most one path in
\mathcal{R},
say
R,
that is not orthogonal to
C^{\prime\prime}
after performing these replacements.
To conclude, now we are going to change the path
R.

Let us traverse along
C^{\prime\prime}
in the clockwise direction.
We may define
x_{R}
to be the first vertex of
L
on
R
and
y_{R}
to be the last such vertex.
Then there exists a unique
x_{R}
-
y_{R}
-subpath
R^{\prime\prime}
of
R
and a unique
x_{R}
-
y_{R}
subpath
L^{\prime}
of
C^{\prime}
such that replacing
R^{\prime\prime}
by
L^{\prime}
results in a new path that is now orthogonal to
C^{\prime\prime}.

This defines our desired path
R^{\prime}
and allows us to define the nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},C^{\prime\prime},C_{i+1},\dots,C_{s}\}
and the radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\cup\{R^{\prime}\}\setminus\{R\}
together with the new walloid
W^{\prime}
to obtain our desired pruning of
(G,\delta,W).

∎

Notice that whenever we find a pruning, some edge of
G
crosses over one of the cycles in some nest in some thicket of
(G,\delta,W).

Since we are in a graph excluding some minor, there are at most
\mathcal{O}(|G|)
many edges.
Moreover, the total number of thickets is upper bounded by some constant depending only on the parameters which also determine the size of the excluded minor.
Indeed, similarly, the size of each nest of each thicket can also be considered to be a constant with the same arguments.
This means that after finding at most
\mathcal{O}(|G|)
many big enough transactions on the thicket societies we have either found a big exposed transaction or may conclude that all thicket societies are of bounded depth.

Notice that, in the end, we want to guarantee that not only each thicket society is of bounded depth, but within every thicket is a vortex which is also of bounded depth, and this vortex is surrounded by a large nest.
The tools we develop in the upcoming subsections will allow us to achieve just that.

Orthogonality.

So far we know that we can always either find a pruning of our blooming orchard, or we find an exposed planar transaction whose strip society is isolated, separating, and rural.
An important ingredient that we will need to develop the tools in the upcoming subsections is that, in the latter case above, we should also be able to make an exposed planar transaction orthogonal, by possibly slightly altering the nest that comes with some thicket society.
For this purpose we develop the following tool that builds on ideas from
[
56
]
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
x,y,t,b,h,
and
s
be positive integers and
(G,\delta,W)
be an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard.
Let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be a thicket society of
(G,\delta,W)
with railed nest
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R})
and
\rho\coloneqq\delta\cap\Delta
be the cylindrical rendition of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
around
c_{\rho}
where
\Delta
is the disk bounded by the
\mathsf{trace}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of the outer cycle of the
(t,s)
-thicket segment of
(G,\delta,W)
that corresponds to
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a planar exposed transaction of order
s\cdot(p+2)
in
\langle H,\Omega\rangle
such that the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is isolated, separating, and rural.
Then there exists

•

an
s
-nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\delta\cap\Delta
around the same arcwise connected set as
\mathcal{C},

•

a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and has the same endpoints as
\mathcal{R}
on
B_{\Lambda},
and

•

a planar exposed transaction
\mathcal{Q}
of order
p
in
\langle H,\Omega\rangle
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
such that the
\mathcal{Q}
-strip society of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is isolated, separating, and rural.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds the outcome above in time
(\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(s\cdot p)+\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(t))\cdot|G|.

Proof.

It is straightforward to obtain a rendition
\rho^{*}
of
\langle H,\Omega\rangle
by combining a vortex-free rendition of the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
and
\delta\cap\Delta
such that
c_{\rho}
is divided into a set
\mathcal{U}
of vortex cells in
\rho^{*}.

Note that, since
\mathcal{P}
is exposed it follows that
|\mathcal{U}|\geq 2.

Moreover, we can define
\rho^{*}
as a
restriction
of
\delta\cap\Delta,
i.e., so that every cell of
\rho^{*}
that is not contained in
c_{\rho}
is a cell of
\delta\cap\Delta.

For what follows, we work with the rendition
\rho^{*}.

Notice that in
\rho^{*}
the drawing of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
never cross one another and this is crucial for the arguments that follow.

Let
q\coloneqq s\cdot(p+2).

Let
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
be an ordering of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
such that while traversing the rotation ordering of
\Lambda
we first encounter one of the two endpoints for all paths in
\mathcal{P}
and then all others.
Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{s}
be the paths in
\mathcal{P}
respecting the ordering
\Lambda_{\mathcal{P}}.

Moreover, for every cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}
we define the
interior
(resp.
exterior
) of
C
as the disk bounded by the trace of
C
that contains (resp. avoids)
B_{\Lambda}.

Now, for
i\in[s]
and
j\in[q],
let
P
be a
V(P_{j})
-
V(P_{j})
-path
B
that is a subpath of
C_{i}.

Let
P_{B}
be the subpath of
P_{i}
that shares its endpoints with
B.

Let
\Delta_{B}
be the disk bounded by the trace of the cycle
B\cup P_{B}
that does not fully either the trace of
C_{1}
or
B_{\Lambda}.

We call
B
a
bend
of
C_{i}
at
P_{j}
if
\Delta_{B}
does not intersect the drawing of
C_{i}\setminus B.

Moreover, we call a bend
B

shrinking
if
\Delta_{B}
is a subset of the interior of
C_{i}
and
expanding
otherwise.
We call an expanding bend
B

loose
if
\Delta_{B}
does not intersect the drawing of any cycle in
\mathcal{C}
that is not
C_{i}
and
\emph{tight}
if any expanding bend
B^{\prime}
of
C_{i}
where
B^{\prime}
is a subpath of
B
is not loose.

We also define the
height
of a bend
B
as the non-negative integer
r+1,
where
r
is the maximum value such that
B
intersects at least one of
P_{j+r}
or
P_{j-r}
and
j+r\leq s
or
j-r\geq 1
(if
j+r
exceeds
q
we consider
P_{j+r}=P_{q}
and if
j-r
drops below
1
we similarly assume that
P_{j-r}=P_{1}
).
Finally, we call any minimal
V(P_{1})
-
V(P_{q})
path contained in a cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}
a
pillar
of
C.

First, notice that an expanding bend
B
of a cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}
that is neither loose nor tight must contain a subpath
B^{\prime}
that is a loose expanding bend of
C.

Moreover, if
B
is any expanding bend of a cycle
C\in\mathcal{C},
then the graph obtained by replacing the subpath
B
of
C
with the path
P_{B}
defines a cycle
C^{\prime}
and the interior of
C^{\prime}
strictly contains the interior of
C,
hence the name expanding.
In the case of a shrinking bend the opposite happens, particularly we obtain a cycle with strictly smaller interior.

Additionally, notice that for every
C\in\mathcal{C},
since
\mathcal{P}
is an exposed transaction,
C
contains at least one pillar and since
C
is a cycle, it must contain an even number of pairwise disjoint pillars.

We proceed to prove the following statement by an induction on the number of cycles in
\mathcal{C}
: If no cycle in
\mathcal{C}
contains a loose expanding bend then for every
i\in[s],

1.

every expanding bend of
C_{i}
has height at most
s-i+1,

2.

C_{i}
contains exactly two disjoint pillars
R_{i},

i\in[2],
and

3.

every shrinking bend of
C_{i}
that is a subpath of
R_{i},

i\in[2],
has height at most
s-i+1.

Now, let
k\in[1,s-1]
and
\mathcal{C}^{k}
be the subset of
\mathcal{C}
that consists of its
k
outermost cycles.
Assume that the statement above holds for
\mathcal{C}^{k}.

We prove that it also holds for
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}.

Assume that no cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
contains a loose expanding bend.
Let
B
be any tight expanding bend of
C_{s-k}
at
P\in\mathcal{P}
which is the innermost cycle contained in
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
and the only cycle of
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
not contained in
\mathcal{C}^{k}.

Notice that any subpath
B^{\prime}
of
B
whose endpoints are the endpoints of a maximal subpath of a path in
\mathcal{P}
that is drawn in
\Delta_{B}
is also an expanding bend of
C_{s-k}
where
\Delta_{B^{\prime}}\subseteq\Delta_{B}.

Since
B
is tight, any such subpath
B^{\prime}
must also be tight.
This implies that the disk
\Delta_{B^{\prime}}
for any such subpath
B^{\prime}
intersects the drawing of some other cycle of
\mathcal{C}.

Since
B
is expanding and hence
\Delta_{B}
is not a subset of the interior of
C_{s-k},
it follows that
\Delta_{B^{\prime}}
intersects the drawing of cycles only in
\mathcal{C}^{k}.

In fact, it follows that any such intersection corresponds to an expanding bend of some cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{k}
at
P
that is contained in
\Delta_{P}.

Then, by assumption, if all these expanding bends are tight, it must be that the maximum height among all of them is
k.

Hence, it must be that the height of
B
is
k+1.

Moreover, it is straightforward to observe that
C_{s-k}
contains exactly two disjoint pillars.
Indeed, if there were at least four disjoint pillars in
C_{s-k}
then it is easy to see that there would exist an expanding bend of
C_{s-k}
on either
P_{1}
and/or
P_{q}
whose height would be larger than
k+1,
which would imply that it is a loose expanding bend, which by assumption cannot exist.

Finally, it is also straightforward to observe that the existence of a shrinking bend of
C_{s-k}
that is a subpath of either of the two pillars of
C_{s-k}
of height more than
k+1,
implies the existence of an expanding bend of the same height, which as we proved cannot exist.

We proceed with the description of an algorithm that runs in time
(\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(s\cdot p)+\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(t))\cdot|G|
that computes the desired
s
-nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
along with the desired railed linkage
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime},
and moreover computes the desired planar exposed transaction
\mathcal{Q}
of order
p
that is orthogonal to the new nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

Step 1:

We obtain our new set of cycles
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\langle C^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,C^{\prime}_{s}\rangle,
where
C^{\prime}_{i},

i\in[s],
is obtained from
C_{i}
by iteratively applying whenever applicable the following two rules.

1:

As long as there exists a path
R\in\mathcal{R},
some
i\in[s-1]
and a subpath
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
such that
R^{\prime}
has both endpoints in
C_{i}
and is otherwise disjoint from all cycles of
\mathcal{C}
and moreover
R^{\prime}
is drawn entirely in the exterior of
C_{i},
we update
C_{i}
to the unique cycle
C^{\prime}
contained in
C_{i}\cup R^{\prime}
different from
C_{i}
whose trace separates the interior of
C_{1}
from
B_{\Lambda}.

We call such a subpath
R^{\prime}
of
R
as above, a
C_{i}
-
expanding
path.
Now, observe that, since
\rho^{*}
is a restriction of
\delta\cap\Delta,

C^{\prime}
is also grounded in
\delta\cap\Delta.

Moreover, the interior of
C^{\prime}
properly contains the interior of
C_{i}
(before the update).

2:

As long as there exists a loose expanding bend
B
of a cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}
we replace
B
by
P_{B}
as defined above.
Now, observe that, since
\rho^{*}
is a restriction of
\delta\cap\Delta,
the updated cycle remains grounded in
\delta\cap\Delta.

Since for both rules above, whenever we update a cycle of our nest, it is guaranteed that the interior of the updated cycle strictly contains the interior of its predecessor, the above procedure will terminate in at most
\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(s\cdot p)\cdot|G|
many steps and the resulting nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
will satisfy the following two properties.
There is no loose expanding bend of any cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime},
which implies that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
satisfies the three properties above, and there is no
C
-expanding subpath of any path in
\mathcal{R}
for any cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

Step 2:

Now, update
\mathcal{R}
to contain the minimal
V(\Lambda)
-
V(C^{\prime}_{1})
subpath of each path in
\mathcal{R}.

It follows from the previous step that any subpath
R^{\prime}
of any path
R\in\mathcal{R}
such that
R^{\prime}
has both endpoints on
C^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in[2,s-1]
and is otherwise disjoint from
C^{\prime}_{i}
must either intersect
C^{\prime}_{i+1}
or it cannot be drawn in the exterior of
C^{\prime}_{i}.

In the case that
R^{\prime}
has both endpoints on
C^{\prime}_{s}
it can only be that
R^{\prime}
is not drawn in the exterior of
C^{\prime}_{s}.

From now on we call such a subpath
R^{\prime}
of a path
R\in\mathcal{R}
a
retrogression
of
R.

Let the paths in
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{t}\}
be ordered according to the occurrence of their endpoints on the rotation ordering of
\Lambda.

Moreover, assume there exists an
i\in[t]
and a
j\in[2,s]
such that
R_{i}
has a retrogression
R^{\prime}
with both endpoints on
C^{\prime}_{j}.

Notice that there exists a subpath
L_{R^{\prime}}
of
C^{\prime}_{j}
that shares its endpoints with
R^{\prime}
which is internally disjoint from
R_{i}.

This is because otherwise, we could find a subpath of
R_{i}
which witnesses that we are in the previous case.
Let
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
be the disk bounded by the trace of
R^{\prime}\cup L_{R^{\prime}}
which avoids the trace of
C_{1}.

We assume that
R^{\prime}
is chosen maximally with the property of being a retrogression.

Suppose there exists some path
Q,
possibly
Q=R,
in
\mathcal{R}
whose drawing intersects the interior of
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
in a node or arc distinct from those of
R^{\prime}.

Then, this intersection of
Q
with
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
must belong to a maximal retrogression
Q^{\prime}
of
Q
and there must exist
j^{\prime}\in[2,s],

j^{\prime}>j,
such that both endpoints belong to
C_{j^{\prime}}.

Hence, there must exist some maximal retrogression
P^{\prime}
of some path
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
such that no other part of
\mathcal{R}
intersects the corresponding disk
\Delta_{P}^{\prime}.

Thus, by replacing
P^{\prime}
with
L_{P^{\prime}},
we obtain a new radial linkage still with the same endpoints as those of
\mathcal{R},
but with strictly less retrogressions.
This means that, after at most
E(\mathcal{R})
many such refinement steps, we must have found a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
which is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

This step can be performed in time
\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(t)\cdot|G|.

Step 3:

We now define a transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p
by skipping the first and last
s
paths in
\mathcal{P}
and then choosing the first path from every bundle of
s
many consecutive paths from the remaining paths of
\mathcal{P}.

By the arguments above the height of every expanding bend of every cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
at
P_{1}
or
P_{s}
is at most
s
and therefore by definition no such bend can intersect any of the paths in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}.

It follows that any other bend of any cycle must be a bend that is a subpath of a pillar of the given cycle.
By definition of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime},
this implies that any such bend of any cycle can intersect at most one path in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}.

This implies that the transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
which is a planar and exposed transaction in
(H,\Omega)
is almost orthogonal to our new set of cycles
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

We need to make one more adjustment to each of the paths in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
to finally make them orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

We define the final transaction
\mathcal{Q}
where each path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
is a path obtained from a path in
P\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
that shares the same endpoints with
P
and is defined by starting from one of the two endpoints of
P
and while moving towards the other endpoint of
P
taking any possible “shortcut”, that is by greedily following along any bend of any pillar of a cycle in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
at
P
that appears, until we reach the other endpoint of
P.

Since as we already observed any bend of any pillar of a cycle can intersect at most one path in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime},
it is implied that
\mathcal{Q}
is a linkage.
Moreover, by construction, it now follows that
\mathcal{Q}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
as desired and that the paths in
\mathcal{Q}
are grounded in
\rho^{*}
which also implies that the
\mathcal{Q}
-strip society of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is isolated, separating, and rural.
Finally, since the shortcuts we take are subpaths of pillars it follows that
\mathcal{Q}
contains at least one edge drawn in the interior of
C^{\prime}_{1}
and therefore is exposed (with respect to
\delta\cap\Delta
).
∎

Rooting transactions.

Recall that every thicket comes with a railed nest
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R})
where
\mathcal{R}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{R}
acts as our connection of the thicket to the cylindrical wall of the walloid of our orchard.
While refining, and possibly splitting, the thicket we wish to maintain the existence of such a radial linkage and we need to make sure that new flowers that potentially need to be extracted from the thicket can be routed through the paths in
\mathcal{R}.

To this end we prove the following lemma.
For a visualization, see
Figure 14
.

Figure 14
:
A visualization of
Lemma 6.6
. The cycles in
\mathcal{C}
are red, the paths in
\mathcal{L}
are green, and the paths in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are orange. The vertices in
X\cup Y
are red and the vertices in
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
are blue.

Lemma 6.6
.

Assume that
G
is a graph embedded in an annulus
A
such that
G
is the union of

•

r
-cycles
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}\}
drawn in its interior such that no disk of
A
bounds some of the cycles in
\mathcal{C},

•

an
X
-
Y
-linkage
\mathcal{L}=\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{r}\},
and a
X^{\prime}
-
Y^{\prime}
-linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\{L_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,L_{r}^{\prime}\},

such that the vertices of
X\cup Y
are drawn in one of the two boundaries of
A,
the vertices of
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
are drawn in the other, and both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are orthogonal to
C.

Then
G
contains an orthogonal
X
-
Y^{\prime}
-linkage of order
r.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds the outcome above in time
\text{$\mathsf{poly}$}(r)\cdot|G|.

Proof.

Notice that for every set
S\subseteq V(G)
such that
|S|<r,
there exists at least one cycle, one path in
\mathcal{L},
and one path in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
fully contained in
G-S.

By applying Menger’s theorem, this implies that there exists an
X
-
Y^{\prime}
linkage
\mathcal{R}
in
G
of order
r.

Moreover, in the terminology of the proof of
Lemma 6.5
, since
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are orthogonal to
\mathcal{C},
we can immediately deduce that there are no
C
-expanding paths of any path in
\mathcal{R}
for any cycle
C\in\mathcal{C}.

Then, by applying the arguments of step two in the proof of
Lemma 6.5
, we can make
\mathcal{R}
orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

∎

Lemma 6.6
allows us to root any given orthogonal transaction on our orthogonal radial linkage.
To formalize this we borrow the following notion from
[
39
]
.

Let
\rho
be a rendition of a society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
together with a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{s}\}
around an arcwise connected set
\Delta^{*}\subseteq\Delta.

We say that a linkage
\mathcal{L}
is
coterminal
with a linkage
\mathcal{R}

up to level
C_{i}
if there exists a subset
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathcal{L}\cap H=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cap H
where
H
is the graph drawn in the
\Delta^{*}
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}(C_{i}).

6.2
Cultivating a transaction

With the tools developed in the previous section, we know that we can always either find a pruning of our blooming orchard, or we find an exposed planar transaction that is isolated, separating, and rural which because of
Lemma 6.5
we can also assume to be orthogonal to the nest of our thicket.
The next step is to apply the ideas of plowing to the transaction to make sure that the area covered by it is homogeneous with respect to its folio of detail
d
which would then allow us to extract additional flowers and parterre from it.

Let
d,x,y,t,b,h,
and
s
be positive integers and
(G,\delta,W)
be an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard.
Moreover, let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be a thicket society with nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\dots,C_{s}\}
of
(G,\delta,W).

We say that a transaction in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is
barren
if it is planar, exposed, and orthogonal to
\mathcal{C},
and the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is isolated, separating, and rural.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a barren transaction in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle.

A
parcel
of
\mathcal{P}
is a cycle
C
consisting of two vertex-disjoint subpaths of
C_{1},
say
X_{1}
and
X_{2},
and a shortest
V(C_{1})
-
V(C_{1})
subpath from two distinct paths
P_{1}\neq P_{2}\in\mathcal{P},
such that
C_{1}
contains an
X_{1}
-
X_{2}
-subpath of
P_{i}
for each
i\in[2],
and
C_{1}
intersects no other member of
\mathcal{P}.

Notice that all but the boundary paths of a barren transaction belong to exactly two parcels and the boundary paths belong to exactly one parcel each.
Let
d
be a positive integer.
The
d
-folio of a parcel
O
of
\mathcal{P}
is the union of the
d
-folios of all vortex-societies of all cells from the
\delta
-influence of
O.

A transaction
\mathcal{P}
is said to be
d
-cultivated
if it is barren and all of its parcels have the same
d
-folio.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
d,x,y,t,b,h,p,
and
s
be positive integers.
Let
(G,\delta,W)
be a
d
-blooming and
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard, let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be one of its thicket societies together with its nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{s}\}.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{lem_cultivate_transaction}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every barren transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
f_{\ref{lem_cultivate_transaction}}(p,d)
in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
there exists a
d
-cultivated transaction
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p.

Moreover,
f_{\ref{lem_cultivate_transaction}}(p,d)\in p^{2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}}}.

Proof.

By
Observation 2.2
we know that the folio of any parcel of any barren transaction in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
has at most
f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d)\in 2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}
members.
Moreover, recall that
f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(z_{1},z_{2})=z_{1}^{2^{z_{1}}}.

We set
f_{\ref{lem_cultivate_transaction}}(d,p)\coloneqq f_{\ref{lem_homogeneity_2d}}(p,f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d))
and thus meet the upper bound claimed in the assertion.

Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be the two outer paths of
\mathcal{P}
and for each
i\in[2]
let
P_{i}^{\prime}
be the shortest
V(C_{1})
-
V(C_{1})
-subpath of
P_{i}
with at least one edge not in
C_{1}.

Then there exist two vertex-disjoint subpaths
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
of
C_{1}
such that each
L_{i}
has one endpoint in common with
P_{1}^{\prime}
and the other endpoint in common with
P_{2}^{\prime},
and every path
P\in\mathcal{P}
contains a unique
V(L_{1})
-
V(L_{2})
-subpath
P^{\prime}.

It follows that
J\coloneqq L_{1}\cup L_{2}\cup\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}P}
is an
f_{\ref{lem_cultivate_transaction}}(d,p)
-ladder.
Moreover, the bricks of
J
are in a one-to-one correspondence with the parcels of
\mathcal{P}.

If we now enumerate all possible
f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d)
members of a
d
-folio of some parcel of
\mathcal{P},
assign distinct numbers to all of them and then assign to each brick of
J
the set of all numbers corresponding to the members of the folio of the corresponding parcel of
\mathcal{P}
we obtain an
f_{\ref{lem_cultivate_transaction}}(d,p)
-ladder where each brick is assigned a
f_{\ref{lem_cultivate_transaction}}(d,p)
-palette.
This allows us to call
Corollary 3.2
to obtain a
p
-ladder
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
where all bricks have the same
f_{\ref{lem_cultivate_transaction}}(d,p)
-palette.
Notice that each of the
p
horizontal paths of
J^{\prime}
is, in fact, a subpath
P^{\prime}
of some path in
\mathcal{P}.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the collection of all paths
Q\in\mathcal{P}
such that
Q^{\prime}
is a horizontal path of
J^{\prime}.

It follows that
\mathcal{Q}
is
d
-cultivated and of order
p
as desired.
∎

6.3
Greenhouses

We are now able to always find large cultivated transactions.
Next, we need an auxiliary tool that will help us refine a thicket and extract additional parterre.
Toward this goal we define an intermediate structure.

Let
p\geq t\geq 3
be integers.
A
(t,p)
-greenhouse segment
is a
(2t+1,p)
-wall
W
that is the union of a
(t,p)
-wall
W_{1},
called the
base
, whose
t
rows are exactly the first
t
rows of
W,
a
(t+1,p)
-wall
W_{2}
whose
t
rows are exactly the rows
t+1
up to
2t+1
of
W,
and a
(2,p)
wall
Q
whose two rows are exactly the rows
t
and
t+1
of
W.

We call the bricks of
Q
the
plots
of
W.

If
G
is a graph,
\delta
is a
\Sigma
-decomposition of
G,

d
is a positive integer, and
W
is a
(t,p)
-greenhouse segment, we say that
W
is
d
-homogeneous
if the
d
-folios of all plots of
W
are the same.

We will use the greenhouse segments as a storage unit for societies we have found deep within a segment and which will be turned into flowers of their respective parterre segments in a second step.
For this reason, greenhouses only appear as an intermediate structure within this subsection.

Figure 15
:
Two illustrations for the proof of
Lemma 6.8
: a greenhouse subdivided into a small sequence of pieces and one of those pieces transformed into a flower segment.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
b,d,h,p,t
be positive integers where
t\geq 3.

Let
G
be a graph
\delta
be a
\Sigma
-decomposition of
G,

W
be a
d
-homogeneous
(t+10,p)
-greenhouse segment in
G,
and let
X
be the perimeter of
W.

Assume further that
p\geq d^{*}\cdot h(2t+10bd)
where
d^{*}
is the size of the
d
-folio of the plots of
W.

Then there exists a sequence
\langle W_{1},\dots,W_{d^{*}}\rangle
of
(t,b,h)
-parterre segments such that

•

for every
i\in[d^{*}]

W_{i}
is fully contained in the graph drawn in the influence of
X,

•

for every
i\in[d^{*}]
the wall
\widetilde{W_{i}}
is a subwall of the base of
W
and its rows coincide with the rows of
W,

•

for every simple society
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
in the
d
-folio of the plots there exists some
i\in[d^{*}]
such that
W_{i}
is a
(t,b,h,H,\Lambda)
-parterre segment.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\{O_{1},O_{2},\dots,O_{d^{*}}\}
be the collection of all societies that belong to the
d
-folio of the plots of
W.

Let us begin by partitioning
W
into a sequence
\langle W^{\prime}_{1},\dots,W^{\prime}_{d^{*}}\rangle
of
(h(2t+10bd),t+10)
-walls.
Fix some
i\in[d^{*}]
and now partition
W^{\prime}_{i}
further into a sequence
\langle W^{i}_{1},\dots,W^{i}_{h}\rangle
of
(2t+10bd)
-walls.
It suffices to show that each
W^{i}_{j}
can be turned into a
(t,b,O_{i})
-flower segment since this means we can find, for each
i\in[d^{*}]
a sequence of
h
disjoint such flower segments, resulting in a
(t,b,h,O_{i})
-parterre segment as desired.

Notice that each
W^{i}_{j}
individually can be seen as a
(t,2t+10bd)
-greenhouse segment.
Since
W
is
d
-homogeneous, it follows that
W^{i}_{j}
is so as well since every plot of
W^{i}_{j}
is a plot of
W.

We may now find a linkage
\mathcal{P}
starting at the first vertex of each of the first
t
columns of
W^{i}_{j},
then following these columns into the
t+2
nd row, from there heading to the last
t
columns of
W^{i}_{j}
and following these down to the bottom row.
See
Figure 15
for an illustration.

This linkage intersects only
2t
plots of
W^{i}_{j}
and will serve as the rainbow of our flower segment.
To create the petals we group the remaining
bd
columns into
b
groups, each containing
10d
columns.
We may then use the same arguments as in the proof of
Lemma 5.3
and
Lemma 5.2
to pick first a plot of each group which is the center of a
(10,10)
-subwall of
W^{i}_{j}
that does not intersect any other group nor
\mathcal{P}.

Within the influence of this plot, we know we find a cell
c
whose vortex society contains
O_{i}
as a minor.
Now
Lemma 5.2
allows us to link the boundary vertices of
c
to the columns of the group we are currently handling.
Again, see
Figure 15
for an illustration.
Since all groups can be handled individually this indeed results in the desired flower segment.
∎

6.4
Swapping a thicket and a parterre

A last issue we have to deal with before we can jump into the proof of our main tool is the following.
Suppose the thicket society of, at least, the first thicket is already of bounded depth.
This means that there is nothing more to be extracted from that thicket and we have to start treating some thicket to the right of it.
However, whenever we extract a parterre segment by first pulling out a part of some
d
-homogeneous transaction in a thicket society of the
i
th thicket and then using
Lemma 6.8
, the parterre will be “in between” thicket segments with respect to the linear ordering of the segments.
As we demand all parterre segments of an orchard to occur in order without being interrupted by other segments, we need a way to move this parterre to the “left” of all thicket segments.
This will cost us a bit of the infrastructure of our thickets, but since we will only extract, and then move, a bounded number of parterre segments in the end, this becomes just a matter of large enough numbers.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
d,x,y,t_{0},t,b,h,s_{0},s,
and
\ell
be positive integers where

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\geq s_{0}+\ell\cdot h(2t_{0}+bd)\text{ and}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\geq t_{0}+2\cdot\ell\cdot h(2t_{0}+bd)

Now let,
(G,\delta,W)
be obtained from a
d
-blooming and
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s)
-orchard
(G,\delta^{*},W^{*})
by replacing the
i
th
(t,s)
-thicket segment with a sequence
\langle W_{1},\dots,W_{\ell},W_{\ell+1},W_{\ell+2}\rangle
where
W_{i},
for each
i\in[\ell]
is a
(t_{0},b,h)
-parterre segment and both
W_{\ell+1}
and
W_{\ell+2}
are
(t_{0},s_{0})
-thicket segments where we allow either
W_{\ell+2},
or both
W_{\ell+1}
and
W_{\ell+2}
to be empty.
Moreover, assume that every member of
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta^{\prime})
is either a society of some parterre segment of
\delta,
or a society of
W_{i}
for some
i\in[\ell].

Then there exists a
d
-blooming
(x,y)
-fertile
(t_{0},b,h,s_{0})
-orchard
(D,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
such that
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta^{\prime})=d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta^{*})
and the number of thicket segments of
(D,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
is exactly the number of thicket segments of
(G,\delta,W)
minus one plus the number of non-empty members of
\{W_{\ell+1},W_{\ell+2}\}.

The proof of
Lemma 6.9
is mostly technical but not very difficult.
We therefore choose to present a rather informal description of the operation instead of a full formalization.
A sketch of the core part of the re-routing necessary is depicted in
Figure 16
.

Proof.

Figure 16
:
A sketch on how to move a flower segment from the “right” of a thicket segment to its “left” in the proof of
Lemma 6.9
.

Let
C
denote the exceptional cycle of
W.

For each
i\in[\ell],

W_{i}
is a
(t_{0},b,h)
-parterre segment.
Hence,
W_{i}
consists of
h

(t_{0},b)
-flower segments in the sequence
\langle W^{i}_{1},\dots,W^{i}_{h}\rangle.

Moreover, for each
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[b],
each flower of
W^{i}_{j}
has at most
d,
indeed, at most
3,
vertices in common with
C
while the rainbow of
W^{i}_{j}
meets
2t_{0}
additional vertices of
C.

Let us call these vertices the
terminals
of
W^{i}_{j}.

To prove out statement it suffices to show that the parterre segments may be “moved” sequentially from one side of a single thicket to the other.
If this is true, then, by using the same arguments, we can move the parterre segment to “the other side” of any number of consecutive thicket segments since we only have to sacrifice the infrastructure locally.
Moreover, if this works for a single parterre segment, then by induction on
\ell
it follows that we may repeat the procedure for each of them.

Indeed, to move a single parterre segment, it suffices, by the same arguments, to show that we may “move” its flower segments individually, one after the other.
To this end, all we have to prove is that a single flower segment may be “moved”.

We allocate the following part of the infrastructure to make our routing:
Starting from
C
we select
2t_{0}+db
many consecutive cycles from the concentric cycles of
W.

From the
(i-1)
st thicket we allocate the first
2t_{0}+db
paths from its radial linkage together with the last
2t_{0}+db
paths of its radial linkage.
Moreover, we reserve the outermost
2t_{0}+db
cycles of its nest.

Let
C^{\prime}
denote the new exceptional cycle of
W^{\prime}
in case
W^{\prime}
is obtained from
W
by forgetting the first
2t_{0}+db
consecutive cycles starting from
C.

Observe that each part of the infrastructure allocated is large enough to host
2t_{0}+db
disjoint paths, which is at least the number of terminals from our flower segment.
By following along the vertical paths of
W
and the allocated infrastructure, we can now find a linkage from the terminals of our flower segment to the intersection of the first
2t_{0}+db
paths of the radial linkage of the
(i-1)
st thicket segment such that

1.

these paths stay within the allocated infrastructure except for their endpoints, and

2.

the endpoint of each path belongs to
C^{\prime}.

See
Figure 15
for an illustration of how those paths are to be chosen.

Notice that this operation reduces the infrastructure by at most
2t_{0}+db
many cycles in the thicket and
W
and at most
2(2t_{0}+db)
many paths from its radial linkage.
Moreover, the operation results, after forgetting the used infrastructure and adjusting the numbers accordingly, in the flower segment to now be on “the other side” of the
(i-1)
st thicket segment.

Since we perform this operation
h
times for each parterre segment and we have
\ell
parterre segments in total, the total amount of lost infrastructure is at most
\ell\cdot h(2t_{0}+bd)
cycles from the nest of each involved thicket segment, at most
2\cdot\ell\cdot h(2t_{0}+bd)
paths from the radial linkage of each involved thicket segment, and at most
\ell\cdot h(2t_{0}+bd)
cycles of
W.

By our choice of numbers this implies that the nests of each thicket segment, as well as the walloid
W
itself, each have at least
t_{0}
cycles left.
Moreover, each thicket segment has at least
t_{0}
paths in its radial linkage that remain untouched and at least
s_{0}
cycles in its nest that have not been used.
Thus, our claim follows.
∎

6.5
The extraction lemma

We are now ready to deal with our most technical tool that will facilitate most of the technicalities of the proof of
Lemma 6.2
.

Let
x,y,t,b,h,
and
s
be positive integers and
(G,\delta,W)
be an
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard.
Moreover, let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be a thicket society of a
(t,s)
-thicket segment of
(G,\delta,W)
with nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{0},\ldots,C_{s+1}\}.

Also, consider
\rho
to be the cylindrical rendition of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
obtained from
\delta\cap\Delta
by declaring the interior of the
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C_{0})
the unique vortex
c_{0}
of
\rho,
where
\Delta
is the
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C_{s+1}).

Moreover, let
\mathcal{P}
be a barren transaction in
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle.

Notice that there exists a rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
in
\Delta
which can be obtained by combining
\rho
with a vortex-free rendition of the strip society of
\mathcal{P}
such that
c_{0}
is divided into two vortices,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}.

We call these two vortices the
residual vortices
of
\rho
and
\mathcal{P}.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
d,x,y,t_{0},b,h,\ell,p,
and
s_{0}
be positive integers where

\displaystyle t\coloneqq

\displaystyle\ell\cdot h(2t_{0}+10bd)+2t_{0}+2,

\displaystyle s\coloneqq

\displaystyle s_{0}+4t+2t_{0}+3,\text{ and}

\displaystyle p\coloneqq

\displaystyle 3t+2s+2.

Let
(G,\delta,W)
be a
d
-blooming and
(x,y)
-fertile
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard,
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be one of its thicket societies together with its nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{0},C_{1},\dots,C_{s},C_{s+1}\},
and
\mathcal{P}
be a
d
-cultivated transaction of order
p
in
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle.

We assume that either
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
has a cross, or its folio contains at least one society that is not a society of
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta).

Let
\mathcal{O}
be the collection of societies of detail
d
that belong to the folio of the parcels of
\mathcal{P}
but not to
d
-
\mathsf{folio}(\delta),
and assume
|\mathcal{O}|\leq\ell.

Let
\delta^{*}=(\Gamma^{*},\mathcal{D}^{*})
be a
\Sigma
-decomposition of
G
obtained by combining
\delta
with a vortex-free rendition of the strip society of
\mathcal{Q}
and let
c_{1},

c_{2}
be the residual vortices of
\delta
and
\mathcal{Q}.

Then,

1.

if

•

\mathcal{O}\neq\emptyset,
or

•

\mathcal{O}=\emptyset
but for each
i\in[2]
there either exists a simple society
O_{i}\notin d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)
such that
O_{i}
belongs to the
d
-folio of a vortex society of
c_{i}
or there exists a cross on the vortex society of
c_{i},

there exists a
d
-blooming and
(x,y)
-fertile
(t_{0},b,h,s_{0},\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
where
\delta^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}),

\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{*},
and every cell of
\delta^{\prime}
that is not a cell of
\delta^{*}
is a vortex.
Moreover, every vortex of
\delta^{\prime}
is either a vortex of
\delta
or contained in one of
c_{1}
or
c_{2}.

2.

Otherwise, there exists a pruning of
(G,\delta,W).

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{2}).

Proof.

We break the proof into five steps as follows.
During the proof we keep one bit of information called the
flag
which will determine which of the two outcomes of the lemma we end up in.
In case the bit is
1,
i.e. the
flag is set
, we find the first outcome, otherwise, we will end up with a pruning of
(G,\delta,W).

In the beginning, our bit equals
0
and the flag is not set.

Step 1

This step is to establish the general setup. We divide
\mathcal{P}
into five transactions (see
Figure 17
) which will be used for different parts of the construction later on.

Step 2

We then check if
\mathcal{O}\neq\emptyset.

If this is true we set the flag and introduce an intermediate setup that will allow us to extract a large greenhouse.
Otherwise, the flag remains unset and we move on.

Step 3

The transaction
\mathcal{P}
defines two new “vortex regions”.
For each of them, we test if it either
a)
has a cross, or
b)
its folio contains a society that does neither belong to the folio of
\delta,
nor to the folio of the parcels of
\mathcal{P}.

If for each of the two regions one of
a)
or
b)
holds and the flag is unset, we set the flag.
Otherwise, we do not touch the flag and move on.

Step 4

Now, if the flag has been set in one of the previous steps we now extract a greenhouse, which will be converted in a sequence of parterre by
Lemma 6.8
, and two new thickets from the original thicket segment.
This yields the first outcome of the assertion and our case distinction terminates here.

Step 5

This step is only reached if the flag is not set at any point.
By our assumptions on
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
this implies that exactly one of the two vortex regions satisfies
a)
or
b)
from
Step 3
and every society of the folio of the parcels of
\mathcal{P}
belongs to
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta).

In this case, we make use of
\mathcal{P}
to find a pruning of
(G,\delta,W).

t

i\in I.

Moreover, if

and

such that

What remains is to describe the final step of the construction.
That is, we have to describe how to obtain the desired

-fertile
(t_{0},b,h,s_{0},\Sigma)
-orchard.
To do this, we extend the radial linkage

through the circles of our walloid

all the way down to its simple face.
The infrastructure described above yields, a large greenhouse and for each

Now,

allows us to transform the greenhouse into a sequence of

Together with the (up to two) new thicket segments we have now reached the situation of

.

and then forgetting about some of the excess infrastructure of the walloid

and its segments, we finally obtain a
(x,y)
-fertile
(t_{0},b,h,s_{0},\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\rho^{*},W^{*})
which has the desired properties by construction.

Step 5: Pruning

All that is left is to discuss the case where the flag was not set.

In the case where neither
c_{1}
not
c_{2}
is promising it follows that the vortex society of
c_{0}
does not contain a cross, nor does its
d
-folio contain a society that does not belong to
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta).

This, however, would mean that the entire thicket could have been forgotten to begin with and thus we may ignore this case.

The only remaining option is that exactly one of
c_{1}
or
c_{2}
is promising.
Without loss of generality we may assume that
c_{2}
is not promising.

For every path
T
in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
let
T^{\prime}
be a shortest
V(I_{2})
-
V(C_{1})
-subpath of
T.

We set

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\{T^{\prime}\mid T\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\}.

Then
\mathcal{T}
is a radial linkage of order
t
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

We then apply
Lemma 6.6
to make
\mathcal{T}
coterminal with
\mathcal{R}
up to level
t+1.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be the resulting orthogonal radial linkage.

Next we define our new nest.
This construction is similar to the construction from
Step 4
.
We begin by fixing a point
x_{1}
in the interior of
c_{1}\cap c_{0}
which avoids the disk induced by the strip society of
\mathcal{P}.

Now, for each
i\in[0,s+1],
let
C_{i}^{*}
be the cycle in
C_{i}\cup P_{t+2+i}
whose trace in
\delta
separates
x_{1}
from the trace of
C_{s+1}
in
\delta.

Moreover, let
\mathcal{C}^{*}\coloneqq\{C_{i}^{*}\mid i\in[s]\}.

Now, notice that the radial linkage
\mathcal{L}
must also be orthogonal to the new nest
\mathcal{C}^{*}.

Let
c_{0}^{*}
be a cell obtained from the disk bounded by the trace of
C_{0}^{*}
which contains the point
x_{1}.

It follows that
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
has a rendition
\rho^{*}
in the disk where
c_{0}^{*}
is its only vortex cell, and
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\rho^{*})\subseteq d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta).

With this we may augment
\delta
to a
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta^{\prime}
of
G
by using
\rho^{*}
and adjust
W
by replacing the thicket with nest
\mathcal{C}
and radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
with a thicket segment with nest
\mathcal{C}^{*}
and radial linkage
\mathcal{L},
thereby obtaining a new walloid
W^{\prime}.

It is now straightforward to verify that
(G,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
is indeed a pruning of
(G,\delta,W)
since no path in
\mathcal{T}_{2}
(except its first one), which is an exposed transaction on
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle,
belongs to any of the thickets of
(G,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime}).

With this, our proof is complete.

6.6
The proof of
Lemma 6.2

We introduce the following convention for the rest of this section.
Let us define the
d
-folio number
to be

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}^{\star}\coloneqq f_{\ref{obs_size_dfolio}}(d).

This is purely for convenience in the proof of
Lemma 6.2
as we need to have a lot of discussion around the maximum number of different members of
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta).

By
Observation 2.2
this number is upper bounded by
\mathsf{d}^{\star}.

Before we begin with the proof of
Lemma 6.2
, we also fix three recursive functions
\mathsf{s},

\mathsf{t},
and
\mathsf{p}.

These functions represent the recursive application of
Lemma 6.10
and ensure that the infrastructure of the final walloid will have order
t^{*},
while its thickets are surrounded by nests of order
s^{*}
for any choice of
t^{*}
and
s^{*}.

Let
s^{*},

t^{*},
and
z
be positive integers and
h,b,d\in\mathbb{N}.

\displaystyle\mathsf{t}(t^{*},0)

\displaystyle\coloneqq t^{*}

\displaystyle\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot h\cdot\big(2\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-1)+10bd\big)+2\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-1)+2

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},0)

\displaystyle\coloneqq s^{*}+6t^{*}+12

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq s^{*}+1+\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z-1)+4\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-1)+3

Please notice that the three functions above also depend on
h,b
and, in particular on
d
as even
\mathsf{d}^{*}
depends on
d.

However, we consider these numbers to be fixed and wish to highlight mostly the dependency on
s^{*}
and
t^{*}
as these are the numbers that will mostly be modulated in later parts of the proof of our main theorem.
From the recursive definitions of the functions above, one can derive the following observations on their order:

\displaystyle\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)

\displaystyle\in(\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot h)^{\mathcal{O}(z)}+\mathcal{O}(zbd)+t^{*}\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z)

\displaystyle\in(\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot h)^{\mathcal{O}(z)}+\mathcal{O}\big(z(s^{*}+bd)\big)+t^{*}.

Moreover, whenever we start with an orchard that is
(x,y)
-fertile, the following upper bound
z^{*}
on the maximum value
z
may attain will follow from our proof.

\displaystyle z^{*}\coloneqq x+\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot(\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot hb+1)2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}

We also fix
19
19

19

Recall that
\mathsf{d}^{\star}
is not a precise value but hides some constant within its
\mathcal{O}
-notation.
a value
p^{*}
which will act as an upper bound on the depth of the vortices of
(G,\delta,W)
as follows.

\displaystyle p^{*}\coloneqq x^{2}y+(t^{*}\cdot s^{*}\cdot(h+1)^{x+hb})^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}

Based on these functions let us briefly provide explicit bounds for the four functions from the statement of
Lemma 6.2
.

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},x,y,d)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z^{*})\in t^{*}+h^{x+hb\cdot 2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}}},

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},x,y,d)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z^{*})\in t^{*}+s^{*}\cdot(x+hb)2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}}+h^{x+hb\cdot 2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}}},

\displaystyle f^{3}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},x,y,d)

\displaystyle\in x+hb2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}}\text{, and}

\displaystyle f^{4}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},x,y,d)

\displaystyle\in x^{2}y+(t^{*}\cdot s^{*}\cdot(h+1)^{x+hb})^{2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(d^{2})}}}

Please note that we do not derive these bounds explicitly in the proof below.
The bounds we provide will all be in terms of
z^{*},

\mathsf{t},
and
\mathsf{s},
however, the bounds above then follow from the respective bounds on these functions.

With this, we are ready for the proof of
Lemma 6.2
.

Proof of
Lemma 6.2
.

We prove the following slightly stronger claim by induction on
z.

Inductive claim.

There exists an algorithm that, given an
(x,y)
-fertile and
d
-blooming single-thicket
(\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z),b,h,\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z),\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W),
outputs a
(z^{*},p^{*})
-ripe and
d
-blooming
(t^{*},b,h,s^{*},\Sigma)
-orchard in time
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{3}).

Here
z
represents an upper bound on the number of times
Lemma 6.10
can yield the first outcome before, in the resulting orchard
(G,\delta,W),

d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)
contains all possible societies of detail
d
and
\delta
has
x
vortices, each with a cross on its society.
The two main points here are that, for an
(x,y)
-fertile orchard, repeated applications of
Lemma 6.10
can never produce strictly more than
x
thickets such that the thicket society of each of them has a cross.
Moreover, any thicket whose thicket society does not have a cross must contain some detail-
d
-society in its folio that does not belong to
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)
since otherwise we could simply discard the thicket as it does not pose an obstacle to embeddability.

Under these principles, there are three situations under which an application of
Lemma 6.10
does not yield a pruning (which is a situation we will handle online).

1.

the outcome increases the number of thickets with a cross on their respective thicket societies,

2.

the outcome produces a parterre segment for some detail-
d
-society that did not belong to
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)
before, or

3.

the outcome increases the number of “flat” thickets whose societies contain some detail-
d
-society as a minor which does not belong to
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta).

As discussed above, the first case can only occur
x
times.
Moreover, the second case can occur at most
\mathsf{d}^{\star}
times since this is an upper bound on the total number of non-isomorphic societies of detail
d.

For the third case, we will see that whenever the total number of “flat” thickets goes above a certain threshold we are able to apply a pigeonhole argument to turn some of them into parterre segments and thereby increase
|d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)|.

For this reason, also the third case can only occur a bounded number of times.

Base of the induction.

For the base of the induction, we consider the case
z=0.

This case reflects the situation where
(G,\delta,W)
has
x
thickets which have a cross on their thicket society and where
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)
contains all societies of detail
d.

In this situation, by the discussion above, there does not exist a thicket that does not have a cross on its society.
Let
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
be the thicket society of some thicket of
(G,\delta,W).

We know that
(G,\delta,W)
is an
(x,y)
-fertile and
d
-blooming
(t^{*},b,h,s^{*}+6t^{*}+12,\Sigma)
-orchard.
What remains to show is that it can be turned into a
(z^{*},p^{*})
-ripe and
d
-blooming
(t^{*},b,h,s^{*}+6t^{*}+12,\Sigma)
-orchard.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\dots,C_{s^{*}+6t^{*}+12}\}
be the nest of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
and let
\Delta
be the
C_{s^{*}+6t^{*}+11}
avoiding disk defined by the trace of
C_{6t^{*}+12}.

Moreover, let
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
be a society defined by
\Delta.

Notice that
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
has a nested cylindrical rendition
\rho
in
\Delta
with a single vortex, say
c_{0},
and nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{1},\dots,C_{6t^{*}+11}\}.

Moreover, by cropping the radial linkage of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
we obtain a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of order
t^{*}
for
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle.

If
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
is of depth at most

\displaystyle 12t^{*}+24+(x+1)\big(2xy+(6t^{*}+12)((15t^{*}+26)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)-1

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle 2s^{\prime}+(x+1)\big(2xy+s^{\prime}((3t^{*}+2s^{\prime}+2)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)-1

where
s^{\prime}\coloneqq 6t^{*}+12=|{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}|,
we are done.
Hence, we may assume that there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{0}
of order
12t^{*}+24+(x+1)\big(2xy+(6t^{*}+12)((15t^{*}+26)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)
in
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle.

By
Lemma 6.4
, there is either a
pruning
of
(G,\delta,W),
or there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}
of order
(x+1)\big(2xy+(6t^{*}+12)((15t^{*}+26)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)
which is exposed.

By
Lemma 6.3
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
must contain a planar transaction
\mathcal{P}_{2}
of order
(6t^{*}+12)((15t^{*}+26)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)
whose strip society is isolated, separating, and rural.

Then, by
Lemma 6.5
, we can assume that (up to possibly slightly changing the cycles in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and the paths in the radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
) that there exists a barren transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{2}
of order
(15t^{*}+26)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}.

Next we apply
Lemma 6.7
to obtain a trasaction
\mathcal{P}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{2}
which is
d
-cultivated and of order
15t^{*}+26=3t^{*}+2s^{\prime}+2
where
s^{\prime}=|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}|.

This finally allows us to use
Lemma 6.10
on
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
and
\mathcal{P}_{3}.

Notice that, since
(G,\delta,W)
has exactly
x
thickets which are not flat and
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)
contains all linear societies of detail at most
d,
the first outcome of
Lemma 6.10
is impossible.
Thus, we must find a
pruning
of
(G,\delta,W)
in time
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{2}).

Notice that every time we find a pruning as the outcome of one of the above steps, at least one of the cycles of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is “pushed” slightly closer to the vortex.
That means, at least one edge of
G
is “pushed” further to the outside, or fully outside, of a thicket in each of these occurrences.
Since there are at most
|E(G)|
many edges and only
6t^{*}+12
many cycles in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime},
we cannot find a pruning more than
(6t^{*}+12)\cdot|E(G)|
times.
As a result, and since we may assume that
G
excludes a graph of bounded size as a minor which means
|E(G)|\in\mathcal{O}(|G|),
after
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{3})
many iterations of the procedure above the only possible case left is that
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
is of depth at most

\displaystyle 12t^{*}+24+(x+1)\big(2xy+(6t^{*}+12)((15t^{*}+26)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)-1.

Since there are only
x
thickets, these arguments establish the base of the induction.

The inductive step.

The arguments that bounded the number of times we may encounter a pruning in the base of our induction still apply when we find a pruning in the inductive step.
In essence, we cannot encounter a pruning more than
\mathcal{O}(|G|)
in total and thus, by iterating the arguments enough times, we must always encounter an outcome that is not a pruning.
To avoid repetition, we continue to properly mark all places in the arguments were pruning might occur, but we will always assume that in these cases we encounter one of the non-pruning outcomes.
Formally, we can establish such a situation by assuming that
(G,\delta,W)
is a minimal counterexample to out inductive claim with respect to improvements via pruning.
Please note that, according to
Lemma 6.4
, it is imperative that we perform all possible improvements via pruning, if we are to also guarantee in the end that not only the vortex societies within thickets have bounded depth but also their entire thicket societies.

From here on we may assume that
z\geq 1.

Step 1: No useless thickets.

First, we may assume that for every thicket segment
\widehat{W}
whose society does not have a cross, there exists some society of detail at most
d
which is a minor of the thicket society of
\widehat{W}
but does not belong to
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(d).

We may assume this since any thicket that does not meet this criterion can safely be forgotten.

Step 2: A bounded number of thickets.

Next, let us assume that there exist more than
(\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot hb+1)2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}
many thickets whose thicket society does not contain a cross.
Since there are at most
\mathsf{d}^{\star}
many societies of detail at most
d
by
Observation 2.2
we may assign to each thicket the
d
-folio of its thicket society.
Since there are at most
2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}
many possible such
d
-folios, there must be at least
\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot hb+1
many thickets with the same
d
-folio, say
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}.

Let
\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\setminus d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta).

Notice that
|\mathcal{F}|\geq 1
by the assumption from
Step 1
.
Moreover,
z\geq|\mathcal{F}|
must hold since we at least allow one “split” for each possible society of detail at most
d.

Now, for each member
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
of
\mathcal{F}
we wish to produce
h
many
(\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-|\mathcal{F}|),b,H,\Lambda)
-flower segments which then can be gathered into a single
(\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-|\mathcal{F}|),b,h,H,\Lambda)
-parterre segment.
To achieve this, the amount of infrastructure of our orchard we must sacrifice is at most

\displaystyle r\coloneqq|\mathcal{F}|\cdot h\cdot(2\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-|\mathcal{F}|)+bd)

cycles from the nest and at most
2r
paths from the radial linkage of every thicket.
By doing so, similar arguments as those for
Lemma 6.9
allow us to produce a new orchard whose walloid is of order
\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-|\mathcal{F}|)
and whose
d
-folio equals
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)\cup\mathcal{F}.

Since these arguments are essentially the same, we omit the discussion on how to move and grow the extracted flower segments through the thickets.
Notice that the following two inequalities follow directly from the recursive definitions of
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{t}
for every pair
z\geq z^{\prime}
of positive integers.

\displaystyle\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-z^{\prime})+2(z^{\prime}h(bd+\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-z^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)

(14)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z-z^{\prime})+z^{\prime}h(bd+\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-z^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z)

(15)

Therefore, if there are at least
x+(\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot hb+1)2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}+1
many thicket segments, under the assumption from
Step 1
, we may first extract
|\mathcal{F}|\geq 1
many new parterre segments and then use the induction hypothesis to complete the proof.
Thus, from now on we may assume that there are at most
x+(\mathsf{d}^{\star}\cdot hb+1)2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}
many thicket segments in
(G,\delta,W)
as desired.

Step 3: Splitting a thicket

What follows is, essentially, a second iteration of the steps from the base of our induction.
The only two changes that occur are:

1.

The numbers are now bigger and depend directly on the functions
\mathsf{t}
and
\mathsf{s}
and

2.

we may now also encounter the first outcome of
Lemma 6.10
which will lead to a proper “split” of the thicket segment.

We begin by selecting
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
to be the thicket society of some thicket of
(G,\delta,W)
as before.

Let
s^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z-1)+4\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-1)+3,
let
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\dots,C_{s^{*}+1+s^{\prime}}\}
be the nest of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
and let
\Delta
be the
C_{s^{*}+1+s^{\prime}}
avoiding disk defined by the trace of
C_{s^{\prime}+1}.

Moreover, let
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
be a society defined by
\Delta.

Notice that
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
has a nested cylindrical rendition
\rho
in
\Delta
with a single vortex, say
c_{0},
and nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{C_{1},\dots,C_{s^{\prime}}\}.

Moreover, by cropping the radial linkage of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
we obtain a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of order
\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)
for
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle.

If
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
is of depth at most

\displaystyle 2s^{\prime}+(x+1)\big(2xy+s^{\prime}((3\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2s^{\prime}+2)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)-1

We may, by transforming
\Delta
into a cell of
\delta
and declare it the vortex of our current thicket segment, obtain a thicket segment with an
s^{*}
-nested cylindrical rendition and a vortex of depth at most
2s^{\prime}+(x+1)\big(2xy+s^{\prime}((3\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2s^{\prime}+2)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)-1
for this segment.
In case this is the outcome for all thicket segments we are done.

Hence, we may assume that there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{0}
of order
2s^{\prime}+(x+1)\big(2xy+s^{\prime}((3\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2s^{\prime}+2)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)
in
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle.

By
Lemma 6.4
, there is either a
pruning
of
(G,\delta,W),
or there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}
of order
(x+1)\big(2xy+s^{\prime}((3\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2s^{\prime}+2)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)\big)
which is exposed.

By
Lemma 6.3
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
must contain a planar transaction
\mathcal{P}_{2}
of order
s^{\prime}((3\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2s^{\prime}+2)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}+2)
whose strip society is isolated, separating, and rural.

Then, by
Lemma 6.5
, we can assume that (up to possibly slightly changing the cycles in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and the paths in the radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
) that there exists a barren transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{2}
of order
(3\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2s^{\prime}+2)^{2^{\mathsf{d}^{\star}}}.

Next we apply
Lemma 6.7
to obtain a transaction
\mathcal{P}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}
which is
d
-cultivated and of order
3\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z)+2s^{\prime}+2.

Recall that
s^{\prime}=|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}|.

We are now ready to call upon
Lemma 6.10
for
\langle H^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
and
\mathcal{P}_{3}.

The second outcome of
Lemma 6.10
is a
pruning
of
(G,\delta,W).

Hence, we may assume that
Lemma 6.10
returns the first outcome.
Let us discuss the numbers involved to see that we may now complete the proof by using the induction hypothesis.

For the purpose of applying
Lemma 6.10
let us set

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-1)\text{, and}

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{s}(t^{*},z-1).

It then follows from the recursive definition of
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{t}
that the size of the nest, the radial linkage of
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle,
and the transaction
\mathcal{P}_{3}
are large enough such that
Lemma 6.10
outputs an
(x,y)
-fertile
(\mathsf{t}(t^{*},z-1),b,h,\mathsf{s}(s^{*},t^{*},z-1),\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
where
d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)\subseteq d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta^{\prime})
and one of the following holds:

•

d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta)\subsetneq d\text{-}\mathsf{folio}(\delta^{\prime}),
or

•

W^{\prime}
has exactly one thicket segment more than
W.

In both cases, the total number of times we are allowed to “split” the vortex has been reduced by at least one.
Hence, by applying the induction hypothesis for
z-1
to
(G,\delta^{\prime},W^{\prime})
the proof is complete.
∎

7
Harvesting
