The goal of this section is to prove the two lemmata of
Subsection 7.5
, namely
Lemma 7.9
and
Lemma 7.10
.
The former shows how we can control the behavior of an extension, invading a well-insulated area of a
\Sigma
-decomposition, i.e., an area containing many concentric cycles around a vortex cell of the decomposition.
The latter shows how we can exploit the structure of such a controlled extension in order to show that whenever an extension that invades a well-insulated area cannot be hit with a bounded number of vertices within this area, then we can find a large packing of the invading part of the extension along the boundary of this area.

7.1
Lines in cylindrical renditions

In this subsection, we examine more closely the structure of minimal separators between disjoint segments of the society corresponding to a cylindrical rendition.
In particular, we show that their structure in the flat part of the cylindrical rendition defines a clear frontier in terms of what we call a
line
.

We need the following proposition on the structure of a low-depth society.

Proposition 7.1

(
[
63
]
)
.

Let
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
be a linear society where
\Lambda=\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\rangle.

If
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
has depth at most
w,
then there is a sequence
\mathcal{B}=\langle B_{i}\mid i\in[l]\rangle
of subsets of
G
such that

1.

{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{B}=V(G),

2.

for every edge
xy\in E(G)
there is some
i\in[l]
such that
\{x,y\}\subseteq B_{i},

3.

for every
i\in[l],

y_{i}\in B_{i},

4.

for every
i,j,h\in[l],
if
i\leq j\leq h,
then
X_{i}\cap X_{h}\subseteq X_{j},
and

5.

for every
i,j\in[l],
if
i\neq j,
then
|B_{i}\cap B_{i+1}|\leq w.

The proof of
Proposition 7.1
builds
\mathcal{B}
by successively looking for separators along prefixes of the linear society
\Lambda
(see also
[
39
, Theorem 12.2]
). This requires a linear number of calls of a min-flow algorithm for flow, each of size bounded by the depth
w.
So we can compute
\mathcal{B}
in time
\mathcal{O}(w\cdot|G|^{2}).

Fibers of a rendition.

Let
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c.

A
fiber
of
\rho
is any fiber
\mu=(M,T,\widehat{\Lambda})
of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle.

Whenever we work with a fiber of a rendition
\rho,
we assume that they are drawn in the closed disk
\Delta
as indicated by the
\Delta
-decomposition
\rho\cap M

of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle,
where
\mathsf{dissolve}(\mu)=\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle.

Combined depth of a cylindrical rendition.

Let
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c.

We define the
combined depth
of
\rho
as the maximum between the depth of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
and the depth of a vortex society of
c
in
\rho
and we denote it by
\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho).

This allows us to assume that
\rho
is accompanied with a sequence
\mathcal{B}_{c}=\langle B_{i}\mid i\in[l]\rangle
of subsets of
G_{c}
for which
Proposition 7.1
holds for every vortex society of
c
in
\rho
where
w\leq\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho).

Lines.

A
\Delta
-line
is a line
L
of
\Delta
in
\rho
connecting (and containing) two distinct points of
B_{\Delta},
with the additional property that
L
does not meet a flap cell of
\rho
or a boundary point of a vortex cell that is not the point of a vertex, i.e.,

{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{c\mid c\in C_{\mathsf{f}}(\rho)\}\cap L=\emptyset\ \ \text{and}\ \bigcup_{c\in C_{\mathsf{v}}(\rho)}({\sf bd}(c)-\widetilde{c})\cap L=\emptyset.

The vertex set of a
\Delta
-line
L
is
\pi_{\rho}(L\cap N(\delta))
and we denote it by
V_{\rho}(L)
or simply
V(L)
when
\rho
is clear from the context.
Given two distinct vertices
y,y^{\prime}\in V(\Lambda)
we use
L_{y,y^{\prime}}
to denote the
\Delta
-line of
\Delta
such that
V_{\rho}(L_{y,y^{\prime}})\subseteq B_{\Delta},
starting at the point of
y,
finishing at the point of
y^{\prime},
and following the rotation ordering of
\Delta.

We also use
\Lambda_{y,y^{\prime}}
for the consecutive sub-sequence of
\Lambda
that starts with
y
and finishes with
y^{\prime}.

The following lemma gives us more information about the structure of a minimum size separator between disjoint segments of a linear society corresponding to a cylindrical rendition in a disk
\Delta
in the form of a
\Delta
-line of minimum length.
The fact that we see this separator as a line is important for the arguments of the rest of the proofs of this section.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c
and let
x
and
y
be two vertices in
\Lambda
where
x
appears before
y
in
\Lambda.

Then there is a
\Delta
-line
L
of
\Delta
between
x
and
y
such that

1.

|V_{\rho}(L)|\leq\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho)+2,

2.

L\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta_{c})
contains at most two points, and

3.

there is no
\Delta
-line
L^{\prime}
of
\Delta
between
x
and
y
such that
|V_{\rho}(L^{\prime})|<|V_{\rho}(L)|.

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
G
after removing every vertex and edge drawn in every cell of
\rho
and then making the vertices in
\widetilde{c}^{\prime}
pairwise-adjacent, for every cell
c^{\prime}
of
\rho
that is not
c.

Also we define
\Gamma^{\prime}
to be the drawing of
G^{\prime}
in
\Delta
obtained from
\Gamma
by drawing the newly added edges in a way so that they do not cross, inside their corresponding cell of
\rho.

Clearly
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a
\Delta
-embedding (and also a plane embedding) of
G^{\prime}.

Moreover,
\rho^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathcal{D})
is a cylindrical rendition of the linear society
\langle G^{\prime},\Lambda\rangle
in
\Delta
around
c
such that
\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho).

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a maximum order transaction between
\Lambda_{xy}
and
\overline{\Lambda}_{xy}
in
\rho^{\prime}.

By definition
|\mathcal{T}|\leq\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho).

Let
S\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
be a minimum
\Lambda_{xy}
-
\overline{\Lambda}_{xy}
separator in
G^{\prime}.

By Menger’s theorem,
|S|\leq\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho).

Since
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a plane embedding of
G^{\prime}
and
S
is a minimum
\Lambda_{xy}
-
\overline{\Lambda}_{xy}
separator, it is implied that there exists a simple closed curve
N
in the plane, that intersects
G^{\prime}
only in vertices and that intersects exactly the vertices in
S.

By definition,
N
must intersect the boundary of
\Delta
in at least two points.
Also, since
\Delta_{c}
is a subset of a face of
\Gamma,
we can assume that
N
intersects the boundary of
\Delta_{c}
in at most two points.
Let
L
be the shortest line between two points of
{\sf bd}(\Delta)
that is a subset of
N
and that is drawn in
\Delta.

Since
N
separates
\Lambda_{xy}
from its complement it must be that
L
intersects
{\sf bd}(\Delta)
in points drawn between the point of
x
and the point of its previous vertex on
\Lambda
and the point of
y
and the point of its next vertex on
\Lambda.

Hence, we can extend
L
so that its endpoints are the points corresponding to
x
and
y
respectively without intersecting any other vertices or edges of
G^{\prime},
by possibly increasing the length of
L
by at most two vertices.

Now, observe that by definition
L
is a
\Delta
-line in
\rho^{\prime}
that satisfies properties
1
,
2
, and
3
in
\rho^{\prime}.

We argue that
L
also satisfies properties
1
,
2
, and
3
in
\rho.

This is indeed the case, since by definition of
\rho^{\prime}
any line of
\Delta
in
\rho
must also be a line of
\Delta
in
\rho^{\prime}
with the same vertex set and vice versa.
∎

7.2
Pseudo-disks in cylindrical renditions

In this subsection, we introduce the concept of a pseudo-disk of a cylindrical rendition to facilitate the procedure of locating a fiber of a linear society, within the area defined by a segment of the linear society corresponding to the rendition and the line that corresponds to the separator of this segment, as demonstrated in the previous subsection.

Pseudo-disks.

Let
\rho
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta.

A
pseudo-disk
of
\rho
is a set
\Psi\subseteq\Delta
such that

1.

\Psi
is the union of the
\Delta
-line
L_{y,y^{\prime}},
a
\Delta
-line
L^{\text{top}}
between
y
and
y^{\prime}
for some
y,y^{\prime}\in V_{\rho}(\Lambda)
where
y
appears before
y^{\prime}
in
\Lambda,
and the arc-wise connected components of
\Delta-(L_{y,y^{\prime}}\cup L^{\text{top}})
whose points in
\Delta
can reach
L_{yy^{\prime}}
without intersecting
L^{\text{top}},

2.

\Lambda_{\Psi}\coloneqq\Lambda_{y,y^{\prime}}-V_{\rho}({L}^{\text{top}})
is non-empty,

3.

L^{\rm top}\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta_{c})
contains at most two points, and

4.

there is no
\Delta
-line
L
between
y
and
y^{\prime}
such that
|V_{\rho}(L)|<|V_{\rho}(L^{\text{top}})|.

We define the
boundary
of
\Psi
as
{\sf bd}(\Psi)\coloneqq L_{yy^{\prime}}\cup L^{\text{top}}.

We call the
\Delta
-line
L^{\text{top}}
the
top-line
of
\Psi
and we call
y
the
starting
and
y^{\prime}
the
finishing
vertex of
\Psi.

We also define
\mathsf{last}(\Psi)\coloneqq y^{\prime}
.
Let
G_{\Psi}
be the subgraph of
G
whose vertices and edges are drawn in
\Psi
minus
L^{\text{top}}.

We call
\langle G_{\Psi},\Lambda_{\Psi}\rangle
the
society
of
\Psi,
we call the set
V(\Lambda_{\Psi})
the
base boundary
of
\Psi,
we call the set
V_{\rho}(L^{\text{top}})
the
line boundary
of
\Psi.

Assume that
L^{\text{top}}\cap{\sf bd}(c)
consists of two distinct points
v
and
v^{\prime}
where
y,v,v^{\prime},y^{\prime}
appear in this order in
L^{\text{top}}
and let
\Lambda^{c}=\Lambda_{\Delta_{c},v}.

Then the
vortex boundary
of
\Psi
is the set

(B_{v}\cap B_{\mathsf{next}_{\Lambda^{c}}(v)})\cup(B_{v^{\prime}}\cap B_{\mathsf{previous}_{\Lambda^{c}}(v^{\prime})})

where
B_{\mathsf{next}_{\Lambda^{c}}(v)}
and
B_{\mathsf{previous}_{\Lambda^{c}}(v^{\prime})}
are members of
\mathcal{B}^{c}
and that has size at most
2\cdot\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho).

If
|L^{\text{top}}\cap{\sf bd}(c)|\leq 1,
then the vortex boundary of
\Psi
is empty.
The
top boundary
of
\Psi
is the union of its line boundary and its vortex boundary and it is denoted by
V_{\rho}(\Psi).

An
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
inside
\Psi
is an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
that is also a fiber of
\langle G_{\Psi},\Lambda_{\Psi}\rangle.

An
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
outside
\Psi
is an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber
(M,T,\widehat{\Lambda})
of
\rho
where none of the points of
V(M)
are in
\Psi.

Chains.

Let
\rho
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle.

A sequence
\mathbf{\Psi}=\langle\Psi_{1},\ldots\Psi_{r}\rangle
of pseudo-disks of
\rho
is called a
chain
of
\rho
if

•

\Psi_{1},\ldots,\Psi_{r}
are pairwise internally-disjoint,

•

the starting vertex of
\Psi_{1}
is the starting vertex of
\Lambda,
and

•

for every
i\in[r-1],
if
y^{\prime}_{i}
is the finishing vertex of
\Psi_{i},

y^{\prime}_{i}
is the starting vertex of
\Psi_{i+1}.

The
starting
vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}
is the starting vertex of
\Psi_{1},
i.e., the starting vertex of
\Lambda.

The
finishing
vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}
is the finishing vertex of
\Psi_{r}
if
\mathbf{\Psi}\neq\langle\rangle,
and the last vertex of
\Lambda
otherwise.
An
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
outside
\mathbf{\Psi}
is an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
that is outside
\Psi_{i},
for every
i\in[r],
if
\mathbf{\Psi}\neq\langle\rangle,
and an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
where
V(M)
does not contain the starting and finishing vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}
otherwise.

The following lemma permits us to associate pairs of boundary vertices of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
to pseudo-disks of some suitably chosen cylindrical rendition of it.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c,

\mathbf{\Psi}
be a chain of
\rho
with starting vertex
x
and finishing vertex
y,
and
z
be a vertex of
\Lambda
that is not in the consecutive sub-sequence of
\Lambda
with first vertex
\mathsf{next}_{\Lambda}(x)
and last vertex
y.

Then
\rho
has a pseudo-disk
{\Psi},
starting from the finishing vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}
and finishing at
z,
that is internally-disjoint from every
\Psi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{\Psi},
and where
|V_{\rho}(\Psi)|\leq 3\cdot\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho)+2.

Proof.

First, we apply
Lemma 7.2
to obtain a
\Delta
-line
L
between
y
and
z
such that
|V_{\rho}(L)|\leq\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho)+2,

L\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta_{c})
contains at most two points, and such that there is no
\Delta
-line
L^{\prime}
between
y
and
z
such that
|V_{\rho}(L^{\prime})|<|V_{\rho}(L)|.

Next, we argue that
L
does not contain any internal point of any of the pseudo-disks of
\mathbf{\Psi}.

Assume towards contradiction that it is not the case.
Let
\Psi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{\Psi}
such that
L
contains an internal point of
\Psi.

Let
L^{\textrm{top}}
be the top-line of
\Psi^{\prime}.

This particularly implies that
L
and
L^{\text{top}}
intersect in at least in two points.
Let
L^{\prime}
be a subline of
L
whose endpoints are points of
L^{\text{top}}.

Let
L^{\text{top}}_{2}
be the corresponding subline of
L^{\text{top}}.

Consider the
\Delta
-line
L^{\prime\prime}
obtained by replacing the subline
L^{\text{top}}_{2}
of
L^{\text{top}}
by
L^{\prime}.

Now, observe that by the minimality of
L
guaranteed by the specifications of
Lemma 7.2
, it must be that
|L^{\prime\prime}|
is strictly smaller the line boundary of
\Psi^{\prime}
which contradicts the definition of
\Psi^{\prime}.

Then, we define the desired pseudo-disk
\Psi
as the union of
L\cup L_{yz}
and the arc-wise connected components of
\Delta-(L\cup L_{yz})
whose points in
\Delta
can reach
L_{yz}
without intersecting
L.

∎

7.3
Critical pseudo-disks and fibers

In this subsection we define the notion of a critical pseudo-disk which will be used to find fibers of a given linear society within pseudo-disks, in a way that will allow us to either find many disjoint, in a sequential fashion along the boundary of the linear society corresponding to our cylindrical rendition or hit all of them by removing a small number of vertices which will be the frontiers of the corresponding critical pseudo-disks in which we locate our fibers.

Critical pseudo-disks.

Let
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c
and let
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
be some linear society.
A pseudo-disk
\Psi
of
\rho
is an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disk
if there is a pseudo-disk
\widetilde{\Psi}
of
\rho
where

1.

the starting vertex of
\widetilde{\Psi}
is the same as
\Psi
and its finishing vertex is the previous of the finishing vertex of
\Psi
in
\Lambda,

2.

\widetilde{\Psi}\subseteq\Psi,

3.

there is an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
inside
\Psi,

4.

there is no
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
inside
\widetilde{\Psi},
and

5.

|V_{\rho}(\Psi)|,|V_{\rho}(\widetilde{\Psi})|\leq 3\cdot\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho)+2.

Figure 19
:
The two pseudo-disks of the definition of an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-critical pseudodisk.

Whenever we consider some
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disk
\Psi
of
\rho,
we accompany it with a pseudo-disk
\widetilde{\Psi},
as above. See
Figure 19
for a visualization of the notion of a
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disk.
The
frontier
of
\Psi
is the set
\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\Psi)\coloneqq V_{\rho}(\Psi)\cup V_{\rho}(\widetilde{\Psi}).

The
debris
of
\Psi,
denoted by
\mathsf{debris}_{\rho}(\Psi)
is the set of vertices of
G-\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\Psi)
that are not
\rho
-bound.
Notice that
\partial_{G}(\mathsf{debris}_{\rho}(\Psi))\subseteq\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\Psi).

Therefore,
|\partial_{G}(\mathsf{debris}_{\rho}(\Psi))|\leq 6\cdot\mathsf{depth}(\rho)+{3}.

We define

\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi})\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\in[|\mathbf{\Psi}|]}\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{i})

and

\mathsf{debris}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi})\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\in[|\mathbf{\Psi}|]}\mathsf{debris}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{i}).

It is easy to observe that, as all pseudo-disks in
\mathbf{\Psi}
are pairwise internally disjoint, it holds that

\displaystyle|\partial_{G}(\mathsf{debris}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi}))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(6\cdot\mathsf{depth}(\rho)+{3})\cdot|\mathbf{\Psi}|

(16)

Lemma 7.4
.

There is an algorithm that, given

•

a cylindrical rendition
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c,
where
w\coloneqq\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho),

•

a linear society
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle,

•

a chain
\mathbf{\Psi}
of
\rho,
and

•

a pseudo-disk
\Psi
of
\rho
starting from the finishing vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}
and finishing at the starting vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}
that is internally-disjoint from every
\Psi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{\Psi}
where
|V_{\rho}(\Psi)|\leq 3w,

if there is an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
outside
\mathbf{\Psi}
that is also a fiber of
\langle G_{\Psi},\Lambda_{\Psi}\rangle,
outputs an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disk
\Psi^{\prime}
of
\rho
such that
\mathbf{\Psi}\oplus\langle\Psi^{\prime}\rangle
is a chain of
\rho,

in time

\mathcal{O}_{|H|}(w\cdot|G|^{3}).

Proof.

By the assumptions of the lemma there exists an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
outside
\mathbf{\Psi}
that is a fiber of
\langle G_{\Psi},\Lambda_{\Psi}\rangle.

Let
x
be the finishing and
y
be the starting vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}
respectively.
Also, let
\Lambda_{xy}
be the maximal sub-sequence of
\Lambda
with first vertex
x
and last vertex
y
such that the point of any other vertex of
\Lambda_{xy}
is not in
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{\Psi}.

Notice that since
\Psi
is internally disjoint from every
\Psi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{\Psi},
it must be that every vertex of the top-line of any
\Psi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{\Psi},
that is also a vertex of
\Lambda_{\Psi},
must also be a vertex of the top-line of
\Psi.

This implies that
\Lambda_{xy}=\Lambda_{\Psi}.

Now, for every vertex
z
of
\Lambda_{xy}
distinct from
y,
let
\Psi_{xz}
be the pseudo-disk of
\rho
given by
Lemma 7.3
.
Moreover, let
\Psi_{xy}\coloneqq\Psi.

Now, consider a vertex
z
of
\Lambda_{xy}
such that the consecutive sub-sequence
\Lambda_{xz}
of
\Lambda_{xy}
with first vertex
x
and last vertex
z
is minimal in length so that there is an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
outside
\mathbf{\Psi}
that is a fiber of
\langle G_{\Psi_{xz}},\Lambda_{\Psi_{xz}}\rangle.

Clearly, the existence of
\Psi
certifies that such a vertex
z
exists.
Now let
z^{\prime}
be the previous vertex from
z
on
\Lambda_{xy}.

Now observe that since
z
is minimal with this property it means that there is no
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
outside
\mathbf{\Psi}
that is a fiber of
\langle G_{\Psi_{xz^{\prime}}},\Lambda_{\Psi_{xz^{\prime}}}\rangle.

Then the pseudo-disk
\Psi_{xz}
is an
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disk certified by the pseudo-disk
\Psi_{xz^{\prime}}.

Also by the specifications of
Lemma 7.3
(or by the assumptions of this lemma in case
z=y
) we have that
\mathbf{\Psi}\oplus\langle\Psi^{\prime}\rangle
is a chain of
\rho.

In order to compute
\Psi,
we start by computing a decomposition as the one in
Proposition 7.1
, which as we already briefly discussed, can be done in time
\mathcal{O}(w\cdot|G|^{2}).

This gives us all the top boundaries that correspond to the pseudo-disks we define by calling upon
Lemma 7.3
.
To finally compute the critical pseudo-disk
\Psi
we iteratively apply at most
\mathcal{O}(|G|)
times the minor-checking algorithm of
[
37
]
that runs in time
\mathcal{O}_{|H|}(|G|^{2}).

∎

Critical pseudo-disks will allow us to find “minimal packings” of fibers along the boundary of linear societies.
The “criticality” in their definition is important in order to show an Erdős-Pósa duality for fibers within bounded-depth societies.
This is the subject of the following subsection.

7.4
Harvesting fibers

In this subsection, we show how to combine all the partial results in the previous subsection in order to either find many sequential fibers of a given linear society or hit all of them with a small number of vertices found within the disk of the given cylindrical rendition.

k
-fold multiplications and shifts.

Let
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
be a linear society and consider that
\mathsf{dec}({H,\widehat{\Lambda}})=\langle\langle H_{1},\widehat{\Lambda}_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H_{q},\widehat{\Lambda}_{q}\rangle\rangle.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},
we define the
k
-
fold multiplication
of
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle,
denoted by
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle^{(k)}
to be the linear society where

\mathsf{dec}(\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle^{(k)})=\langle\langle H_{1}^{1},\widehat{\Lambda}_{1}^{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H_{1}^{k},\widehat{\Lambda}_{1}^{k}\rangle,\ \ldots\ ,\langle H^{1}_{q},\widehat{\Lambda}^{1}_{q}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H^{k}_{q},\widehat{\Lambda}^{k}_{q}\rangle\rangle

and where, for
(i,j)\in[k]\times[q],

\langle H_{j}^{i},\widehat{\Lambda}_{j}^{i}\rangle
is isomorphic to
\langle H_{j},\widehat{\Lambda}_{j}\rangle.

Corridors and
\Delta
-compact sets.

Let
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c.

Let
\Psi
be a pseudo-disk of
\rho
with starting vertex
y
and finishing vertex
y^{\prime}
whose top line is
L^{\text{top}}
and assume that
\Psi
is a closed disk of
\Delta.

Then we define
\widehat{\Psi}
as the set containing the interior of the set
\Psi\setminus c
and all internal points of
L_{y,y^{\prime}}.

We call any subset of
\Delta
that is a set
\widehat{\Psi}
as above a
corridor
of
\rho
and its
frontier
is the set of all vertices of
G
whose points belong in
{\sf bd}(\Psi)
but not in the interior of
L_{y,y^{\prime}}\cup\Delta_{c}.

The union
K
of a finite set of pairwise internally disjoint corridors of
\Delta
is called a
corridor system
of
\rho
and the union of the frontiers of the corridors of this set is the
frontier
of
K.

We say that a vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
is
\Delta
-
compact
if there is a corridor system
K
of
\rho
such that

•

none of the points of
S
belongs in
K
and

•

the frontier of
K
is a subset of
S.

Moreover, we assume any
\Delta
-compact set
S
to be
accompanied
by such a corridor system of
\rho.

Observation 7.5
.

Let
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c.

If
S
and
S^{\prime}
are two
\Delta
-compact sets of
G
then
S\cup S^{\prime}
is also a
\Delta
-compact set of
G.

The notion of a corridor and its frontier is important in our proofs as when the outcome of our Erdős-Pósa duality is a “cover”
S,
then we require that this cover does not contain vertices drawn on the interior of the corridor as well as that all vertices of the boundary of the corridor belong in
S.

That way, the covers that we consider give us “valuable space” for rerouting the fibers that are not intersected by
S
in a way that we can bound the number of vertices on the boundary.

The algorithm that extracts the Erdős-Pósa duality is heavily based on the notion of a critical pseudo-disk in bounded depth societies and is the following.

Algorithm

\mathbf{find\_all}(\Delta,G,\Lambda,\rho,k,\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle)

Input
:

A cylindrical rendition
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c_{0})
of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta,

an integer
k\in\mathbb{N},

a linear society
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
where
\mathsf{dec}({H,\widehat{\Lambda}})\coloneqq\langle\langle H_{1},\widehat{\Lambda}_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H_{q},\widehat{\Lambda}_{q}\rangle\rangle.

Output
:

either a
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber of
\rho
or

a set
S\subseteq V(G)
such that
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle-S
has no
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber

\mathbf{\Psi}=\langle\rangle

use
Lemma 7.3
to find a pseudo-disk
\Psi
of
\rho
starting from the finishing vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}

and finishing at the starting vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}.

for

i\in[q]

\ell=0,

while

\ell\leq k
and
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle\setminus\mathbf{\Psi}
has an
\langle H_{i},\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}\rangle
-fiber that is also a fiber of
\langle G_{\Psi},\Lambda_{\Psi}\rangle

use

Lemma 7.4
to find an
\langle H_{i},\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disk
\Psi^{\prime}
of
\rho
such that

\mathbf{\Psi}\oplus\langle\Psi^{\prime}\rangle
is a chain of
\rho^{\prime}.

\mathbf{\Psi}\coloneqq\mathbf{\Psi}\oplus\langle\Psi^{\prime}\rangle

\ell\coloneqq\ell+1

Set
\Psi
to the pseudo-disk given
Lemma 7.3
of
\rho
starting from the finishing vertex of

\mathbf{\Psi}
and finishing at the starting vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}.

if

\ell<k

then output
\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi})\cup V_{\rho}(\Psi)
and stop

otherwise use the last
k

\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disks of
\mathbf{\Psi}
in order to find a

\langle H_{i},\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber of
\rho.

concatenate every
\langle H_{i}^{1},\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}^{1}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber of
\rho,
for
i\in[q],
in order to build a
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber of
\rho.

Notice that whenever the output of the previous algorithm is a “cover” set
S,
it is important that
S
is
\Delta
-compact.
This is indeed the case since the separators extracted are the boundaries of the pseudo-disks found in the algorithm.

Lemma 7.6

(Harvesting lemma)
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{func_sharvsp}}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given

•

a cylindrical rendition
\rho=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D},c)
of a linear society
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle
in a disk
\Delta
around a cell
c,
where
w\coloneqq\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho),
or

•

a linear society
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle,
and

•

a
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

either outputs

•

an
\langle H,{\Lambda}^{\prime}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber of
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle,
for a shift
\Lambda^{\prime}
of
\widehat{\Lambda},
or

•

a
\Delta
-compact set
S\subseteq V(G),
where
|S|\leq f_{\ref{func_sharvsp}}(k,w,|H|)
and
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle-S
has no
\langle H,\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle
-fiber, for any shift
\Lambda^{\prime}
of
\widehat{\Lambda},

in time

\mathcal{O}_{|H|}(kw\cdot|G|^{3}).

Moreover

f_{\ref{func_sharvsp}}(k,w,|H|)=\mathcal{O}_{|H|}(kw).

Proof.

We define
f_{\ref{func_sharvsp}}(k,w,|H|)\coloneqq k\cdot(3w+2)\cdot|H|^{2}.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{q}
be the shifts of
\widehat{\Lambda},
where
q=|\widehat{\Lambda}|\leq|H|.

For every
i\in[q],
we execute the algorithm
\mathbf{find\_all}(\Delta,G,\Lambda,\rho,k,\langle H,\Lambda_{i}\rangle).

If for some
i\in[q]
the output is an
\langle H,\Lambda_{i}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber of
\rho
we can conclude.
Hence, we can assume that for every
i\in[q],
the output of the corresponding execution is a set
S_{i}=\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{i})\cup V_{\rho}(\Psi_{i}),
where
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i}
is a chain of
\rho
and
\Psi_{i}
is a pseudo-disk of
\rho
starting from the finishing vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i},
finishing at the starting vertex of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i},
and that is internally-disjoint from every pseudo-disk of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i}.

First, observe that by the definition of
\Delta
-compact sets, for every
i\in[q],
the set
S_{i}
is
\Delta
-compact.
We define
S\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\in[q]}S_{i}.

Then,
Observation 7.5
implies that
S
is
\Delta
-compact, as required.

Also, note that for fixed
i\in[q],

\mathbf{\Psi_{i}}
can contain at most
k\cdot|H|
critical pseudo-disks of
\rho,
since
|\mathsf{dec}(H,\Lambda_{i})|\leq|H|,
and for each such critical pseudo-disk by definition we have that its frontier has size at most
3w+2.
Hence in total
|S_{i}|\leq k\cdot(3w+2)\cdot|H|
and thus
|S|\leq k\cdot(3w+2)\cdot|H|^{2}
as required.

It remains to show that
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle-S
has no
\langle H,\Lambda_{i}\rangle
-fiber, for any
i\in[q].

Assume towards contradiction that
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle-S
has an
\langle H,\Lambda_{i}\rangle
-fiber, for some
i\in[q],
and that
\mathsf{dec}(H,\Lambda_{i})\coloneqq\langle\langle H_{1},\Lambda^{i}_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H_{q},\Lambda^{i}_{d_{i}}\rangle\rangle.

Then, it must be that
\langle G,\Lambda\rangle-S
has a
\langle H,\Lambda^{i}_{j}\rangle
-fiber, for every
j\in[d_{i}],
such that all vertices of
\Lambda^{i}_{j}
appear before the vertices of
\Lambda^{i}_{j^{\prime}}
in
\Lambda,
where
j<j^{\prime}\in[d_{i}]
and moreover such that these fibers are pairwise-disjoint.

First, observe that since the algorithm outputted the set
\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{i})\cup V_{\rho}(\Psi_{i})
it is implied that there is an
l\in[d_{i}]
such that if there is a
\langle H,\Lambda^{i}_{l}\rangle
-fiber outside
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i},
this fiber is not a fiber of
\langle G_{\Psi_{i}},\Lambda_{\Psi_{i}}\rangle,
implying that any such fiber is intersected by
V_{\rho}(\Psi_{i}).

So, our only hope to find a fiber for each
\langle H,\Lambda^{i}_{j}\rangle,

j\in[l],
in the correct order, is within
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i}.

Now, observe that by the way the chain
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i}
of
\rho
is defined, for any sequence
\Psi_{1},\ldots,\Psi_{l}
of pseudo-disks of
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i},
respecting the order in
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i},
such that for every
j\in[l],
there is an
\langle H,\Lambda^{i}_{j}\rangle
-fiber of
\rho
inside
\Psi_{j},
there must be an index
j^{\prime}\in[l]
such that
\Psi_{j^{\prime}}
is a
\langle H,\Lambda^{i}_{j^{\prime}}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disk of
\rho,
which implies that the set
\mathsf{frontier}_{\rho}(\mathbf{\Psi}_{i})
intersects this fiber.
This contradicts the existence of the sequence of fibers we require in their correct order within
\mathbf{\Psi}_{i},
and this concludes the proof.

The algorithm above assumes that we have at hand all separators from a decomposition as the one in
Proposition 7.1
.
As we already briefly explained, this can be done in time
\mathcal{O}(w\cdot|G|^{2})
and it gives us frontiers of the corresponding pseudo-disks we compute by calling upon
Lemma 7.4
.
The total running time is dominated by the time required to find a
\langle H_{i},\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}\rangle
-critical pseudo-disk
\Psi^{\prime}
of
\rho
in the while-loop of algorithm
find_all
.
This is done, using
Lemma 7.4
, in time
\mathcal{O}_{|H|}(w\cdot|G|^{3})
time and is applied
\mathcal{O}(k)
many times.
∎

7.5
Main harvesting lemmata

In this subsection, we conclude with the proofs of the main lemmata of the entire section.
An important notion for our proofs is the one of an extension of some graph
Z
in a graph
G.

Extensions.

Given a graph
M
and a set
T\subseteq V(M)
we call
(M,T)
a
subdivision pair
if every vertex not in
T
has degree two in
M.

Given a subdivision pair
(M,T)
we define
\mathsf{dissolve}(M,T)
to be the graph obtained from
M
after dissolving all vertices in
V(M)\setminus T.

We define the
detail
of a subdivision pair
(M,T)
to be the number of edges of
\mathsf{dissolve}(M,T).

Consider two graphs
Z
and
G.

We say that a subdivision pair
(M,T)
is an
extension of
Z
in
G
if

1.

M
is a connected subgraph of
G,

2.

every vertex of
V(M)\setminus T
has degree two, and

3.

\mathsf{dissolve}(M,T)
contains
Z
as a minor.

It is important to take into account that the graph
M
of an extension is always connected.
Also the vertices of
V(M)\setminus T
can be seen as subdivision vertices of paths joining the “terminals” in
T.

By considering a minimal subgraph
M
satisfying the above properties, we can obtain the following observation.

Observation 7.7
.

Let
Z
and
G
be two graphs.
If
Z
is a minor of
G,
then there exists an extension of
Z
in
G
that has detail
\mathcal{O}(|Z|^{2}).

We define the function
\mathsf{h}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
so that for every graph
Z
that is a minor of a graph
G,

\mathsf{h}(|Z|)
is the bound on the detail of the minimal extension given by
Observation 7.7
.

An extension
(M,T)
of a graph
Z
in a graph
G
is
\delta
-bound
if
M
is
\delta
-bound. For the purposes of our local structure theorem we are interested in “covering” the
\delta
-bound extensions of some obstruction
Z\in{\sf obs}(\mathcal{H}).

Disk collection of a
\Sigma
-decomposition.

Let
\delta
be a
\Sigma
-decomposition of a graph
G.

A
disk collection of
\delta
is a set
\mathbf{\Delta}
of
|\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{v}}(\delta)|
pairwise-disjoint
\delta
-aligned disks of
\Sigma
where every disk
\Delta
of
\mathbf{\Delta}
contains a vortex
c^{\Delta}
of
\delta.

We assume that a disk collection
\mathbf{\Delta}
is accompanied with a
starting vertex assignment function

y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}\colon\mathbf{\Delta}\to V(G)
mapping
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}
to a vertex
y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}(\Delta).

For brevity, we use the term
svaf
instead of the longer one “starting vertex assignment function”.
We also define
\Lambda^{(\Delta)}\coloneqq\Lambda_{\Delta,y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}(\Delta)},
i.e.,
y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}(\Delta)
“fixes” the starting vertex of a linear ordering of the vertices on the boundary of
\Delta.

Hence, for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},

\rho^{\Delta}\coloneqq\delta\cap\Delta
is a cylindrical rendition of the society
\langle G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}\rangle.
around a vortex cell
c^{\Delta}.

Moreover, we say that a disk
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}
is
q
-insulated
if
\rho^{\Delta}
is a
q
-
nested
cylindrical rendition around the vortex
c^{\Delta}.

We call the triple
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
a
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple
of
G
and moreover
q
-insulated
if every disk
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}
is
q
-insulated.

We say that
(M,T)
is
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting
if at least one of the points of
V(M)
is drawn in the closure of
\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{\Delta}.

Covers from harvesting.

Let
k,d\in\mathbb{N},
and let
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
be a
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple of a graph
G.

Consider a disk
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}
and for every linear society
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
of detail at most
d,
apply
Lemma 7.6
to
\rho^{\Delta},

\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle,
and
k.

Let
S^{\Delta}
be the union of all sets that correspond to the second outcome of all previous applications of
Lemma 7.6
.
We define a
(d,k)
-cover of
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
as the set
\bigcup_{\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}}S^{\Delta}.

Notice that, using
Lemma 7.6
, we get the following observation on the time it takes to compute a
(d,k)
-cover of
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}).

Observation 7.8
.

There is an algorithm that, given
k,d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and a
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
of a graph
G,
outputs a
(d,k)
-cover
S
of
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
in time
\mathcal{O}_{d}(w\cdot k\cdot|\mathbf{\Delta}|\cdot|G|^{3}),
where
w\coloneqq\max\{\mathsf{cdepth}(\rho^{\Delta})\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}\}.

Moreover
|S|\leq\mathcal{O}_{d}(w\cdot k\cdot|\mathbf{\Delta}|).

Crops.

Let
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
be a disk-collection triple of a graph
G,

(M,T)
be a subdivision pair where
M
is a subgraph of
G,
and consider a disk
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}.

Let
M^{\prime}
be the union of all paths in
M\cap\Delta
connecting a vertex of
B_{\Delta}
with a vertex whose point is in
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}
and whose internal vertices are not in
B_{\Delta}.

We say that
(M,T)

has a crop in
\Delta
if
M^{\prime}
is a non-empty graph and, if this is the case, we define
\mathsf{crop}(M,T,\Delta)
as the fiber
(M^{\prime},T\cap V(M^{\prime}),\widehat{\Lambda})
of
\rho^{\Delta}
where
\widehat{\Lambda}\subseteq\Lambda^{(\Delta)}.

For an example of the crop operation, see
Figure 20
.

Figure 20
:
A disk
\Delta
containing some vortex
c
in is interior and a fiber
(M,T,\Lambda)
of
\langle G\cap\Delta,\Lambda\rangle.
We depict by red the vertices not in
T.

If
M^{\prime}
is the subgraph of
M
obtained after removing the black edges and the resulting set
S
of isolated vertices, then
(M^{\prime},T-S,\Lambda-S)={\sf crop}(M,T,\widehat{\Lambda}).
The subgraphs formed by the black edges are excluded because of the blue-dotted
\Delta
-lines.

Intuitively the operation of cropping filters out parts of the subdivision pair that invade the disks of our disk-collection, that do not reach all the way to the vortex cells of the renditions associated to the disk-collection.
The “forgotten” parts have the nice property that they use only flat parts of
\delta.

Exploiting this fact, we shall later see that all forgotten parts can be reconstructed as all such flat parts are sufficiently represented in the parterre segments of our walloid.
Hence, in the final proofs of this section, we only deal with the essential part of the extension of
Z
which is what remains after cropping.
Our goal is to sufficiently represent all essential parts in the form of flowers extracted from the thickets of our walloid.

Notice that the parts of an extension that survive the crop operation and are not covered by a
(d,k)
-cover
S
must invade the vortices only through the corridors corresponding to
S.

The space provided by the corridors allows for the application of the rerouting of
Lemma 3.4
(based on the vital linkage theorem), which implies that if the graph
M
of an extension invades the vortex of a disk
\Delta,
then an equivalent extension will invade it in a way that only a bounded number of vertices of
M
intersect the boundary of
\Delta.

Therefore we may assume that the restriction of
M
in
\Delta
is “represented” by an alternative fiber of bounded detail.
This is proved in
Lemma 7.9
.
With such a “representation” lemma at hand we can later argue, in
Lemma 7.10
, that if
S
does not hit such a fiber, then a big enough packing of this fiber exists in
G-S.

Lemma 7.9

(Representation lemma)
.

There exist functions
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},

f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N},
and
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that,

•

for every graph
Z,

•

every
q
-insulated
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
of a graph
G,
for some
q\geq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{1}(|Z|),

if,

•

for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},

\Psi^{\Delta}
is a corridor system of
\rho^{\Delta},

•

\Psi^{+}\coloneqq{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{\Psi^{\Delta}\cup\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}\},
and

•

(M,T)
is a
\delta
-bound
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting extension of
Z
in
G^{\prime}\coloneqq G-\pi_{\delta}(({\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{\Delta})\setminus\Psi^{+}),

then there exists a
\delta
-bound
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting extension
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
of
Z
in
G^{\prime}
where

•

(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop in
\Delta
for at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|)
many distinct disks
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},

•

for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},
if
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop in
\Delta,
then
\mathsf{crop}(M^{\prime},T^{\prime},\Delta)
has detail
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(|Z|),
and

•

all edges in
E(M^{\prime})\setminus E(M)
are drawn inside
\Psi={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{\Psi^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}\}.

Moreover

f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{1}(|Z|)=\mathcal{O}(\ell(\mathsf{h}(|Z|))),

f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|)=2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell(\mathsf{h}(|Z|)))},
and
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(|Z|)=\ell(\mathsf{h}(Z)).

Proof.

We define
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{1}(|Z|)\coloneqq(3\mathsf{h}(|Z|)+1)(3\ell(\mathsf{h}(|Z|))+2),

f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|)\coloneqq f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{3}(\mathsf{h}(|Z|)),
and
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(|Z|)\coloneqq f_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}^{2}(\mathsf{h}(|Z|))).

To begin, we assume that the detail of
(M,T)
is at most
d\coloneqq\mathsf{h}(|Z|)
and that no two vertices in
T
are adjacent in
M.

Indeed, the former property is implied by
Observation 7.7
and can be achieved by removing vertices of
M
as long as the conditions that
M
is connected,
\mathsf{dissolve}(M,T)
contains
Z
as a minor,
(M,T)
is
\delta
-bound, and
\Delta
-respecting are satisfied.
For the latter, if two vertices in
T
are adjacent in
M,
we simply subdivide this edge once, without affecting any of the desired properties.

The fact that
(M,T)
is
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting implies that
M
is not entirely drawn in the interior of some
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}
without intersecting its boundary
B_{\Delta}.

Also, notice that by definition of an extension, the graph
M-T
is a graph whose connected components are paths.
Let
\mathcal{L}
be a linkage in
G^{\prime}
that is the set of connected components of
M-T.

Observe that since the detail of
(M,T)
is at most
d,
we also have that the order of
\mathcal{L},

r\coloneqq|\mathcal{L}|,
is at most
d.

Now, the proof is essentially a careful application of
Lemma 3.4
.
Assume that
\mathbf{\Delta}=\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{b}\},
for some
b\in\mathbb{N}.

Now, for every
i\in[b],
we define the following.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{i}
be the nest around
c^{\Delta_{i}}
in
\rho^{\Delta_{i}}.

Since by assumption,
|\mathcal{C}^{i}|=q\geq(3d+1)(3\ell(r)+2),
by the pigeonhole principle, there is a subset
\mathcal{C}^{i}_{2}=\{C^{i}_{1},\ldots,C^{i}_{p}\}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{i}
of
p\coloneqq 3\ell(\mathcal{O}(r))+2
many consecutive cycles of
\mathcal{C}^{i}
such that, no vertex in
T
and no terminal vertex of
\mathcal{L}
is drawn in the disk
\Delta_{C_{p}}
minus every point of this disk drawn in the interior of the disk
\Delta_{C^{i}_{1}}.

Let
\rho_{2}^{\Delta_{i}}
be the
p
-nested cylindrical rendition obtained from
\rho^{\Delta_{i}}
by considering only the subset
\mathcal{C}^{i}_{2}
of the original nest
\mathcal{C}^{i}.

For every
i\in[b],
we define
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\Psi^{\Delta_{i}}\cup\Delta_{c^{\Delta_{i}}}.

Note that
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
is an arcwise connected set and that
M\cap\Delta_{i}
is drawn in
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}.

We are now in the position to apply
Lemma 3.4
for the graph
G,
the linkage
\mathcal{L}
in
G,
the
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta
of
G,
the sequence
(\rho^{\Delta_{1}}_{2},\ldots,\rho^{\Delta_{b}}_{2})
of
p
-insulated disks of
\delta,
and the arc-wise connected sets
\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{\prime}_{b}
that are subsets of
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{b}
respectively, with the property necessary to apply that lemma, that is every vertex and edge of
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}\cap\Delta_{i}
is drawn in
\Delta^{\prime}_{i},
in order to obtain a linkage
\mathcal{R}
with the following properties.

1.

\mathcal{R}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{L},

2.

\mathcal{R}
is a subgraph of the graph
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}\cup(\bigcup_{i\in[b]}({\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}^{i}_{2}\cap G^{\prime})),

3.

for every
i\in[b],
the number of disjoint subpaths of paths in
\mathcal{R}
with at least one endpoint in
B_{\Delta_{i}}
that contains an edge of drawn in the interior of the disk
\Delta_{C^{i}_{1}}
is at most
f^{2}_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}(r),
and

4.

a path in
\mathcal{R}
contains an edge drawn in the interior of the disk
\Delta_{C^{i}_{1}}
for at most
f^{3}_{\ref{lem_taming_linkage_nests}}(r)
distinct indices
i\in[b].

To conclude, we define the desired extension of
Z
in
G^{\prime}
as the pair
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
where
M^{\prime}
is obtained from
M
by replacing every path in
M^{\prime}
that corresponds to a path in
\mathcal{L}
by the corresponding path having the same terminals in
\mathcal{R}
and
T^{\prime}=T.

Clearly, since
\mathcal{R}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{L}
and is a subgraph of
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}\cup(\bigcup_{i\in[r]}({\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}^{i}_{2}\cap G^{\prime})),
and as such intersects no vertex in
T,
it is implied that
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is indeed a
\delta
-bound extension of
Z
in
G^{\prime}.

Moreover, all edges in
E(M^{\prime})\setminus E(M)
are drawn inside
\Psi
as required, since
\mathcal{R}
is a subgraph of
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}\cup(\bigcup_{i\in[r]}({\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}^{i}_{2}\cap G^{\prime}))
and by definition the graph
\bigcup_{i\in[r]}({\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}^{i}_{2}\cap G^{\prime})
is drawn inside
\Psi.

Additionally, the fact that
\mathcal{R}
satisfies property
4
above, combined with the definition of crop, implies that
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop in
\Delta
for at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|)
many distinct disks
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}.

Finally, the fact that
\mathcal{R}
satisfies property
3
above, again combined with the definition of crop, implies that for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},
if
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop in
\Delta,

then
\mathsf{crop}(M^{\prime},T^{\prime},\Delta)
has detail
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(|Z|).

This follows from the fact that, for every
i\in[r],
every path of
M^{\prime}
that connects a vertex of
B_{\Delta_{i}}
with a vertex whose point is in
\Delta_{c^{\Delta_{i}}}
must contain a subpath of a path in
\mathcal{R}
that satisfies
3
.
Since the number of such paths is bounded, this bounds the detail of any crop of
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
in
\Delta_{i}.

∎

We are now in the position to prove the main lemma of this section which can be seen as a form of a local Erdős-Pósa duality inside a collection of bounded depth disks.
It essentially implies that for every graph on
h
vertices, an
(\alpha,k)
-cover of a
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple provides such a duality for all their extensions.
The key condition is that
\alpha
is bounded by a function in
h,
depending on the unique linkage function
\ell.

Lemma 7.10

(Harvesting lemma)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N},

Z
be a graph on
h
vertices, and
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
be a
q
-insulated
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple of a graph
G,
where
q\geq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{1}(h).

If
S
is the
(f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h),k)
-cover of
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
and
(M,T)
is a
\delta
-bound extension of
Z
in
G^{\prime}\coloneqq G-S,
then there exists a
\delta
-bound and
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting extension
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
of
Z
in
G^{\prime}
such that

•

|T^{\prime}|\leq\mathsf{h}(h)
and

•

for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},
if
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop in
\Delta

and
\mu=\mathsf{crop}(M^{\prime},T^{\prime},\Delta),
then

–

\langle G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}\rangle
has an
\langle H,{\Lambda}^{\prime}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber, where
\langle H,{\Lambda}^{\prime}\rangle
is a shift of
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle\coloneqq\mathsf{dissolve}(\mu),
and

–

|V({\Lambda}^{\prime})|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h).

Proof.

Let
d\coloneqq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h).

Firstly, by definition of the
(d,k)
-cover of
(\delta,\Delta,y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}),

S=\bigcup_{\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}}S_{\Delta},
where for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},

S^{\Delta}
is the union of
\Delta
-compact sets obtained by the application of
Lemma 7.6
to every linear society of detail at most
d.

As a result, by
Observation 7.5
, each
S^{\Delta},

\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},
is itself a
\Delta
-compact set and as such it is accompanied by a corridor system
K^{\Delta}
of
\rho^{\Delta}.

Let

K^{+}={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{K^{\Delta}\cup\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}\mid i\in[r]\}.

Now, notice that since, for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},
every
\Delta
-compact set
S^{\Delta}
is accompanied by the corridor system
K^{\Delta},
by definition it must be that
(M,T)
is also an extension of
Z
in the graph
G^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq G-\pi_{\delta}(({\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{\Delta})\setminus K^{+}).

Assume that it is the case that
(M,T)
is also
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting.
Then, we are in the position to apply
Lemma 7.9
on
Z,

\delta,

\mathbf{\Delta},

K^{\Delta},
for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},
and
(M,T),
in order to obtain a
\delta
-bound and
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting extension
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
of
Z
in
G^{\prime\prime}
with the following additional properties.

1.

(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop in
\Delta
for at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(h)
many distinct disks
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},

2.

for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},
if
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop in
\Delta,
then
\mathsf{crop}(M^{\prime},T^{\prime},\Delta,\Delta)
has detail
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h),
and

3.

all edges in
E(M^{\prime})\setminus E(M)
are drawn inside
K={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\{K^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}\}.

The fact that
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
satisfies property
3
says that
M^{\prime}
differs from
M
only on vertices and edges drawn within the corridor systems accompanying each
\Delta
-compact set
S^{\Delta},
for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}.

In particular, by definition of compact sets, this implies that
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is also an extension of
Z
in
G^{\prime}.

This combined with the fact that
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
satisfies property
2
implies the following.
Assume that for a given
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},

(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop in
\Delta.

This implies that
\mu^{\Delta}\coloneqq\mathsf{crop}(M^{\prime},T^{\prime},\Delta)
is a
\mathsf{dissolve}(\mu^{\Delta})
-fiber of
\rho^{\Delta}
of detail at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(|Z|).

Assume that
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle=\mathsf{dissolve}(\mu^{\Delta}).

As we previously observed,
\mu^{\Delta}
is a
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of the graph
G^{\prime}
and more specifically it is also
a
\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle
-fiber of
\langle G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}\rangle-S^{\Delta}.

Hence, an application of
Lemma 7.6
on
\rho^{\Delta},

\langle H,\widehat{\Lambda}\rangle,
and
k,
must output an
\langle H,\Lambda^{\prime}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber of
\langle G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}\rangle
as required.

It remains to handle the case where
(M,T)
is not
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting.
By definition, this implies that
M
is drawn in the interior of exactly one of the disks say
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}.

However, the fact that
(M,T)
is
\delta
-bound, implies that there is a path
P
in
G
that is internally disjoint from
M
whose endpoints are a vertex of
M,
say
x
and a vertex of
B_{\Delta}.

We then define
M^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq M\cup P
and
T^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq T\cup\{x\}.

Clearly,
(M^{\prime\prime},T^{\prime\prime})
is a
\delta
-bound and
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting extension of
Z
in
G^{\prime}
and we can now reduce to the previous case.
∎

8
Plucking flowers from thickets

The main outcome of
Section 7
, namely
Lemma 7.10
, states a type of local Erdős-Pósa duality confined inside a collection of bounded depth disks of our
\Sigma
-decomposition.
In this section, we advance towards our goal by first applying that lemma to the
\Sigma
-disk-collection
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
that naturally corresponds to the thickets of our refined orchard, for a given graph
Z.

If none of the
(f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(|Z|),k)
-covers, that correspond to the disks of
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}),
obtained from the application of
Lemma 7.10
, intersect the graph
M
of an extension of
Z,
then we can find a sequence of many copies of the crop of
\Delta\cap M
inside
\Delta,
for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}.

We, then proceed with a series of successive transformations of these sequences that, in the end, will allow us to see every thicket as a flower that will be used in order to certify the existence of a guest walloid, as required for the proof of our local structure theorem in
Section 9
.

8.1
Certifying flatness

As we already mentioned, we use
Lemma 7.10
in order to extract
a “packing” of fibers rooted on a collection of disks around the vortices of our
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta.

This collection will later permit us to see the thickets of our orchard as the flowers of a guest walloid.
Recall that in an orchard all flaps of
\delta
are already represented by its flower segments, so the goal of applying
Lemma 7.10
for the purposes of this section, is in order to guarantee that we can also sufficiently represent the parts of an extension that invade the vortices of our
\Sigma
-decomposition.
To make the interaction
between the vortex-invading part of an extension of some graph
Z
with its flat part more precise,
we first give a way to formally certify flatness.

Flatness certifying complements.

Let
Z
be a graph and
\text{\bf\mu}=\langle\mu^{1},\ldots,\mu^{z}\rangle
be a sequence of fibers where
\mu^{i}=(M^{i},T^{i},\Lambda^{i}),i\in[z].

A
\Sigma
-
flat

Z
-
certifying complement
of
μ
is a tuple
\textbf{\bf C}=({\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},{M}^{\mathsf{c}},{T}^{\mathsf{c}},{\delta}^{\mathsf{c}},Y)
where

1.

{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}
is the closure of
\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{\Delta}
where
\mathbf{\Delta}=\{\Delta^{1},\ldots,\Delta^{z}\}
is a collection of pairwise-disjoint disks of
\Sigma,
all oriented counter-clockwise,

2.

({M}^{\mathsf{c}},{T}^{\mathsf{c}})
is a pair, where
{M}^{\mathsf{c}}
is a graph,
{T}^{\mathsf{c}}\subseteq V({M}^{\mathsf{c}})
and a vertex
x\in V(M^{\mathsf{c}})\setminus{T}^{\mathsf{c}}
has degree two if
x\not\in\bigcup_{i\in[z]}V(\Lambda^{i}),
otherwise it has degree zero or one in
M^{\mathsf{c}}.

3.

{\delta}^{\mathsf{c}}
is a vortex-free
{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}
-decomposition of
{M}^{\mathsf{c}},

4.

for every
i\in[z],

V(M^{i})\cap V({M}^{\mathsf{c}})=V(\Lambda^{i})
and the points, relatively to
\delta^{\mathsf{c}},
of all vertices of
V(\Lambda^{i})
are on the same boundary of
{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},

5.

for every
i\in[z]
and every
y\in V(\Lambda^{i})\setminus T^{i}
it holds that
\deg_{M^{i}}(y)+\deg_{M^{c}}(y)=2,

6.

Y=\{y^{1},\ldots,y^{z}\},
where for
i\in[z],
the ordering of the vertices of
V(M^{i})
whose points are on the boundary of
{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},
when starting from
y^{i}
and following the clockwise ordering of the boundary of
{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}
where they belong, is the same as the ordering of
\Lambda^{i}
(following the counterclockwise ordering of
{\sf bd}(\Delta_{i})
), and

7.

({M}^{\mathsf{c}}\cup\bigcup_{i\in[z]}M^{i},T^{\mathsf{c}}\cup\bigcup_{i\in[z]}T^{i})
is an extension of
Z.

We call this extension the
(\textbf{\bf C},\text{\bf\mu})
-
certifying
extension of
Z.

The
detail
of the pair
(\mathbf{C},\text{\bf\mu})
is the number of terminals of the extension of
Z
in condition
7
above.
Notice that a bound on the detail of
\mathbf{C}
implies also a bound on the total number of terminals of the fibers of
\text{\bf\mu}.

We also define

d1(\textbf{\bf C})=d1(\delta^{\mathsf{c}}).

We say that a society
\langle H,\Lambda\rangle
is
prime
if
\mathsf{dec}(H,\Lambda)=\langle\langle H,\Lambda\rangle\rangle.

Also, we say that a fiber
\mu=(M,T,\Lambda)
is
prime
if
\mathsf{dissolve}(\mu)
is prime.
Given two disjoint fibers
\mu_{1}=(M_{1},T_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
\mu_{2}=(M_{2},T_{2},\Lambda_{2}),
we say that they are
disjoint
if
V(M_{1})\cap V(M_{2})=\emptyset.

Given that
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are disjoint, we define
\mu_{1}\oplus\mu_{2}=(M_{1}\cup M_{2},T_{1}\cup T_{2},\Lambda_{1}\oplus\Lambda_{2}).

Figure 21
:
A visualization of
Lemma 8.1
for one, say
\Delta,
of the disks of
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}.
The fiber
\mu^{\Delta}
consists of the first prime fiber of each of the

p^{\Delta}
groups of pairwise equivalent fibers.
Each fiber intersects the disk
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}
of the vortex
c^{\Delta}
because it is a result of the cropping operation.

The following lemma is a consequence of
Lemma 7.6
and the definition of a
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complement (for a visualization, see
Figure 21
).

Lemma 8.1
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{internal_certification_without_thickets}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given

•

k,h,q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

z,w\in\mathbb{N},

•

a graph
G,

•

a
q
-insulated
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
of
G,
where

–

q\geq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{1}(h),

–

|\mathbf{\Delta}|\leq z,
and

–

for every disk
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},

\rho^{\Delta}
has combined depth
\leq w,

outputs a set
S,
where
|S|\leq f_{\ref{internal_certification_without_thickets}}(z,w,k,h),

such that for every graph
Z
on
\leq h
vertices, either

G-S
contains no
\delta
-bound extension of
Z

or there exists a
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}
and a set
\text{\bf\mu}\coloneqq\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta^{\prime}}\}
of fibers such that

•

for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
there exists a
p^{\Delta}\in\mathbb{N},
where

–

for every
j\in[p^{\Delta}],
there exists a set
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta},\ldots,\mu_{k,j}^{\Delta}\}
of
k
pairwise disjoint and pairwise equivalent prime fibers, where

*

for every
i\in[k],

\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq(M_{i,j}^{\Delta},T_{i,j}^{\Delta},\Lambda_{i,j}^{\Delta})
and
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
has a vertex drawn in
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}},
and

*

\Lambda_{j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\Lambda_{1,j}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{k,j}^{\Delta},

–

\Lambda^{\Delta}\coloneqq\Lambda_{1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
and
\Lambda^{\Delta}\subseteq\Lambda^{(\Delta)},
and

–

\mu^{\Delta}=\mu_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\ldots,\oplus\mu_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
is a fiber of
(G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}),

•

μ
has a
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complement
C
where
d1(\textbf{\bf C})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every
d\in\mathbb{N},
and

•

the boundary of each of the fibers in
μ
has at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h)
vertices,
|\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(h),
and the detail of
(\textbf{\bf C},\text{\bf\mu})
is at most
\mathsf{h}(h),

in time

\mathcal{O}_{h}(zwk\cdot|G|^{3}).

Moreover

f_{\ref{internal_certification_without_thickets}}(z,w,k,h)=\mathcal{O}_{h}(zwk).

Proof.

Let
S
be the
(f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}({\sf h}(h)),k)
-cover of
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}),
where
\delta=(\Gamma,\mathcal{D}).
As each disk in
\mathbf{\Delta}
has combined depth
\leq w,
from
Lemma 7.6

we have that
|S|\leq f_{\ref{func_sharvsp}}(k,w,f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}({\sf h}(h))))\cdot z.

Also, from
Observation 7.8

S
can be found in time
\mathcal{O}_{h}(k\cdot w\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3})
-time.

We claim that the lemma holds if we set
f_{\ref{internal_certification_without_thickets}}(z,w,k,h)=f_{\ref{func_sharvsp}}(k,w,f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}({\sf h}(h)),k))\cdot z.

Suppose that
Z
is a graph on at most
h
vertices
such that
G^{\prime}\coloneqq G-S
contains some
\delta
-bound extension
(M,T)
of
Z,
as the one in
Observation 7.7
, that has detail bounded by
{\sf h}(h).
Then,
consider a
\delta
-bound and
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting extension
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
of
Z
in
G^{\prime}
where
|T^{\prime}|\leq{\sf h}(h)
as the one of
Lemma 7.10
. Let also
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}

be the set of the disks of
\mathbf{\Delta}

where
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
has a crop and, for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime},
let

\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}=\mathsf{crop}(M^{\prime},T^{\prime},\Delta).
From
Lemma 7.9
,
|\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}({\sf h}(h)).

We next claim that
\overline{\text{\bf\mu}}\coloneqq\{\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\}
has a
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complement

\textbf{\bf C}.

Indeed, this complement is the tuple
\textbf{\bf C}=({\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},{M}^{\mathsf{c}},{T}^{\mathsf{c}},{\delta}^{\mathsf{c}},Y)
where

1.

{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}
is the closure of
\Sigma\setminus{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime},

2.

{M}^{\mathsf{c}}\coloneqq{M^{\prime}\cap{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}}
and
{T}^{\mathsf{c}}\coloneqq T^{\prime}\cap{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}
(as
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is
\mathbf{\Delta}
-respecting,
M^{\mathsf{c}}
is non-empty.),

3.

\delta^{\mathsf{c}}=(\Gamma\cap{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},\{\Delta_{c}\in\mathcal{D}\mid\Delta_{c}\subseteq{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}\})\cap M^{\mathsf{c}},
i.e., we first take the restriction of
\delta
inside
{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}
and then we consider the rendition induced by
M^{\mathsf{c}},
and

4.

Y=\{y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}(\Delta)\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\}.

Notice that
d1(\textbf{\bf C})=d1(\delta^{\mathsf{c}})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every
d\in\mathbb{N}.
Also
|T^{\prime}|\leq{\sf h}(h)
implies
directly that
(\mathbf{C},\overline{\text{\bf\mu}})
has detail
\leq{\sf h}(h).

From
Lemma 7.10
,
\mathsf{dissolve}(\overline{\mu}^{\Delta})
has a shift
\langle H^{\Delta},{\Lambda}^{\prime\Delta}\rangle
such that
\langle G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}\rangle
contains an
\langle H^{\Delta},{\Lambda}^{\prime\Delta}\rangle^{(k)}
-fiber
\widetilde{\mu},

where
|V({\Lambda}^{\prime\Delta})|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h).

Let

{\sf dec}(\langle H^{\Delta},{\Lambda}^{\prime\Delta}\rangle)=\langle\langle H_{1}^{\Delta},{\Lambda}^{\prime\Delta}_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta},{\Lambda}_{p^{\Delta}}^{\prime\Delta}\rangle\rangle.

For every
j\in[p^{\Delta}],

the fibers
\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta},\ldots,\mu_{k,j}^{\Delta}
are defined
as the pairwise equivalent prime fibers such that
{\sf dissolve}(\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta})=\langle H_{j}^{\Delta},{\Lambda}^{\prime\Delta}_{j}\rangle
and where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mu_{k,1}^{\Delta}\ \oplus\ \ \cdots\ \ \oplus\ \ \mu_{1,j}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mu_{k,j}^{\Delta}\ \ \oplus\ \ \cdots\ \ \oplus\ \mu_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mu_{k,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}.

(17)

Let
\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq(M_{i,j}^{\Delta},T_{i,j}^{\Delta},\Lambda_{i,j}^{\Delta}).
If some
j\in[p^{\Delta}],

M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
has no vertex drawn in

\Delta_{c^{\Delta}},
this means that
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
is drawn
in a
\delta
-aligned closed disk
\Delta_{j}
of
\Delta^{\prime}
that does not intersect the vortex disk
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}
and where
{\sf bd}(\Delta_{j})\cap{\sf bd}(\Delta)
is a line where all the boundary vertices of
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
are drawn. We update
\overline{\mu}
by eliminating
\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mu_{k,j}^{\Delta}
in (
17
) and update
C
by enhancing it with the “flat” drawing of
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
by setting

\Sigma^{\mathsf{c}}\coloneqq\Sigma^{\mathsf{c}}\cup\Delta_{j},

M^{\mathsf{c}}\coloneqq M^{\mathsf{c}}\cup M_{i,j}^{\Delta},

T^{\mathsf{c}}\coloneqq T^{\mathsf{c}}\cup T_{i,j}^{\Delta}

and update
\delta^{\mathsf{c}}
as in step 3 above.
By applying these enhancements, we guarantee that
every graph
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
must have some vertex drawn in
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}.

We next observe that
\mu^{\Delta}=\mu_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\ldots,\oplus\mu_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
is created by concatenating the first, i.e.,

\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta},
fiber from each of the above
p^{\Delta}
groups of
k
pairwise equivalent fibers
each. Notice also that for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},

{\sf dissolve}(\mu^{\Delta})
is a shift of

{\sf dissolve}(\overline{\mu}^{\Delta})
and that
\mu^{\Delta}
as well as
\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}

have a boundary of
|V({\Lambda}^{\prime\Delta})|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h)
vertices. This implies that
\textbf{\bf C}^{\prime}=({\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},{M}^{\mathsf{c}},{T}^{\mathsf{c}},{\delta}^{\mathsf{c}},Y^{\prime})

is a
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complement of
{\text{\bf\mu}}\coloneqq\{{\mu}^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\}

where
Y^{\prime}
is a suitable update of
Y
according to to the shifts required for transforming
\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}
to
{\mu}^{\Delta},
for each
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}.

Clearly, it holds that
d1(\textbf{\bf C}^{\prime})=d1(\textbf{\bf C})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every
d\in\mathbb{N}.

∎

Notice that in the proof above, the
Z
-certifying complement
C
is constructed based on the way that
(M^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is drawn outside of the disks of
\mathbf{\Delta},
according to
\delta.

Moving on, we will successively derive several collections
μ
of fibers whose
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complements will be constructed based on the previous
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complements and not explicitly from the
\Sigma
-decomposition
\delta.

We refer to each set
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\Delta},

j\in[p^{\Delta}]
as in the conclusion of
Lemma 8.1
, as the
j
-
th package
(of fibers) in the disk
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}.

While
\mu^{\Delta}
is built by concatenating the first fiber of each
j
-th package, one might
instead use any other in
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\Delta},
as they are all equivalent.
We refer to the sets
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\Delta},\ldots,\mathcal{M}_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
as
packages
of
\text{\bf\mu}.

8.2
Flatness certifying complements of thickets

Our next step is to apply
Lemma 8.1
in the thickets of a
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard.
In this subsection we consider such a thicket and we explain how the packages of the fibers in the output of
Lemma 8.1
can be seen as fibers rooted on the boundary of a thicket society of our given orchard.

Thickets and their renditions.

Let
(G,\delta,W)
be a
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard and let
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{f}}(\delta)
be the vortex of one of the thickets of
W
whose railed nest is
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R}).

Let also
\Delta
be the disk bounded by the trace of the outer cycle
C_{s+1}
of the thicket containing the vortex
c.

We define
\mathbf{\Delta}
as the set of all such disks corresponding to the thickets of
(G,\delta,W)
and we refer to it as the
disk collection
of
(G,\delta,W).

For each
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta},
we denote its corresponding railed nest by
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta},\mathcal{R}^{\Delta}),
where
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}=\{C_{0}^{\Delta},\ldots,C_{s+1}^{\Delta}\}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\Delta}=\{R_{1}^{\Delta},\ldots,R_{t}^{\Delta}\}.

Also,

•

for
i\in[t],
we update
R_{i}^{\Delta}
to be the unique connected component of
R_{i}^{\Delta}\cap\Delta
that has one endpoint in
V(C_{0}^{\Delta})
and we call the other endpoint
\widehat{y}_{i}^{\Delta}
(that is a vertex of
B_{\Delta}
),

•

we define
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\langle\widehat{y}_{1}^{\Delta},\ldots,\widehat{y}_{t}^{\Delta}\rangle,
and

•

we update
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\{C_{1}^{\Delta},\ldots,C_{s}^{\Delta}\},

We refer to
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}=\{\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}\}
as the
boundary
collection of the
(t,b,h,s,\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W).

We also use
\widehat{y}^{\Delta}
as a simpler alternative for the first vertex of
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta},
i.e., the vertex
\widehat{y}_{1}^{\Delta}.

We now explain how to see the fibers in
\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\prime}\}
in the output of
Lemma 8.1
as fibers whose boundaries are in
B_{\Delta}.

This is done in
Lemma 8.2
below.
We stress that the constants
h^{\prime},

k^{\prime},
and
t^{\prime}
in the statement of
Lemma 8.2
are irrelevant as they are not used in neither its conclusion nor its proof.

Lemma 8.2
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{5}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given

•

k,h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and

•

a
(z,p)
-ripe and
d^{\prime}
-blooming
(t^{\prime},k^{\prime},h^{\prime},s,\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W),
where
s\geq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{1}(h)+1,

outputs a set
S,
where
|S|\leq f_{\ref{extending_internal_Certification_in_thickets}}(z,p,k,h,s)
such that for every graph
Z
on at most
h
vertices, either
G-S
contains no
\delta
-bound extension of
Z
or there is a subset
{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\prime}
of the disk collection
\mathbf{\Delta}
of
(G,\delta,W),
a svaf
y_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}},
and a set
\text{\bf\mu}\coloneqq\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\prime}\}
of fibers such that

•

for every
\Delta\in{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}}
there exists some
p^{\Delta}\in\mathbb{N}
where

–

for every
j\in[p^{\Delta}],
there exists a
j
package
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta},\ldots,\mu_{k,j}^{\Delta}\}
of
k
pairwise disjoint and pairwise equivalent prime fibers, where

*

for every
i\in[k],

\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq(M_{i,j}^{\Delta},T_{i,j}^{\Delta},\Lambda_{i,j}^{\Delta})
and
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
has some vertex drawn in
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}
and

*

\Lambda_{j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\Lambda_{1,j}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{k,j}^{\Delta},

–

\Lambda^{\Delta}\coloneqq\Lambda_{1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
and
\Lambda^{\Delta}\subseteq\Lambda^{(\Delta)},
and

–

\mu^{\Delta}=\mu_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\ldots,\oplus\mu_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
is a fiber of
(G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}),

•

μ
has a
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complement
C
where
d1(\textbf{\bf C})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every
d\in\mathbb{N},
and

•

the boundary of each of the fibers in
μ
has at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h)
vertices,
|\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|),
and the detail of
(\textbf{\bf C},\text{\bf\mu})
is at most
{\sf h}(h).

Moreover, the above algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}_{h}({k}\cdot(s+p)\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3})
and
f_{\ref{extending_internal_Certification_in_thickets}}(z,p,k,h,s)={\mathcal{O}_{h}(z\cdot k\cdot(s+p))}.

Proof.

We prove the lemma for the following quantities.

•

w\coloneqq 2s+p
and

•

f_{\ref{extending_internal_Certification_in_thickets}}(z,p,k,h,s)\coloneqq f_{\ref{internal_certification_without_thickets}}(z,w,k,h).

We pick some svaf
y_{\mathbf{\Delta}}
and we consider the
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
of
G.

This gives rise to some linear ordering
\Lambda^{(\Delta)}
for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}.

Let
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}
and let
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta},\mathcal{R}^{\Delta})
be the corresponding railed nest.
We also consider the
\delta
-aligned disks
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{s}
where
{\sf bd}(\Delta_{i})
is the
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C^{\mathsf{si}})
-avoiding disk of the
\delta
-trace of the cycle
C_{i}^{\Delta},

i\in[s],

\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}
is a subset of the interior of
\Delta_{1},

\Delta_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Delta_{s},
and
\Delta_{s}
is a subset of the interior of
\Delta.

Also consider the
s
-nested cylindrical rendition
\rho^{\Delta}=\delta\cap\Delta
of the linear society
\langle G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}\rangle
around the cell
c_{\Delta}.

Observe that
\langle G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{(\Delta)}\rangle
corresponds to a thicket society of the
(t,s)
-thicket that corresponds to
\Delta,
and that since
(G,\delta,W)
is
(z,p)
-ripe,
\rho^{\Delta}
has combined depth at most
w.

We are now in a position to apply
Lemma 8.1
for the
q
-insulated
\Sigma
-disk-collection triple
(\delta,\mathbf{\Delta},y_{\mathbf{\Delta}})
of
G,
for
q\coloneqq s,
and
k.

This takes time
\mathcal{O}_{h}(k\cdot w\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3})=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k\cdot z\cdot(s+p)\cdot|G|^{3}).

Given that
S
is the output of the application of
Lemma 8.1
above, we have that
|S|\leq f_{\ref{internal_certification_without_thickets}}(z,w,k,h)=f_{\ref{extending_internal_Certification_in_thickets}}(z,p,k,h,s).

Moreover, it holds that for every graph
Z
on at most
h
vertices, either
G-S
contains no
\delta
-bound extension of
Z
or there is a set
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{\Delta}
and a set
\text{\bf\mu}\coloneqq\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\}
of fibers such that

1.

for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime},
there exists some
p^{\Delta}\in\mathbb{N}
where

•

for every
j\in[p^{\Delta}]
there exists a
j
package
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta},\ldots,\mu_{k,j}^{\Delta}\}
of
k
pairwise disjoint and pairwise equivalent prime fibers, where

–

for every
i\in[k],

\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq(M_{i,j}^{\Delta},T_{i,j}^{\Delta},\Lambda_{i,j}^{\Delta})
and
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
has some vertex drawn in
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}
and

–

\Lambda_{j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\Lambda_{1,j}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{k,j}^{\Delta},

•

\Lambda^{\Delta}\coloneqq\Lambda_{1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
and
\Lambda^{\Delta}\subseteq\Lambda^{(\Delta)},
and

•

\mu^{\Delta}=\mu_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\ldots,\oplus\mu_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
is a fiber of
(G\cap\Delta,\Lambda^{({\Delta})}),

2.

μ
has a
Z
-certifying complement
C
where
d1(\textbf{\bf C})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every
d\in\mathbb{N},
and

3.

the boundary of each of the fibers in
μ
has at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h)
vertices,
|\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|),
and the detail of
(\textbf{\bf C},\text{\bf\mu})
is at most
{\sf h}(h).

exactly as desired and we can conclude.
∎

8.3
A variant of the combing lemma

We need the following variant of the Combing lemma in
[
26
]
.
The statement is slightly different (and simpler) than the one of
[
26
, Theorem 5]
and is suitable for the needs
of the final result of this subsection.
The proof uses the same ideas and the bulk of them have already been used for the results of
Subsection 3.2
.
This lemma will be useful for the proof of the main result of this section,
Lemma 8.4
, that is proven in
Subsection 8.4
.

Lemma 8.3
.

There exists two functions
f^{1}_{\ref{combing_variant}}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
f^{2}_{\ref{combing_variant}}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that the following holds: Let
r\coloneqq 2\overline{r}+1,
let

(M,T)
be an extension of detail
\leq h
,
and let
G

be the graph obtained by the union of
M,

with the cycles of some collection
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{d}\}
of pairwise disjoint nested cycles and the paths of some linkage
\mathcal{L}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{a}\}

such that
a\coloneqq f^{1}_{\ref{combing_variant}}(h),

d\coloneqq f^{2}_{\ref{combing_variant}}(h)+r
and is odd, and
G
has a
\Sigma^{(0,0)}
-decomposition
\delta
and there is an annulus
A\subseteq\Sigma^{(0,0)}

with the following properties

•

If
\Delta_{\rm in}
and
\Delta_{\rm out}
are the closures of the two connected components of
\Sigma^{0,0}\setminus A,

then
\Delta_{\rm in}
and
\Delta_{\rm out}
are both
\delta
-alinged.

•

For every
i\in[d],
the boundary
{\sf bd}(\Delta_{\rm out}),
as well as the traces of the cycles
C_{1},\ldots,C_{i-1},

are subsets of the one, say
\Delta_{i},
of the two open disk bounded by
\mathsf{trace}_{\delta}(C_{i})

while the boundary
{\sf bd}(\Delta_{\rm in}),
as well as the traces of the cycles
C_{i+1},\ldots,C_{d},
are subsets of the other. We also set
\Delta_{0}\coloneqq\Delta_{\rm out}
and

\Delta_{d+1}\coloneqq A\cup\delta_{\rm out}.

•

For every
i\in[a],
one, say
y_{i}^{\rm out},
of the endpoints of
P_{i}
is drawn in
{\sf bd}(\Delta_{\rm out})

and the other, say
y_{i}^{\rm in},
is drawn in
{\sf bd}(\Delta_{\rm in})
and all other vertices of

P
are drawn in the interior of
A.

•

the vertices
y_{1}^{\rm out},\ldots,y_{a}^{\rm out}
are drawn in this order on the boundary of
\Delta_{\rm out}.

•

M
and
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}
are vertex disjoint.

•

None of the vertices in
T
is drawn in
A.

•

the linkage
\mathcal{L}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}.

•

\delta
has only two vortices
c^{\rm out}
and
c^{\rm in}

where
\Delta_{c^{\rm out}}=\Delta^{\rm out}
and
\Delta_{c^{\rm in}}=\Delta^{\rm in}.

For every
l,l^{\prime}\in[0,d+1]
where
l<l^{\prime},
we use notation
A_{l,l^{\prime}}
for the closure of
\Delta_{l}\setminus\Delta_{l^{\prime}}
and we denote by
{\mathcal{L}}_{l,l^{\prime}}
be the linkage consisting of
every connected component of
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}\cap A_{l,l^{\prime}}
that has endpoints drawn in different boundaries of
A_{l,l^{\prime}}.

Then there exists an extension
(M^{\prime},T)
where, given that
q\coloneqq f^{2}_{\ref{combing_variant}}(h)/2

•

{\sf dissolve}(M^{\prime},T)={\sf dissolve}(M,T),

•

M^{\prime}\cap(\Delta_{\rm in}\cup\Delta_{\rm out})\subseteq M\cap(\Delta_{\rm in}\cup\Delta_{\rm out}),
and

•

M^{\prime}\cap A_{z+1,z+r}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{{q}+1,{q}+r}.

Figure 22
:
A visualization of the proof of
Lemma 8.3
. The rivers of
\mathcal{L}_{G}

are blue while the
0
-mountains and the
(d+1)
-valleys are grey. The cycles in
\mathcal{C}
are red and the substitution paths
P_{i}^{\,{\sf h},{\rm out}},

P_{i}^{\,{\sf h},{\rm in}},
and
P_{i}^{\,{\sf v}},
for
i\in[a]
are green. The annulus
A_{z+1,z+r}
is light green.

Proof.

Let
a
be the smallest even number that is no smaller than
\ell(h),
where
\ell\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is the linkage function (see
Subsection 3.2
).
Let also
b\coloneqq\frac{3}{2}a+1,

d\coloneqq 2ab+r.
We prove the lemma for
f^{1}_{\ref{combing_variant}}(h)\coloneqq a
and
f^{2}_{\ref{combing_variant}}(h)\coloneqq 2ab.
Observe that
q=ab.

Also keep in mind that
f^{1}_{\ref{combing_variant}}(h)=\mathcal{O}(\ell(h))
and
f^{2}_{\ref{combing_variant}}(h)=\mathcal{O}((\ell(h))^{2}).

Notice first that, as none of the vertices in
T
is drawn in
A,

we may subdivide once all neighbors in
G
of the terminals in
T,
remove the terminals and by considering their neighbors as new terminals create a linkage
\mathcal{L}_{G}
of size
\leq h
.
Notice that the result follows if instead we prove it for the graph
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}_{G}
: in the result of the theorem we may apply the reverse transformation, that is add back the terminal vertices and the removed edges and then dissolve the subdivision vertices.
Therefore we assume that
G={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}_{G}
for some linkage
\mathcal{L}_{G}.

Observe that all components of

G\cap A
are paths that, in turn, are all disjoint from
{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{L}.

Notice also that for every cell
c
of
\delta
where
c\subseteq A,

it holds that
\sigma_{\delta}(c)
is either a path between two of the vertices in
\pi_{\delta}(\widetilde{c})
or three paths from a vertex
of
\sigma_{\delta}(c)-\pi_{\delta}(\widetilde{c})
to the three vertices of
\pi_{\delta}(\widetilde{c}).
This permits us to see the part of
G
that is drawn inside
A
as a planar embedding.
Next observe that
(\mathcal{L}_{G},B)
is an LB-pair of
G,
where
B={\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{C}.

Among all linkages that are equivalent to
\mathcal{L}_{G}
we assume that
\mathcal{L}_{G}
minimizes the quantity

\mathsf{cae}(\mathcal{L}_{G},B)\coloneqq|E(\mathcal{L})\setminus E(B)|.

By
Proposition 3.3
we know that
{\sf tw}(\mathcal{L}_{G}\cup B)\leq\ell(h)=a.

Similarly to what we did in
Subsection 3.2
, we may classify the connected components of
G\cap A,
to rivers, mountains, and
valleys: A
river
is a path whose endpoints are drawn in different
boundaries of
A.

For every
i\in[0,d]
an
i
-mountain is a connected component of
G\cap A_{i,d+1}
and for every
i\in[1,d+1]
an
i
-valley is a connected component of
G\cap A_{0,i}.

Also the
height
(resp.
depth
) of an
i
-mountain (
i
-valey)
P
is
the maximum
j
such that
C_{i+j-1}
(resp.
C_{i-j+1})
intersects
P.

As we already argued
in
Subsection 3.2
, because of
[
26
, Lemma 4]
, there are no more than
{\sf tw}(\mathcal{L}_{G}\cup B)\leq a
rivers. we set
b\coloneqq\frac{3}{2}a+1.

Moreover, because of
[
26
, Lemma 7]
,
the height (resp. depth) of an
l
-valey (resp.
l
-mountain)
is never more than
\frac{3}{2}{\sf tw}(\mathcal{L}_{G}\cup B)<b.

With these bounds at hand, we may reroute the

\leq a
rivers by reproducing a simpler version of the proof of
[
26
, Theorem 5]
. Let
R_{1},\ldots,R_{q}
be the rivers, for
q\leq a.

For
i=1,\ldots,q,
let
x_{\rm out}^{i}
be the last vertex of
C_{ib}
that
R_{i}
meets when traversed from its endpoint in
B_{\Delta_{\rm out}}
to its endpoint in
B_{\Delta_{\rm in}}.
Symmetrically,
for
i=1,\ldots,q,
let
x_{\rm in}^{i}
is the last vertex of
C_{d-ib-1}
that
R_{i}
meets when traversed from its endpoint in
B_{\Delta_{\rm in}}
to its endpoint in
B_{\Delta_{\rm out}}.
We then discard from each
P_{i}

the subpath with endpoints
x_{\rm out}^{i}
and
x_{\rm in}^{i}
and replace each of the removed paths by the union of three paths: two “horizontal” paths
P_{i}^{\,{\sf h},{\rm out}},
