P_{i}^{\,{\sf h},{\rm in}}
and one “vertical”
P_{i}^{\,{\sf v}},
as indicated in
Figure 22
.
The fact that, for
i\in[q]
the horizontal path
P_{i}^{\,{\sf h},{\rm out}},
does not intersect the surviving portions of the paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{i-1}
follows by the fact that
all
(i-1)b
-mountains have height
<b
and the symmetric argument holds for the horizontal path
P_{i}^{\,{\sf h},{\rm in}}.
It is now easy to see that the above substitution creates a linkage that is equivalent to
\mathcal{L}_{G}
does not
change outside
A,
and moreover, it traverses the annulus
A_{z+1,z+r}
by exclusively using the paths in
\mathcal{L}_{z+1,z+r},
as required.
∎

8.4
Plucking a flower

We have now gathered all the tools that we need for the proof of the final result of this subsection.
The statement is similar to the one of
Lemma 8.2
however, we now need to “root” the fibers in
\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\prime}\}
not only to vertices of the boundaries are in
B_{\Delta}
(this is already done in
Lemma 8.2
) but to the particular vertices of this boundary that are crossed by the linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\Delta}
of the thicket. i..e, the linear ordering
V(\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta}).

This rerouting is important as it permits us to eventually see each thicket as a flower.

Similarly to the case of
Lemma 8.2
, constants
h^{\prime}
and
k^{\prime}
in the statement of
Lemma 8.4
are irrelevant, as they are not used neither in its conclusion nor in its proof.
However, now we need to give a lower bound, not only for
s,
but also to the order of the thicket, that is
t=|\mathcal{R}|^{\Delta},
in order to obtain our rerouting.

Lemma 8.4
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}},f^{2}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N},
and
f^{3}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given

•

k,h\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and

•

a
(z,p)
-ripe and
d^{\prime}
-blooming
(t,k^{\prime},h^{\prime},s,\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W),
where
s\geq f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,h),

t\geq f^{2}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,h),

outputs a set
S,
where
|S|\leq f^{3}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(z,p,k,h)
such that for every graph
Z
in
h
vertices, either
G-S
contains no
\delta
-bound extension of
Z
or there is some subset
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
of the disk collection
\mathbf{\Delta}
of
(G,\delta,W)
and a set
\text{\bf\mu}\coloneqq\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta^{\prime}}\}
of fibers such that

1.

for every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
there exists some
p^{\Delta}\in\mathbb{N}
where

i.

for every
j\in[p^{\Delta}],
there exists a
j
-package
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{{\Delta}}\coloneqq\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta},\ldots,\mu_{k,j}^{\Delta}\}
of
k
pairwise disjoint and pairwise equivalent prime fibers, where

–

for every
i\in[k],

\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq(M_{i,j}^{\Delta},T_{i,j}^{\Delta},\Lambda_{i,j}^{\Delta})
and

–

\Lambda_{j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\Lambda_{1,j}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{k,j}^{\Delta},

ii.

\Lambda^{\Delta}\coloneqq\Lambda_{1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta},

\Lambda^{\Delta}\subseteq\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta},
and
\Lambda^{\Delta}
and
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta}
have the same starting vertex
y^{\Delta}.

iii.

\mu^{\Delta}=\mu_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\ldots,\oplus\mu_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
is a fiber of
(G\cap\Delta,\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta}),

2.

μ
has a
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complement
C
where
d1(\textbf{\bf C})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every
d\in\mathbb{N},
and

3.

the boundary of each of the fibers in
μ
has at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h)
vertices,
|\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|),
and the detail of
(\textbf{\bf C},{\text{\bf\mu}})
is at most
{\sf h}(h).

Moreover, the above algorithm runs in
\mathcal{O}_{h}(k\cdot(s+p)\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3})
-time.
Moreover,
f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,h)=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k^{2}),

f^{2}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,h)=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k),
and
f^{3}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,h)={\mathcal{O}_{h}(zk(k^{2}+p))}.

Proof.

Let also
\gamma:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be a function
such that
\gamma(x)
is an upper bound to the number of different societies on
\leq x
vertices. Clearly, we may assume that
\gamma(x)=2^{\mathcal{O}(x^{2})}.

We define the following quantities:

•

a\coloneqq f^{1}_{\ref{combing_variant}}({\sf h}(h)),

•

\breve{k}=a\cdot k,

•

\widetilde{k}\coloneqq\gamma(a)\cdot\breve{k}=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k),

•

f^{2}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,h)=a\cdot\widetilde{k}

•

\widehat{k}\coloneqq(a+1)\cdot\widetilde{k},

•

\overline{r}\coloneqq 1+a\cdot\widetilde{k},

•

r=2\overline{r}+1,

•

d=f_{\ref{combing_variant}}^{2}({\sf h}(h))+r,
and

•

f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}({k},h)\coloneqq(d+2)\cdot(\widehat{k}\cdot{\sf h}(h)+1)=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k^{2}).

•

f^{3}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(z,p,k,h)\coloneqq f_{\ref{extending_internal_Certification_in_thickets}}(z,p,\widehat{k},h,f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(\widehat{k},h))=\mathcal{O}_{h}(zk(k^{2}+p))

We fist apply
Lemma 8.2
for
s\coloneqq f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(\widehat{k},h)
,
h,
and
k\coloneqq\widehat{k}.

As a result of this, we obtain
a set
S,
where
|S|\leq f_{\ref{extending_internal_Certification_in_thickets}}(z,p,\widehat{k},h,f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(\widehat{k},h))
and
such that for every graph
Z
on
\leq h
vertices, if
G-S
contains a
\delta
-bound extension of
Z
then there is some subset
{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\prime}
of the disk collection
\mathbf{\Delta}
of
(G,\delta,W),
a svaf
y_{\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}},

and a set
\text{\bf\mu}\coloneqq\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\prime}\}
of fibers such that
all the conditions of the lemma are satisfied,
except from sub-condition
1.ii
, where we only have that

\Lambda^{\Delta}\subseteq\Lambda^{(\Delta)}.

Let
\text{\bf\mu}\coloneqq\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta^{\prime}}\}
and

\textbf{\bf C}=({\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},{M}^{\mathsf{c}},{T}^{\mathsf{c}},{\delta}^{\mathsf{c}},Y)
be such that conditions
2
and
3
are satisfied for
\widehat{k}.
The algorithm of
Lemma 8.2
runs in
\mathcal{O}_{h}(\widehat{k}\cdot(s+p)\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3})
-time.

Let
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}.
As we did in the proof of
Lemma 8.2
we consider the railed nest
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta},\mathcal{R}^{\Delta})
corresponding to
\Delta

and the
\delta
-aligned disks
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{s}
where
{\sf bd}(\Delta_{i})
is the
\delta
-trace of the cycle
C_{i}^{\Delta},

i\in[s],

\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}
is a subset of the interior of
\Delta_{1},

\Delta_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Delta_{s},

and
\Delta_{s}
is a subset of the interior of
\Delta.

Recall that every graph
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
contains at least one vertex from
B_{\Delta}
and at least one vertex drawn in
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}}.
This implies the existence
of a
B_{\Delta}
-
B_{c^{\Delta}}
-linkage
\mathcal{L}
of
k\cdot p^{\Delta}
paths in
G\cap\Delta,
where each path is a subgraph of some
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}.

Our next observation is that, because the detail of
(\textbf{\bf C},{\text{\bf\mu}})
is at most
{\sf h}(h),

each
|T_{i,j}^{\Delta}|
is bounded by
{\sf h}(h).

As a result of this, the set
\overline{T}=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in[\widehat{k}]\times[p^{\Delta}]}T_{i,j}^{\Delta}
has at most
\widehat{k}\cdot{\sf h}(h)
vertices.
Given that
s\geq f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,h)=(d+2)\cdot(\widehat{k}\cdot{\sf h}(h)+1)
, there is some

i^{\prime}\in[s]
such that none of the terminals in
\overline{T}

is drawn in the closure of
\Delta_{i}\setminus\Delta_{i^{\prime}+d+1}.

This closure is an annulus containing the drawings of all
{d}

cycles in
\{C_{i^{\prime}+1}^{\Delta},\cdots,C_{i^{\prime}+d-1}^{\Delta}\}.

For the sake of simplicity, we rename these cycles and we index them in reverse ordering, that is from outside towards inside, that is

C_{1}=C_{i^{\prime}+d-1}^{\Delta},
…,
C_{d}=C_{i^{\prime}+1}^{\Delta}.

Similarly, we set
D_{0}=\Delta_{i^{\prime}+d},\ldots,D_{d+1}=\Delta_{i^{\prime}+2}.

Resuming we have
d
cycles drawn in the interior of the annulus
formed by the closure, denoted by
A^{\Delta},
of
D_{0}\setminus D_{d+1}.
Moreover none of the terminals of the fibers in the
j
-packages of
\Delta
is drawn in
A^{\Delta}.

As
\widehat{k}=(a+1)\cdot\widetilde{k}
,
in each
j
-package
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{{\Delta}}=\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta},\ldots,\mu_{\widehat{k},j}^{\Delta}\}
we discard every
\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta}
where
i\neq 0\pmod{a+1}
and we shift the
indices of the surviving elements so that
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{{\Delta}}=\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta},\ldots,\mu_{\widetilde{k},j}^{\Delta}\}.

Clearly, conditions
1
,
2
, and
3
above hold also if we replace
\widehat{k}
by
\widetilde{k}.
We next extract from every discarded
fiber a path of the
B_{\Delta}
-
B_{c^{\Delta}}
-linkage
\mathcal{L}.

This gives rise to
\widetilde{k}\cdot p^{\Delta}
sub-linkages
\mathcal{L}_{i,j},[i,j]\in[\widetilde{k}]\times[p^{\Delta}]
of
\mathcal{L},
each containing
a
paths, such that for every
[i,j]\in[\widetilde{k}]\times[p^{\Delta}],
the graph
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}

is drawn “between” the paths in
\mathcal{L}_{i,j}

and the paths in
\mathcal{L}_{i+1,j},
where we agree that

\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{k}+1,j}=\mathcal{L}_{1,j+1},
for
j\in[p^{\Delta}],
and
\mathcal{L}_{1,p^{\Delta}+1}=\mathcal{L}_{1,1}
(see
Figure 23
).
Furthermore, we may assume that the the linkages
\mathcal{L}_{i,j},[i,j]\in[\widetilde{k}]\times[p^{\Delta}]
are orthogonal to the cycles of
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta}
(up to possibly obtaining a pruning of
(G,\delta,W)
).
Observe also that, because of Condition 2,
for each
[i,j]\in[\widetilde{k}]\times[p^{\Delta}]

there is an expansion
(M_{i,j},T_{i,j})

of
Z
such that
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
is a subgraph of
M_{i,j},

T_{i,j}^{\Delta}\subseteq T_{i,j},

|T_{i,j}^{\Delta}|\leq|T_{i,j}|\leq{a},
and
M_{i,j}\cap A^{\Delta}=M_{i,j}^{\Delta}\cap A^{\Delta}.

’s as well, so that, for each

Given the statement of

, the above rerouting

:

Let

For

\overline{\mu}_{i,j}=(\overline{M}_{i,j}^{\Delta},\overline{T}_{i,j}^{\Delta},\overline{\Lambda}_{i,j}^{\Delta}),
where

\overline{M}_{i,j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq M_{i,j}^{\Delta}\cap D^{\Delta}

\overline{T}_{i,j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq T_{i,j}^{\Delta}\cap D^{\Delta}

and

In

the vertices of
\overline{\Lambda}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\overline{\Lambda}_{1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\overline{\Lambda}_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}

are depicted in blue ).

We have now arrived to the construction of the
j
-packages

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Delta}_{j}\coloneqq\{\overline{\mu}_{1,j}^{\Delta},\ldots,\overline{\mu}_{\widetilde{k},j}^{\Delta}\}
that are close to what we need.
A first difference is that the concatenation of the boundaries of the fibers in these
j
-packages are
\overline{\Lambda}^{\Delta}
and not

(denoted be the square orange vertices in

).
Two more differences are that the

\overline{\mu}_{i,j}^{\Delta}
’s in each

-package
are not necessarily prime and they are not necessarily pairwise equivalent. We first discard fibers so to enforce the pairwise equivalence.
For this observe first that as each
\overline{\mu}_{i,j}
has at most

{\sf h}(h)
terminals and at most
a
boundary vertices,
there are
\gamma(a)
pairwise non-equivalent graphs in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Delta}_{j}.
As
\widetilde{k}=\gamma(a)\cdot\breve{k},
we may find
\breve{k}
-paiwise equivalent
fibers in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Delta}_{j}
and update

\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Delta}_{j}
by discarding all the rest.

For every
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}

and every
j\in[p^{\Delta}],
we define
\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\overline{\mu}_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}_{1,j}
and

\overline{\text{\bf\mu}}=\{\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\}
and we claim that

\overline{\text{\bf\mu}}
has a
Z
-certifying complement

\overline{\textbf{\bf C}}
where
d1(\textbf{\bf C})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every
d\in\mathbb{N}.
Indeed this assignment is now build if in
\textbf{\bf C}=({\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},{M}^{\mathsf{c}},{T}^{\mathsf{c}},{\delta}^{\mathsf{c}},Y)

we update

•

{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}\coloneqq{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}\cup\bigcup_{\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}}A^{\Delta},

•

•

\delta^{\mathsf{c}}=(\Gamma\cap{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},\{\Delta_{c}\in\mathcal{D}\mid\Delta_{c}\subseteq{\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}}\})\cap M^{\mathsf{c}}
and

•

Y=\{\overline{y}^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}\}

where, for
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime},

\overline{y}^{\Delta}
is the first vertex of
\overline{\Lambda}^{\Delta}.

Clearly, the boundary of each of the fibers in
\overline{\text{\bf\mu}}
has at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h)
vertices,
|\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|),
and the detail of

is not bigger than the one of
\text{\bf\mu},
that is at most
{\sf h}(h).
Finally, as no vortex of
\delta
has been moved in the new

we also have that
d1(\overline{\textbf{\bf C}})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every

We next force the remaining missing property of the
j
-packages, that is primality, again to the cost of sacrificing
some of the fibers in each
j
-package
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Delta}_{j}.
For this, we will substitute each
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Delta}_{j}

by many others each one containing
k
prime fibers.
For every
(i,j)\in[\breve{k}]\times[p^{\Delta}]
we define
\langle H_{i,j},\widehat{\Lambda}_{i,j}\rangle={\sf dissolve}({\overline{\mu}_{i,j}^{\Delta}}),
and let

\mathsf{dec}({H_{i,j},\widehat{\Lambda}_{i,j}})\coloneqq\langle\langle H^{1}_{i,j},\widehat{\Lambda}^{1}_{i,j}\rangle,\ldots,\langle H^{\ell_{j}}_{i,j},\widehat{\Lambda}^{\ell_{j}}_{i,j}\rangle\rangle.

We next partition, for each
(i,j)\in[\breve{k}]\times[p^{\Delta}],
the fiber
\overline{\mu}_{i,j}^{\Delta}
into
\ell_{j}
fibers
\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta,1},\ldots,\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta,\ell_{j}}

so that, for
l\in[\ell_{j}],

{\sf dissolve}(\mu_{i,j}^{\Delta,l})=\langle H^{l}_{i,j},\widehat{\Lambda}^{l}_{i,j}\rangle.

As, for every
j\in[p^{j}],
all fibers in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\Delta}_{j}
are pairwise isomorphic, it follows that
for every
j\in[p^{\Delta}]
and every
l\in[\ell_{j}]
all fibers
in
\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta,l},\ldots,\mu_{\breve{k},j}^{\Delta,l}\}
are also pairwise isomorphic. Also the fibers corresponding to the
j
-package
\overline{M}_{j}^{\Delta}
are in order:

\displaystyle{\mu}_{1,j}^{{\Delta},1},\ldots,{\mu}_{1,j}^{{\Delta},\ell_{j}},\ \ \ldots\ \ ,{\mu}_{{i},j}^{{\Delta},1}\ldots,{\mu}_{{i},j}^{{\Delta},\ell_{j}},\ \ \ldots\ \ ,{\mu}_{\breve{k},j}^{{\Delta},1},\ldots,{\mu}_{\breve{k},j}^{{\Delta},\ell_{j}}

(18)

We apply the following procedure on the sequence in (
18
):

For
l\coloneqq 1,\ldots,\ell_{j},
we consider the first
k

fibers of the set
\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta,l},\ldots,\mu_{\widehat{k},j}^{\Delta,l}\}
that appear in (
18
) and up to the position where
the
k
-th such fiber appears, discard all elements appearing before it that do not belong in
\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta,l},\ldots,\mu_{\breve{k},j}^{\Delta,l}\}.
In the end of this repetition, discard all remaining elements that have not be considered.

We index the sequence obtained by the above procedure as follows:

\displaystyle{\mu}_{1,j}^{{\Delta},1},\ldots,{\mu}_{k,j}^{{\Delta},1},\ \ \ldots\ \ ,{\mu}_{{1},j}^{{\Delta},l}\ldots,{\mu}_{{k},j}^{{\Delta},l},\ \ \ldots\ \ ,{\mu}_{{1},j}^{{\Delta},\ell_{j}},\ldots,{\mu}_{{k},j}^{{\Delta},\ell_{j}}

(19)

Recall that
\ell_{j}\leq a
and
\breve{k}=a\cdot k,
therefore

\ell_{j}\cdot k\leq\breve{k}.
This implies that for each
l\in[\ell_{j}],
the set
\{\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta,l},\ldots,\mu_{\widehat{k},j}^{\Delta,l}\}
has enough elements so to produce the sequence in (
19
). Moreover, for every
l\in[\ell],
all fibers
in
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\Delta,l}=\{{\mu}_{{1},j}^{{\Delta},l},\ldots,{\mu}_{{k},j}^{{\Delta},l}\}
are pairwise isomorphic. By applying the above construction for every

j=1,\ldots,p^{\Delta},
we produce a sequence
This gives rise to the following sequence of
\ell^{\Delta}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in[p^{\Delta}]}\ell_{j}
packages.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\Delta,1},\ldots,\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\Delta,\ell_{1}},\ \ \ldots\ \ ,\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\Delta,1},\ldots,\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\Delta,\ell_{l}},\ \ \ldots\ \ ,\mathcal{M}_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta,1},\ldots,\mathcal{M}_{p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta,\ell_{l}}

(20)

and each of them contains
k
pairwise isomorphic prime fibers.

Notice that if, after the above rearrangement, we consider the concatenation of the first fibers of each of the packages in (
20
) we again obtain the same fiber
\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}.
This implies that the newly defined sequence of packages in (
20
) along with
\overline{\textbf{\bf C}}
and
\overline{\text{\bf\mu}})
satisfy all conditions of the conclusion of the lemma, with the difference that, now
the boundary of each
\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}
is
\overline{\Lambda}^{\Delta}
and not
\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta}.

In order to deal with this, we recall first that for every
\overline{y}\in V(\overline{\Lambda}^{\Delta})

there is some
y\in V(\widehat{\Lambda}^{\Delta})

such that
y
and
\overline{y}
are the endpoints of
some subpath
P_{\overline{y}}
of
{\mathcal{R}}^{\Delta}.

Next, we enhance each fiber
(M^{\Delta},T^{\Delta},\Lambda^{\Delta})
in the packages of
(
20
) as follows

•

we set

M^{\Delta}=M^{\Delta}\cup\bigcup_{\overline{y}\in V(\overline{\Lambda}^{\Delta})}P_{\overline{y}},

•

T^{\Delta}
remains the same. Notice that for the current
\overline{\textbf{\bf C}}
and
\overline{\text{\bf\mu}},
every vertex
of
\overline{\mu}^{\Delta}
is a non-terminal vertex (i.e., a subdivision vertex) of the graph of the
(\overline{\textbf{\bf C}},\overline{\text{\bf\mu}})
-certifying extension of
Z,

•

given that
\Lambda^{\Delta}=\langle\overline{y}^{1},\ldots,\overline{y}^{r}\rangle,
we set
\Lambda^{\Delta}=\langle{y}^{1},\ldots,{y}^{r}\rangle.

:
A visualization of the “upper part” of the fibers in the packages in (

In other words, we use the paths in the linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\Delta}

In order to root the boundary of every fiber in the packages of (

) to vertices of

starting with the vertex

(corresponding to

All the transformations of this proof can be implemented in linear time. Therefore the running time of the algorithm is dominated by one of the applications of the algorithm of
Lemma 8.2
plus
\mathcal{O}(t)
and that runs in
\mathcal{O}_{h}(\widehat{k}\cdot(s{+p})\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3})
-time. As
\widehat{k}=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k),
we obtain the claimed running time.
∎

Notice that in the statement of
Lemma 8.4
we do not demand that each
M_{i,j}^{\Delta}
has some vertex drawn in
\Delta_{c^{\Delta}},
as we did in
Lemma 8.1
or in
Lemma 8.2
. This extra feature in
Lemma 8.2
was necessary for the proof of
Lemma 8.4
as it permitted
us to detect the sub-linkages
\mathcal{L}_{i,j},[i,j]\in[\widetilde{k}]\times[p^{\Delta}]
of
\mathcal{L}
and then apply the combing
Lemma 8.3
in other to reroute the fibers produced by
Lemma 8.2
.
For the use we make of
Lemma 8.4
in the next section, this additional feature is not necessary anymore.
However, we could also further discard all fibers extracted by
Lemma 8.4
that do not invade their corresponding vortices and update
\overline{\textbf{\bf C}}
by enhancing it with the “flat” drawings of the discarded fibers as we did in
Lemma 8.1
. For instance, this extra modification would discard the rightmost package in
Figure 26
that contains fibers that have flat embeddings away from the vortex (depicted by the red territory). In the visualization of the flower of
Figure 27
we assume that this rightmost package has been cropped.

9
The local structure theorem

This section is the first key step in unifying most of the previously introduced machinery into a single statement: the local structure theorem.
This theorem takes as input simply a large wall
W
and integers
k
and
h
and outputs either a large clique-minor which is grasped by
W,
or produces the entire orchard infrastructure necessary for the application of
Lemma 8.4
together with a small set
S
and a list of walloids representing all graphs of size at most
h
which are not fully controlled by
S.

9.1
Finding the universal obstruction

To make the outcome of
Lemma 8.4
more accessible, we start with an intermediate statement that directly extracts our obstructions from the orchard and its thickets whenever the set
S
misses a graph
Z
on at most
h
vertices.

Lemma 9.1
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}},f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N},

f^{3}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N},
and an algorithm that, given two positive integers
k
and
h
and a
(z,p)
-ripe and
h
-blooming
(t,k,{\sf h}(h),s,\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W)
as input, where

\displaystyle s\coloneqq f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h)\text{ and }t\coloneqq f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),

outputs a set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
f^{3}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(z,p,k,h)
such that for every graph
Z
on at most
h
vertices, either

1.

G-S
contains no
\delta
-bound extension of
Z,
or

2.

there exists
\mathscr{W}^{Z}=\langle\mathscr{W}^{Z}_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\rangle\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathsf{excl}(Z)}
such that
G
contains a subdivision
W^{Z}_{k}
of
\mathscr{W}^{Z}_{k}
and the base cylinder of
W^{Z}_{k}
is a subgraph of the base cylinder of
W,

in time

\mathcal{O}_{h}(k\cdot(k^{2}+p)\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3}).

Moreover

f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h)=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k^{2}),

f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h)=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k^{3}),

and

f^{3}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(z,p,k,h)=\mathcal{O}_{h}(zk(k^{2}+p)).

Figure 27
:
A visualization of the thicket in the proof of
Lemma 9.1
and the position of the flower and the rainbow in it.

Proof.

Let
t^{\prime}=f^{2}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,{\sf h}(h))\cdot k,

\overline{t}\coloneqq t^{\prime}+2k,

s^{\prime}=f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,{\sf h}(h))
and
\overline{s}\coloneqq s^{\prime}+k.

We prove the lemma for

•

f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h)\coloneqq f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,{\sf h}(h))+k,

•

f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h)\coloneqq f^{2}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,{\sf h}(h))\cdot k,

and

•

f^{3}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(z,p,k,h)\coloneqq f^{3}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(z,p,k,{\sf h}(h)).

Consider a
(q,p)
-ripe and
h
-blooming
(\overline{t},k,{\sf h}(h),\overline{s},\Sigma)
-orchard
(G,\delta,W).
Let also
W^{-}
be the walloid obtained if we first remove
the
k
cycles around the exceptional face plus, for each thicket, the parts that are indicated by the two bold blue cycles and the purple edges in
Figure 27
. We also use
\mathcal{L}^{*}
for the linkage corresponding to the purple edges.
This gives rise to a
(z,p)
-ripe and

h
-blooming
({t}^{\prime},k,{\sf h}(h),s^{\prime},\Sigma)
-orchard
(D,\delta,W^{-}).

It also gives us space to define a linkage of
k
-paths “above” each
of the thickets of
(G,\delta,W).
In
Figure 27
, this linkage is visualized as the set of bold green paths joining the orange vertices and we refer to it as the
k
-rainbow
of this thicket.

We next apply
Lemma 8.4

on
(G,\delta,W^{-})
for
k\coloneqq k,

s\coloneqq s^{\prime},

t\coloneqq t^{\prime},

h={\sf h}(h),
and find a
set
S,
where
|S|\leq f^{3}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(z,p,k,{\sf h}(h))={\mathcal{O}_{h}(zk(k^{2}+p))}
and such that, for every
\delta
-bound extension of
Z
in
G-S
there is some subset
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
of the disk collection
\mathbf{\Delta}
of
(G,\delta,W)

and a set
\text{\bf\mu}\coloneqq\{\mu^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta^{\prime}}\}
of fibers so that
conditions
1
,
2
, and
3
of
Lemma 8.4
are satisfied. Let also
\mu^{\Delta}=\mu_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\ldots,\oplus\mu_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta},
where, for
j\in[p^{\Delta}],

\mu_{1,j}^{\Delta}\coloneqq(M_{1,j}^{\Delta},T_{1,j}^{\Delta},\Lambda_{1,j}^{\Delta}).

Also, again from
Lemma 8.4
,
S
can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}_{h}(k\cdot(s^{\prime}+p)\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3})
-time.
As
s^{\prime}=f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(z,p,k,h)
and
f^{1}_{\ref{rooted_certification_into_flower}}(k,h)=\mathcal{O}_{h}(k^{2}),
the algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}_{h}(k\cdot(k^{2}+p)\cdot z\cdot|G|^{3}),
as claimed.

Let now
C
be a
\Sigma
-flat
Z
-certifying complement

\textbf{\bf C}=({\Sigma}^{\mathsf{c}},{M}^{\mathsf{c}},{T}^{\mathsf{c}},{\delta}^{\mathsf{c}},Y)
where
d1(\textbf{\bf C})\subseteq d1(\delta),
for every
d\in\mathbb{N},
because of by Condition
2
. Let also
(Q,T)
be
the
(\textbf{\bf C},\text{\bf\mu})
-certifying extension of
Z.
We define

M={\sf dissolve}(Q,T\cup\bigcup_{\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta^{\prime}}}B_{\Delta}\cap V(Q))

and keep in mind that
Z
is a minor of
dissolve
(Q,T)
that, in turn, is a minor of
M.
Moreover, because of Condition
3
, the boundary of each of the fibers in
μ
has at most
f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h)
vertices,
|\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}|\leq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(|Z|),
and
|T|={\sf h}(h).

Therefore, we are now in the position to define the function
\mathsf{ext}(h)\coloneqq f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{2}(h)\cdot f_{\ref{my_crops_findslo}}^{3}(h)+\mathsf{h}(h),
introduced in
Subsection 1.5
, so that
|M|\leq\mathsf{ext}(h).

We call a cell
c\in\mathcal{C}(\delta^{\mathsf{c}})

trivial

if
\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c)
is the graph with one edge joining the two vertices in

\pi_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(\widetilde{c}).
We denote by
\mathcal{C}^{*}(\delta^{\mathsf{c}})

the set of non-trivial cells in
\delta^{\mathsf{c}}(c).

We now build a subgraph partition of
M
as follows.

\mathcal{P}=\{\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c)\mid c\in\mathcal{C}^{*}(\delta^{\mathsf{c}})\}\cup\{M_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta^{\prime}}\}.

Consider now the hypergraph
K_{\mathcal{P}}

and observe that
\delta^{\mathsf{c}}
and
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}
define a
\Sigma
-embedding
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
where
every edge
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
is embedded as a disk
\Delta_{e}.

Given the triple
(M,\mathcal{P},\Gamma),
we also define the function

\eta:\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}\to V(M)
as follows: let
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}.

If
e=\pi_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(\widetilde{c})
for some
c\in\mathcal{C}^{*}(\delta^{\mathsf{c}}),
then
|e|\leq 3
and we define
\eta(e)
to be an, arbitrarily chosen, vertex of
e.

If
e=V(\Lambda_{1}^{\Delta}),
for some
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime},

then we define
\eta(e)
to be the first vertex of
\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)}=\Lambda_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}.
This generates the set of linear societies

\mathbf{B}=\{\langle G_{e},\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)}\rangle\mid e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}\text{\penalty\ and $(G_{e},\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)})$ is not disk-embeddable}\},

and therefore
{\bf p}\coloneqq(\Sigma,\mathbf{B})
is an embedding pair.
Let
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}=\langle\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the guest walloid generated by
{\bf p}.

It remains to prove that a subdivision of
\langle\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
can be found as a subgraph of
G.

We examin two cases:

Case 1.

Suppose that
e=\pi_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c),
for some
c\in\mathcal{C}^{*}(\delta^{\mathsf{c}}),
where
\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c)
is
\Delta_{c}
-embeddable so that the vertices in
\pi_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(\widetilde{c})
are drawn in the boundary of
\Delta_{c}.
This means that the society
\langle\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c),\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)}\rangle

is not disk embeddable and belongs in
\mathbf{B}
and also belongs in
{\sf h}(h)1(\textbf{\bf C}).
From Condition
2

of
Lemma 8.4
we have that

\langle\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c),\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)}\rangle\in{\sf h}(h)1(\delta),
therefore
W^{-},
anf therefore also
W,
contains a
(t^{\prime},k,{\sf h}(h),\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c),\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)})
-parterre segment. Notice that societies isomorphic to
\langle\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c),\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)}\rangle

can appear at most
|\mathcal{C}^{*}(\delta^{\mathsf{c}})|
times
and, because
|\mathcal{C}^{*}(\delta^{\mathsf{c}})|\leq|T|\leq{\sf h}(h),

the aforementioned
(t^{\prime},k,{\sf h}(h),\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c),\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)})
-parterre segment contains enough many

(t^{\prime},k,\sigma_{\delta^{\mathsf{c}}}(c),\Lambda_{e,\eta(e)})
-flower segments to represent all of them.
We just proved that all flowers of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{k}
generated by cells of
\delta^{\mathsf{c}}
are present in
W.

Case 2.

Let now
e=V(\Lambda^{\Delta})
for some
\Delta\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\prime}.
Condition
1
of
Lemma 8.4
implies that the thicket of

(G,\delta,W)
corresponding to
\Delta
also contains a

(t^{\prime},k,M_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta},\Lambda_{1,1}^{\Delta}\oplus\cdots\oplus\Lambda_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta})
-flower segment. For this, we may just see the graphs in
M_{1,1}^{\Delta},\ldots,M_{1,p^{\Delta}}^{\Delta}
below the
k
-rainbow of the thicket and bring back the edges of the paths in the linkage that we initially discarded from
W,
for every thicket
\mathcal{L}^{*}
(denoted in purple in
Figure 27
).

As all
k
-flowers
of
W^{\mathbf{p}_{k}}
can be found either in the

-parterres of

(G,\delta,W)
or in the thickets of
(G,\delta,W),
we conclude
that

indeed contains a subdivision of

as required.
∎

9.2
Bringing everything together

With
Lemma 9.1
at hand, we are finally ready to prove the main result of this section and the most central statement of this paper.

Theorem 9.2

(Local structure theorem)
.

There exist functions
f^{1}_{\ref{thm_local_structure}},f^{2}_{\ref{thm_local_structure}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given two integers
k
and
h,
a graph
G
and an
f^{1}_{\ref{thm_local_structure}}(h,k)
-wall
W
in
G
finds, in time
\mathcal{O}_{h,k}(|G|^{3})
either

1.

a
K_{3k^{2}+k\cdot h}
minor grasped by
W,

2.

there are non-negative integers
h^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}\leq 2
such that every graph on at most
h
vertices can be embedded in every surface of Euler-genus at least
2h^{\prime}+c^{\prime}
and
G
contains a subdivision of a walloid
W^{\prime}
which is the cylindrical concatenation of
h^{\prime}
many
k
-handle segments and
c^{\prime}
many
k
-crosscap segments such that the base cylinder of
W^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
W,
or

3.

a set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
f^{2}_{\ref{thm_local_structure}}(h,k)
and a list of graphs
\mathcal{Z},
all of which are of size at most
h,
such that, if
J
is the unique component of
G-S
which contains a full row and a full column of
W,

(a)

for every graph
Z
on at most
h
vertices, if
Z\notin\mathcal{Z}
then
J
is
Z
-minor-free, and

(b)

for every graph
Z\in\mathcal{Z}
there exists an embedding pair
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)}
such that the algorithm outputs a subdivision
W_{Z}
of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{k}
of
G
where the base cylinder of
W_{Z}
is a subgraph of
W.

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{thm_local_structure}}(k,h)

\displaystyle\in\penalty\ 2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}\text{ and }f^{2}_{\ref{thm_local_structure}}(k,h)\in\penalty\ 2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}.

Proof.

Let us start by discussing the numbers.
We set
g\coloneqq g(h)
to be the smallest integer
g
such that every graph on at most
h
vertices embeds in
every
surface of Euler-genus at least
g.

Observe that, since there are, up to isomorphism, only finitely many graphs on at most
h
vertices, and thus
g
is well-defined.
Indeed, it holds that
g\in\mathsf{poly}(h).

Similarly, we know that for every extension of a graph on at most
h
vertices it holds that the graph of the extension has at most
\mathsf{h}\coloneqq\mathsf{h}(h)\in\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
terminals.
Hence, it will suffice to only consider folios of detail
\mathsf{h}.

We then set

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{thm_local_structure}}(h,k)\coloneqq\penalty\

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}}\bigg(h,k,f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}\bigg(f_{\ref{lem_garden_creation}}\big(f^{1}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})

\displaystyle+f^{2}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})+1,k,h,\mathsf{h}\big),h,g\bigg)\bigg)

and

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\ref{thm_local_structure}}(h,k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq f^{2}_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}}(h,k,k)+f^{3}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(q,p,k,h).

The values for
x,y,q,
and
p
are bounded in functions purely depending on
k
and
h
as explained below.

As a first step, we want to get the first two outcomes of the assertion out of the way and create a meadow for further refinement.
Calling upon
Observation 4.1
yields either

1.

a
(3k^{2}+k\cdot h)
-minor grasped by
W,

2.

a “
k
-Dyck-walloid
20
20

20

That is a walloid which is the cylindrical concatenation of only
k
-handle and
k
-crosscap segments.
” representing a surface of Euler-genus at least
g
whose base cylinder is a subgraph of
W,
or

3.

a set
S_{1}\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
f^{2}_{\ref{obs_create_meadow}}(h,k,k)
and an
(x,y)
-fertile
\big(z_{1},\Sigma\big)
-meadow
(G_{1},\delta_{1},W_{1})
where
\Sigma
is of Euler-genus less than
g
where

Here we have

\displaystyle z_{1}=\penalty\ f_{\ref{lem_meadow_plowing}}\big(

\displaystyle f_{\ref{lem_garden_creation}}(f^{1}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})

\displaystyle+f^{2}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})+1,k,h,\mathsf{h}),\mathsf{h},g\big).

In the first and second cases, we are immediately done.
In the third case, we obtain the desired meadow and the following bounds on
x
and
y
:

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{O}(k^{4}\cdot h)\text{ and}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\in 2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{h}(1)}}.

Next, we want to plow our meadow.
By using
Lemma 4.3
we obtain an
(x,y)
-fertile
\big(z_{2},\Sigma\big)
-meadow
(G_{1},\delta_{2},W_{2})
which is, in addition,
\mathsf{h}
-plowed.
Moreover,

\displaystyle z_{2}=\penalty\ f_{\ref{lem_garden_creation}}\big(

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})

\displaystyle+f^{2}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})+1,k,h,\mathsf{h}\big).

Once we have our plowed meadow, it is time for proper domestication.
We will turn the meadow into a garden using
Lemma 5.4
.
This returns an
(x,y)
-fertile
\big(z_{3},k,h,\Sigma\big)
-garden
(G_{1},\delta_{3},W_{3})
which is
\mathsf{h}
-blooming.
Here it holds that

\displaystyle z_{3}=f^{1}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})+f^{2}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})+1

From here we want to proceed and create a ripe orchard.
By using

we may assume that
(G_{1},\delta_{3},W_{3})
is in fact an
(x,y)
-fertile and
\mathsf{h}

We then apply

to obtain a

-ripe and
\mathsf{h}
-blooming
\big(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),\Sigma\big)
-orchard

Here we have

\displaystyle z_{4,1}

\displaystyle=f^{1}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h}),

\displaystyle z_{4,2}

\displaystyle=f^{2}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h}),

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\leq f^{3}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h})\text{, and}

\displaystyle\leq f^{4}_{\ref{ripe_orchard}}(f^{2}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),k,h,f^{1}_{\ref{lemma_grounded_extensions_to_obstructions}}(k,h),x,y,\mathsf{h}).

We now make use of

.
This produces a set

Notice that the algorithm from

partitions the set of all graphs on at most

10
Extensions of graphs in their guest walloids

This section is dedicated to the proof of the following theorem which is of crucial importance in our quest of proving Erdős-Pósa dualities.
The results of these sections will be used in the proof of
Lemma 11.7
in order to show that the walloids we consider contain large half-integral brambles.

Theorem 10.1
.

For every graph
Z,
there exists a function
f^{(Z)}_{\ref{lemma_walloid_contains_obstruction}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for every embedding pair
\mathbf{p}
of
\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)},

Z
is a minor of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{f^{(Z)}_{\ref{lemma_walloid_contains_obstruction}}(|Z|)}.

Moreover

f^{(Z)}_{\ref{lemma_walloid_contains_obstruction}}(|Z|)=2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell(\mathsf{h}(|Z|)))}.

This theorem can be seen as an extension of the fact (see
[
68
]
) that every planar graph
Z
is a minor of some large enough grid, whose size only depends on the size of
Z.

This result has been further extended by Gavoille and Hilaire
[
25
]

for the Dyck-walloids
21
21

21

See
Theorem 9.2
for an explanation of this term.
that are the
\mathscr{W}^{\bf p}
’s corresponding to embedding pairs of the form
\mathbf{p}=(\Sigma,\emptyset).

Theorem 10.1
is an extension of these results to every embedding pair.

10.1
Designs of embedding pairs

In this subsection, we introduce the notion of a
design
of embedding pairs.
This notion, while similar to that of a walloid (they are in fact equivalent as parametric graphs), its definition will prove more beneficial towards showing
Theorem 10.1
.

Filaments.

Let
z,t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}
and consider a
(z,t)
-wall
W.

Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{t}
and
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{z}
be the horizontal and vertical paths of
W
respectively.
For every
i\in[t-1]
and
j\in[z-1],
let
B^{j}_{i}
denote the brick of
W
such that
B^{j}_{i}\cap P_{i}\neq\emptyset,

B^{j}_{i}\cap P_{i+1}\neq\emptyset,

B^{j}_{i}\cap Q_{j}\neq\emptyset,
and
B^{j}_{i}\cap Q_{j+1}\neq\emptyset.

Also, let
Z^{j}_{i}
be the path
P^{i+1}\cap Q_{i+1}.

Now, given an
h\in\mathbb{N}
and a choice of
i\in[t-1]
and
j\in[z-1],
we say that the graph
B^{j}_{i}\cup\mathcal{P}
is an
h
-filament
of
W\cup{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{P}
if
\mathcal{P}
is a set of
h
many pairwise disjoint
Z^{j}_{i}
-
P_{t}
-paths that are orthogonal to the horizontal paths of
W
and are only allowed to intersect the paths
P_{i^{\prime}}\cap Q_{j+1},
for every
i^{\prime}\in[i+1,t].

We also refer to the endpoints of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
in
Z^{j}_{i}
as the
boundary
vertices of the
h
-filament.
We refer to the set
\mathcal{P}
as the
paths
of the filament.

Frames.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2},

f\in\mathbb{N}
and consider an
((f+1)2t,2t)
-wall
W.

Let
\mathbf{P}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{f}\}
be a set of sets of paths and
I\coloneqq\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{f}\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}.

Moreover, let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq t/2.

We say that the graph
W\cup{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{P}
is a
(t,I)
-frame
if for every
i\in[f],
the graph
F_{i}\coloneqq B^{it^{\prime}}_{t^{\prime}}\cup{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathcal{P}_{i}
is an
h_{i}
-filament of
W\cup{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{P}.

We refer to the set
\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{f}\}
as the
set of filaments
of
W\cup{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}\mathbf{P}.

Designs of embedding pairs.

Let
Z
be a graph and
\mathbf{p}\coloneqq(\Sigma,\mathbf{B})
be an embedding pair of
\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)}.

We define the
(t,\mathbf{p})
-design
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}
to be a parametric graph
\langle\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t}\rangle_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}}
as follows.
Let
b\coloneqq|\mathbf{B}|,

\mathbf{B}=\{\langle Z_{1},\Lambda_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle Z_{b},\Lambda_{b}\rangle\}
and
f\coloneqq 2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+b.

For every
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2},

\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t}
is obtained from a
(t,I)
-frame
F,
where
I=\langle h_{1},\ldots,h_{f}\rangle,
as follows.

•

For
i\in[2\mathsf{h}],

h_{i}\coloneqq t,

•

for
i\in[2\mathsf{h}+1,2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}],

h_{i}\coloneqq 2t,
and

•

for
i\in[2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+1,f],

h_{i}\coloneqq|\Lambda_{i}|t.

Moreover, for the
i
-th
h_{i}
-filament of
F,
let
u^{i}_{1},\ldots,u^{i}_{h_{i}}
be its boundary vertices in left to right order. Then

•

for odd
i\in[2\mathsf{h}],
connect
u^{i}_{j}
with
u^{i+1}_{j},
for every
j\in[h_{i}],

•

for
i\in[2\mathsf{h}+1,2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}],
connect
u^{i}_{j}
with
u^{i}_{t^{\prime}+j},
for every
j\in[t^{\prime}],
and

•

for
i\in[2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+1,f],
let
\mathsf{dec}(Z_{i},\Lambda_{i})=\langle\langle Z^{i}_{1},\Lambda^{i}_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle Z^{i}_{q_{i}},\Lambda^{i}_{q_{i}}\rangle\rangle.

Then, for every
p\in[q_{i}],
we take
t
many disjoint copies of
\langle Z^{i}_{p},\Lambda^{i}_{p}\rangle
and connect, for every

the
l
-th vertex of the

-th copy of
\Lambda^{i}_{p}
with

where

The following observation follows easily from the definition of designs.

Observation 10.2
.

Let
Z
be a graph and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)}
be an embedding pair of
Z.

Then, for every
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2},

\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t}
is a minor of
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{2t}.

10.2
Designs as minors of walloids

In this subsection, we demonstrate how for a given embedding pair of some graph, we can find a large design of it as a minor of a large enough instance of its corresponding walloid.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
Z
be a graph and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)}
be an embedding pair of
Z.

Then, for every
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2},

\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t}
is a minor of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{p}=(\Sigma,\mathbf{B}),
let
\mathsf{h}
and
\mathsf{c}
be such that
\Sigma=\Sigma^{(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c})},
and assume that
\langle\langle Z_{1},\Lambda_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle Z_{q},\Lambda_{q}\rangle\rangle
are the linear societies contained in
\mathbf{B}
arranged in the order they appear in
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}},

\langle Z_{1},\Lambda_{1}\rangle
being the linear society of the leftmost flower segment of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}},
while
\langle Z_{q},\Lambda_{q}\rangle
being the linear society of the rightmost flower segment of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}.

We describe how to find
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t}
as a minor of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t}.

a
b
c
d
a^{\prime}
b^{\prime}
c^{\prime}
d^{\prime}

Figure 28
:
Finding two
t
-filaments that represent a handle in a
5t
-handle segment in the proof of
Lemma 10.3
.

Defining the underlying wall.

We define two sets of
t
pairwise disjoint paths, say
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime},
which are subgraphs of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t}
as follows.
We define
\mathcal{P}
as the paths starting from the leftmost boundary vertices of the first handle segment of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{4t},
ending at the rightmost boundary vertices of the last flower segment of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t},
where all such endpoints are vertices of the first
t
cycles of the base cylinder of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t},
with the additional property that whenever these paths intersect a handle, crosscap, or flower segment, they use the edges of their corresponding rainbows as depicted in
Figure 28
,
Figure 29
, and
Figure 30
respectively.
Moreover, we define
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
as the subpaths of the last
t
cycles of the base cylinder of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t}
starting from the leftmost boundary vertices of the first handle segment of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t},
ending at the rightmost boundary vertices of the last flower segment of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t},
intersecting all segments of the walloid.
The paths in
\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
will serve as the horizontal paths of the underlying wall of
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t}.

We proceed with the definition of multiple sets of pairwise disjoint paths, depending on the three different types of segments present in
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t}.

Let
i\in\mathsf{h}
and let

be the

-th handle segment of

We define three sets, say
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{j},

of paths that are vertical paths of

as follows.

\mathcal{H}_{i}^{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{3}
) contains the first (resp. last)
t
many vertical paths of
W
and
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{2}
is the set that contains the
t
many vertical paths of
W
whose topmost endpoints are the last
t
among the leftmost endpoints of the rightmost rainbow of
W,
union the set that contains the
t
many vertical paths of
W
whose topmost endpoints are the first
t
among the rightmost endpoints of the leftmost rainbow of
W.

Now, let
i\in\mathsf{c}.

We define two sets, say
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{j},

j\in[2],
of paths that are vertical paths of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t}

\mathcal{C}_{i}^{1}
(resp.

) contains the first (resp. last)

Finally, let

\mathcal{F}_{i}^{j},

of paths that are vertical paths of

\mathcal{F}_{i}^{1}
(resp.

) contains the first (resp. last)

many vertical paths of the

Clearly, the graph

U\coloneqq{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\cup\bigcup_{i\in[\mathsf{h}]}(\mathcal{H}_{i}^{1}\cup\mathcal{H}_{i}^{2}\cup\mathcal{H}_{i}^{3})\cup\bigcup_{i\in[\mathsf{c}]}(\mathcal{C}_{i}^{1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{i}^{2})\cup\bigcup_{i\in[f]}(\mathcal{F}_{i}^{1}\cup\mathcal{F}_{i}^{2}))

is an
((f+1)2t,2t)
-wall, where we define

U
will serve as the underlying wall of the frame we will define for the design we’re looking for.

The previous lemma combined with

\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)}

\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}\approx\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}.

10.3
Graphs as minors of their designs

In this subsection, we demonstrate how for a given embedding pair of some graph
Z,
we can find
Z
as a minor of a large enough instance of the design corresponding to its embedding pair, whose size only depends on the size of
Z.

Lemma 10.4
.

For every graph
Z,
there exists a function
f^{(Z)}_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for every embedding pair
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)},

Z
is a minor of
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{f^{(Z)}_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)}.

Moreover

f^{(Z)}_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)=2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell(\mathsf{h}(|Z|)))}.

Proof.

We define
f^{(Z)}_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)\coloneqq\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{ext}(|Z|)^{4}).

Let
\mathbf{p}=(\Sigma,\mathbf{B})
and assume that
\mathbf{p}
is generated by the triple
(M,\mathcal{P},\Gamma)
where,
M
is a connected graph that contains
Z
as a minor, where
|M|\leq\mathsf{ext}(|Z|),

\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}(M),
and
\Gamma
is a
\Sigma
-embedding of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}.

Let
\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\Sigma=\Sigma^{(\mathsf{h},\mathsf{c})}.

We treat hyperedges of order two in
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
as regular edges of a graph and whenever we refer to a hyperedge as an edge we mean a hyperedge of order two.
We first obtain a new hypergraph
H
from
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
as follows.

•

For every vertex
u\in V(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}),
let
E_{u}
denote the set of hyperedges that contain
u.

We define a hypergraph
H^{\prime\prime}
from
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}
by replacing
u
by a path
P_{u}
on
|E_{u}|
many fresh vertices, where every vertex of
P_{u}
replaces
u
in exactly one distinct hyperedge in
E_{u}.

Observe that
H^{\prime\prime}
is
\Sigma
-embeddable.
Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a
\Sigma
-embedding of
H^{\prime\prime}
obtained from
\Gamma
in the obvious way.

•

Next, from
H^{\prime\prime}
we define a hypergraph
H^{\prime}
by considering, for every
i\in[\mathsf{h}],
a non-contractible cycle
C_{i}^{h}
cutting through the
i
-th handle of
\Sigma
that intersects
\Gamma^{\prime}
only in edges of
H^{\prime\prime}
and exactly once per edge.
We then subdivide each edge that
C_{i}^{h}
intersects twice and we consider
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{h}
to be the unique set of edges incident to the newly introduced vertices.
Additionally, for every
i\in[\mathsf{c}],
we consider a non-contractible cycle
C_{i}^{c}
cutting through the
i
-th crosscap of
\Sigma,
i.e., a cycle such that one of the two sides is a Moebius strip, that intersects
\Gamma^{\prime}
only in edges of
H^{\prime\prime}
and exactly twice per edge.
We then subdivide each edge that
C_{i}^{c}
intersects twice, drawing a vertex at each point of intersection, and we consider
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{c}
to be the unique set of edges incident to the newly introduced vertices.

•

Finally, for every
i\in[\mathsf{h}]
(resp. every
i\in[\mathsf{c}]
), we define the sets
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
(resp. the set
S_{i}
) such that
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{h}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{c}
) is an
A_{i}
-
B_{i}
(resp.
S_{i}
-
S_{i}
) linkage in
H^{\prime}.

We define
H
from
H^{\prime}
by adding
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
(resp.
S_{i}
) as hyperedges.

Let
\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\in[\mathsf{h}]}\mathcal{T}_{i}^{h}\cup\bigcup_{i\in[\mathsf{c}]}\mathcal{T}_{i}^{c}.

Observe that by construction, hyperedges of order at least three in
H
are pairwise disjoint,
|H|\leq\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{P}}|^{2})=\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{ext}(|Z|)^{2}),
and that the hypergraph
H-\mathcal{T}
is plane-embeddable.
Let

\mathbf{B}^{+}\coloneqq\{\langle A_{1},\Lambda^{A}_{1}\rangle,\langle B_{1},\Lambda^{B}_{1}\rangle\mid i\in[\mathsf{h}]\}\cup\{\langle S_{1},\Lambda^{S}_{1}\rangle\mid i\in[\mathsf{c}]\}\cup\mathbf{B}

which is a set of linear societies each corresponding to a hyperedge of
H
such that, for every
i\in[\mathsf{h}],

V(\Lambda^{A}_{i})=A_{i},

V(\Lambda^{B}_{i})=B_{i},
and the
j
-th vertex of
\Lambda^{A}_{i}
is adjacent (via
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{h}
) the
|\Lambda^{B}_{i}|-j+1
-th vertex
\Lambda^{B}_{i},
while for every
i\in\mathsf{c},

V(\Lambda^{C}_{i})=C_{i}
and the
j
-th vertex of
\Lambda^{C}_{i}
is adjacent (via
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{c}
) to the
j+|\Lambda^{C}_{i}|/2
-th vertex of
\Lambda^{C}_{i}.

Moreover, every hyperedge of
H
that does not correspond to a linear society in
\mathbf{B}^{+}
is of order two.
Moreover, let
b\coloneqq|\mathbf{B}|
and let

\mathbf{B}=\{\langle Z_{i},\Lambda_{i}\rangle\mid i\in[b]\}.

Let
t^{\prime}\coloneqq f^{(Z)}_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|).

We are now in the position to describe how to find
Z
as a minor of the
(t^{\prime},\mathbf{p})
-design
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t^{\prime}}.

and
f\coloneqq|\mathbf{B}^{+}|=2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+b.

Let
F
be the underlying
(t^{\prime},I)
-frame of
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t^{\prime}},
where
I=\langle h_{1},\ldots,h_{f}\rangle.

We can first observe that by construction, for every odd
i\in[2\mathsf{h}],
we can map
\Lambda^{A}_{i}
(resp.
\Lambda^{B}_{i}
) to the boundary vertices of the
i
-th (resp.
i+1
-th) filament of
F
respecting the linear ordering as well as embed the edges in
\mathcal{T}^{h}_{i}
by using the edges between the boundary vertices of the two filaments.
Moreover, for every
i\in[2\mathsf{h}+1,2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+1]
we can map
\Lambda^{C}_{i^{\prime}},
where
i^{\prime}\coloneqq 2\mathsf{h}-i,
to the boundary vertices of the
i
-th filament of
F
respecting the linear ordering as well as embed the edges in
\mathcal{T}^{c}_{i}
using the edges between the boundary vertices of the filament.
Lastly, for every
i\in[2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}+1,f],
we can map
\Lambda_{i^{\prime}},
where
i^{\prime}\coloneqq 2\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c},
to boundary vertices of the
i
-th filament of
F
respecting the linear ordering such that we find
\langle Z_{i^{\prime}},\Lambda_{i^{\prime}}\rangle
as a minor within the
i
-th filament of our design.

It remains to show how to appropriately model the edges of
H
that do not correspond to hyperedges of
\mathbf{B}^{+}.

This part is implied from standard techniques in the following way.
First, we subdivide all such edges twice.
For every
i\in[f],
let
\Lambda
denote the linear society we have associated to the
i
-th filament in the previous step.
We begin by considering a wall
W_{i}
of order
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{ext}(|Z|)^{4})
in our design whose central brick is the brick of the
i
-th filament.
Then, we connect each vertex
v_{i}
of
\Lambda
to a set, say
B_{i},
of boundary vertices of
W_{i}
that correspond to each neighbour of
v_{i}
that was created by the previously subdivided edges.
We do this while making sure that the subgraph of
W_{i}
we have used to make this connection is disjoint from all others and that the vertices in
B_{i}
are pairwise at distance at least
\max\{|B_{i}|\mid v_{i}\in\Lambda\}\in\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{ext}(|Z|)^{2})
(i.e., if they are on the same vertical path of
W_{i}
there should be at least
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{ext}(|Z|)^{2})
horizontal paths between the ones they belong to and symmetrically if they belong on the same horizontal path).
By performing this step we have successfully managed to find a subcubic model for each of the vertices of
\Lambda.

This can be done via standard routing arguments (similarly to
Lemma 5.2
for example).
Now, every edge of
H
we want to model, can be modelled as a
B_{i}
-
B_{j}
-path for distinct
i,j\in[f].

Moreover, the collection of all these paths we are looking form a linkage.
However, this is an instance of the disjoint paths problem in a planar graph as certified by
H-\mathcal{T}
being plane-embeddable.
Hence, again by standard routing arguments, we can show that if we carefully choose the constants hidden in the definition of
f^{(Z)}_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)
we can find the desired paths using the infrastructure of
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{t^{\prime}}
away from the walls
W_{i},

i\in[f]
we have chosen at the previous step.
∎

We are now ready to proceed with the proof of the main theorem.

Proof of
Theorem 10.1
.

We define
f_{\ref{lemma_walloid_contains_obstruction}}(|Z|)\coloneqq 5f^{(Z)}_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|).

Let
z\coloneqq f^{(Z)}_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|).

Then, the proof follows by an application of
Lemma 10.3
, in order to find the
(t,\mathbf{p})
-design
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{z}
as a minor of
\mathscr{W}_{5z}.

We conclude by calling upon
Lemma 10.4
to find
Z
as a minor of
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{z}.

∎

11
Universal obstructions for
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth

In this section, we make a first step in tying together all of the previously established structural theorems to show that
Theorem 9.2
either provides an obstruction to
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth or can be used to produce an object we call a “
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition” of small width.

We first, in
Subsection 11.1
, provide a formal definition of
\mathcal{H}
-tree decompositions and their width and then show that the resulting parameter equals
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}.

Then, in
Subsection 11.2
we provide an extension of the notion of brambles to the setting of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth.
These notions build the foundation for both the lower and the upper bound through our universal obstructions.
The proof of
Theorem 1.4
is then presented in
Subsection 11.3
.

Finally, towards the proof of
Theorem 1.5
in
Subsection 11.4
we provide an argument that shows how
Theorem 9.2
can be used to obtain an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition of small width in the absence of any of our universal obstructions.

11.1
A torso-free definition of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth

Let us begin by finally introducing a definition of tree-decompositions.

Let
G
be a graph.
A
tree decomposition
of
G
is a pair
(T,\beta)
where
T
is a tree and
\beta\colon V(T)\to 2^{V(G)}
such that

•

\bigcup_{t\in V(T)}\beta(t)=V(G),

•

for every
e\in E(G)
there exists
t\in V(T)
such that
e\subseteq\beta(t),
and

•

for every
v\in V(G)
the set
\{t\in V(T)\mid v\in\beta(t)\}
induces a connected subgraph of
T.

We refer to the vertices of
T
as the
nodes
of the tree decomposition and to the sets
\beta(t)
as its
bags
.
For every
t\in V(T)
the sets in
\{\beta(d)\cap\beta(t)\mid dt\in E(T)\}
are called the
adhesions
of the node
t.

The
width
of
(T,\beta)
is defined as
\max_{t\in V(T)}|\beta(t)|-1
and its
adhesion
is defined as
\max_{t\in V(T)}\max_{A\in\{\beta(d)\cap\beta(t)\penalty\ \mid\penalty\ dt\in E(T)\}}|A|.

The
treewidth
of
G,
denoted by
\mathsf{tw}(G),
is defined to be the minimum width over all tree decompositions of
G.

Now let
\mathcal{H}
be any proper minor-closed graph class
22
22

22

Please note that the definition can also be extended to any graph class
\mathcal{H},
but in this paper we are only interested in minor-closed graph classes.
.
A tree decomposition
(T,\beta)
for a graph
G
is called an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition
if for every leaf
d\in V(T)
it holds that
G[\beta(d)\setminus A_{d}]\in\mathcal{H}
where
A_{d}
is the unique adhesion set of
d.

The
width
of an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition
(T,\beta)
is defined as

\displaystyle\max\penalty\ \{0\}

\displaystyle\cup\{|\beta(t)|-1\penalty\ \!\mid\!\penalty\ t\in V(T)\text{ is not a leaf of }T\}

\displaystyle\cup\{|A_{d}|-1\penalty\ \!\mid\!\penalty\ d\in V(T)\text{ is a leaf with adhesion set }A_{d}\}.

Where we consider
\emptyset
to be the adhesion set of the node
d
if
d
is the unique vertex of
T.

We begin with the simple observation that
\mathcal{H}
-tree decompositions allow for a purely decomposition-based definition of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed graph class.
Then for every graph
G
it holds that
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G)
is equal to the minimum width over all
\mathcal{H}
-tree decompositions of
G.

Proof.

We first show that there always exists an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition of width
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G).

To see this let
X\subseteq V(G)
be a set of vertices such that every component of
G-X
belongs to
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathsf{tw}(\mathsf{torso}(G,X))
is minimum over all choices for such a set
X.

Let
G^{+}\coloneqq\mathsf{torso}(G,X)
and let
(T^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
be a tree decomposition of minimum width for
G^{+}.

It follows that the width of
(T^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
equals
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G).

Let
J
be any component of
G-X,
then
N(J)
is a clique of
G^{+}.

Hence, there exists a node
t_{J}\in V(T^{\prime})
such that
N(J)\subseteq\beta(t_{J}).

Therefore, for every such component
J
we may introduce a new vertex
d_{J}
adjacent to
t_{J}
and declare the set
V(J)\cup N(J)
to be the bag of
d_{J}.

Let
(T^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})
be the resulting tree decomposition of
G.

Suppose there exists some leaf
d\in V(T^{\prime\prime})
such that
G[\beta(d)\setminus A_{d}]\notin\mathcal{H}.

This means, in particular, that
d\in V(T^{\prime}).

For each of these leaves
d
we introduce a new vertex
d^{\prime}
adjacent to
d
and declare its bag to also be
\beta(d)
which implies that
|\beta(d^{\prime})|-1=|\beta(d)|-1\leq\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G).

It follows that the resulting decomposition, let us call it
(T,\beta),
is now an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition for
G.

Moreover, its width is exactly the width of
(T^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}).

For the reverse inequality let
(T,\beta)
be an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition of
G
of minimum width, say
k.

In case
T
consists of a single vertex we must have
G\in\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G)=0=k.

Hence, we may assume that
T
has more than one vertex.
Let
L
be the set of all leaves of
T.

Then, for every leaf
d\in L
let
J_{d}\coloneqq G[\beta(d)\setminus A_{d}]
and let
F\coloneqq\bigcup_{d\in L}V(J_{d}).

Finally, let
(T^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
be obtained from
(T,\beta)
by deleting, for each
d\in L,
the set
V(J_{d})
from
\beta(d)
and setting
T^{\prime}\coloneqq T.

Notice that for each
d\in L
and every component
K
of
J_{d}
it holds that
N(K)\subseteq A_{d}.

Hence,
(T,\beta^{\prime})
is indeed a tree decomposition of
\mathsf{torso}(G,V(G)\setminus F).

Thus, the
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth of
G
is at most
k
as desired.
∎

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\varnothing}
be the graph class containing only the empty graph
K_{0}
and observe that
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{H}_{\varnothing})=\{K_{1}\}.

It is also easy to observe that
\mathcal{H}_{\varnothing}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}
is the same as
{\sf tw}.

According to the next observation, excluding
K_{1}
defines an equivalent parameter as when excluding any
\leq
-antichain containing some planar graph.

Recall that due to the Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour
[
62
]
, a proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
has bounded treewidth if and only if
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
contains a planar graph.

Observation 11.2
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed graph class.
Then
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}\sim\mathsf{tw}
if and only if
\mathsf{obs}(\mathcal{H})
contains a planar graph.

Proof.

To see this, notice that by
Lemma 11.1
it suffices to consider
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition of small width since any tree decomposition of bounded width can easily be turned into a
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition by duplicating all leaves.
Let
G
be some graph with
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G)\leq k
and let
(T,\beta)
be an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition of minimum width of
G.

Now let
d\in V(T)
be some leaf with adhesion set
A_{d}.

Then
G_{d}\coloneqq G[\beta(d)\setminus A_{d}]\in\mathcal{H}
which means that
\mathsf{tw}(G_{d})\leq h_{\mathcal{H}}
by the Grid Theorem
[
62
]
.
We may now replace
d
by a whole tree decomposition of width at most
h_{\mathcal{H}}
for
G_{d},
then add
A_{d}
to every bag of this decomposition and finally join an arbitrary node of its tree to the ancestor of
d
in
T.

The result of performing this operation for every leaf of
T
is a tree decomposition of width at most
k+h_{\mathcal{H}}
for
G.

∎

11.2
Strict
\mathcal{H}
-brambles

To provide good lower bounds for
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth we adapt the notion of brambles to our setting.
The basic ideas can be traced back to the papers by Reed but we adapt the proof ideas of Adler, Gottlob, and Grohe
[
1
]
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed graph class
23
23

23

Notice that, as in the previous section, we only define these notions for minor-closed graph classes due to our setting, but they work the same way for general graph classes. For
Lemma 11.3
to hold, one should assume just hereditarity though.
.
Let
G
be a graph.
A
strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble
in
G
is a collection
\mathcal{B}
of connected subgraphs of
G
such that

1.

V(B^{\prime})\cap V(B)\neq\emptyset,
and

2.

\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{H}=\emptyset.

A
hitting set
for
\mathcal{B}
is a set
S\subseteq V(G)
such that
V(B)\cap S\neq\emptyset
for all
B\in\mathcal{B}.

The
order
of
\mathcal{B}
is then defined as the minimum size of a hitting set for
\mathcal{B}.

Finally, we define the
strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble number
of
G,
denoted by
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{sbn}(G),
to be the maximum order of a strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble in
G.

Note that the notion of strict brambles is a special case of the classic and more general notion of brambles that provides an exact min-max duality in relation to the treewidth of a graph (see
[
74
]
).
The relation between strict brambles and classic brambles, as well as precise dualities for strict brambles, have also been studied in the literature (see
[
43
,
61
,
6
,
46
]
).

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed graph class.
Then for every graph
G
it holds that
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{sbn}(G)\leq\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G)+1.

Proof.

Let
k\coloneqq\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G)+1
and suppose towards a contradiction that
G
contains a strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble
\mathcal{B}
of order at least
k+1.

Notice that for every set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
k
there most exist at least one member
B
of
\mathcal{B}
such that
S\cap V(B)=\emptyset.

Moreover, there exists a unique component
K_{S}
of
G-S
such that for every
B\in\mathcal{B}
that avoids
S
we have that
B\subseteq K
since the union of any two members of
\mathcal{B}
is connected.

Now, notice that for every edge
t_{1}t_{2}\in E(T)
there exists a unique
i\in[2]
such that the union of the bags in the unique subtree
T_{t_{i}}
of
T
which is rooted at
t_{i}
and avoids
t_{3-i}
contains the vertex set of the component
K_{\beta(t_{1})\cap\beta(t_{2})}
as defined above.
This is simply because
|\beta(t_{1})\cap\beta(t_{2})|\leq k
while
\mathcal{B}
is of order at least
k+1.

Let us call
t_{i}
the
dominant
endpoint of
t_{1}t_{2}.

This observation induces an orientation
\vec{T}
of
T
where each edge is oriented towards its dominant endpoint.

Suppose there exists a node
t\in V(T)
such that
t
is not the dominant endpoint of at least two of its incident edges, say
td_{1}
and
td_{2}.

Since the unions of the bags of the two trees
T_{d_{i}}
must intersect in a subset of
\beta(t)
it follows that for each
i\in[2],
the bags in the subtree
T_{d_{i}}
must contain the vertex set of some element
B_{i}
that avoids
\beta(t).

Since
V(B_{1})\cap V(B_{2})\neq\emptyset
this is impossible and thus for each node of
T
there exists at most one incident edge for which the node is not dominant.

Thus, there must exist some node
t
of
T
which is not dominant for any of its incident edges, i.e.,
t
is a sink of
\vec{T}.

In this case, none of the subtrees rooted at a neighbor of
t
can contain the vertex set of a member of
\mathcal{B}
which avoids
\beta(t).

Hence,
\beta(t)
is a hitting set for
\mathcal{B}
which contradicts our assumptions.
∎

Similar to strict
\mathcal{H}
-brambles we may define a more local variant which also poses as an obstruction to
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}.

Highly linked sets.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed class.
Let also
G
be a graph,
X\subseteq V(G)
be a set of vertices, and
k\in\mathbb{N}.

We say that the set
X
is
k
-
\mathcal{H}
-linked in
G
if for every
S\subseteq V(G),
where
|S|\leq k,
there exists a component
J_{S}
of
G-S
such that
J_{S}\not\in\mathcal{H}
and
|V(J)\cap X|>\frac{2}{3}|X|.

As before, we associate a graph parameter with the notion of
\mathcal{H}
-linked sets.
For every graph
G
let
\mathcal{H}
-
\mathsf{link}(G)
denote the maximum
k
such that there exists a
k
-
\mathcal{H}
-linked set in
G.

Lemma 11.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed graph class.
For every graph
G
it holds that
2\cdot\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{link}(G)\leq\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{sbn}(G).

Proof.

Let
G
be a graph and
X
be a
2k
-
\mathcal{H}
-linked set on
G
for some positive integer
k.

We define the following collection of subgraphs:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{X}\coloneqq\{J_{S}\mid S\subseteq V(G)\text{ and }|S|\leq k\}.

Moreover, we claim that
\mathcal{B}_{X}
is a strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble of order
k
in
G.

To see this we simply have to check the axioms of strict
\mathcal{H}
-brambles.

First notice that, by definition of
\mathcal{H}
-linked sets,
B\notin\mathcal{H}
for all
B\in\mathcal{B}_{X}.

Moreover, every such
B
is connected.
This takes care of the second axiom.

For the first axiom let
J_{S_{1}},J_{S_{2}}\in\mathcal{B}_{X}
be two distinct members of our collection.
Notice that
|S_{1}\cup S_{2}|\leq 2k.

Thus, there also exists a component
J_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}
of
G-S_{1}-S_{2}
such that
J_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\notin\mathcal{H}
and
|V(J_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\cap X)|>\frac{2}{3}|X|.

It follows that
V(J_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}})\cap V(J_{S_{i}})\neq\emptyset
for both
i\in[2].

In fact, this means
J_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\subseteq J_{S_{i}}
for both
i\in[2]
and therefore
V(J_{S_{1}})\cap V(J_{S_{2}})\neq\emptyset
as desired.

Finally, to see that
\mathcal{B}_{X}
is of order
k
as claimed simply notice that, by definition, for every set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
k
there exists an element
J_{S}\in\mathcal{B}_{X}
which fully avoids
S.

∎

Finally, we complete the circle by showing that the absence of
k
-
\mathcal{H}
-linked sets implies that
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}
is bounded.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed graph class.
For every graph
G
it holds that
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G)\leq 4\cdot\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{link}(G)+4.

Proof.

Let
k\coloneqq\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{link}(G)+1,
then
G
does not contain a
k
-
\mathcal{H}
-linked set.

We prove the following claim by induction on
|G|-|X|.

The assertion will then follow immediately.

Inductive Claim.

Let
G
be a graph with no
k
-
\mathcal{H}
-linked set.
For every set
X\subseteq V(G)
with
|X|\leq 3k+1
there exist an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition
(T,\beta)
of width at most
4k
and a leaf
d\in V(T)
such that
X\subseteq\beta(d)
and
|\beta(d)|\leq 4k.

Let
X\subseteq V(G)
be any set of size at most
3k+1.

In case
|X|<3k+1
we either have that
|G|<3k+1
in which case we are immediately done, or may pick an arbitrary vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus X
and apply the induction hypothesis to
X\cup\{v\}.

Hence, we may assume
|X|=3k+1.

In the following, we define the desired
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition step by step.
First let us introduce a node
d_{0}
and set
\beta(d_{0})\coloneqq X.

Our goal is to make
d_{0}
into a leaf of the final decomposition.
To meet the requirements of
\mathcal{H}
-tree decompositions this means we should introduce a node
d_{1}
adjacent to
d_{0}
and also set
\beta(d_{1})\coloneqq X.

This way we know that
G[\beta(d_{0})\setminus(\beta(d_{0})\cap\beta(d_{1}))]\in\mathcal{H}.

For each component
G^{\prime}
of
G-X
we now introduce a new vertex
t_{G^{\prime}}
adjacent to
d_{1}.

In the following, we describe how to extend this construction using our induction hypothesis.

Towards this goal, let us fix
G^{\prime}
to be the subgraph of
G
induced by a single component of
G-X
together with
X.

Now, since
X
is not
k
-
\mathcal{H}
-linked there exists a set
S\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
of size at most
k
such that one of the following is true.

Case 1

There exists a component
J_{S}
of
G^{\prime}-S
such that
|V(J_{S})\cap X|>\frac{2}{3}|X|
and
J_{S}\in\mathcal{H},
or

Case 2

for every component
J
of
G^{\prime}-S
it holds that
|V(J)\cap X|\leq\frac{2}{3}|X|\leq 2k+\frac{1}{3}\leq 2k.

In either case we will set
\beta(t_{G^{\prime}-X})\coloneqq X\cup S.

Notice that this means that
|\beta(t_{G^{\prime}-X})|\leq 4k+1
which still fits our bounds.

Case 1.

For each component
J
of
G^{\prime}-S
introduce a vertex
t_{J}
adjacent to
t_{G^{\prime}-X}
and set
\beta(t_{J})\coloneqq(V(J)\cap X)\cup S.

Notice that for the component
J_{S}
as defined above this means
|\beta(t_{J_{S}})|\leq 4k+1
and for each other component
J
of
G^{\prime}-S
we have that
|V(J)\cap X|<\frac{1}{3}|X|\leq k
implying
|\beta(t_{J})|\leq 2k.

We then introduce a new node
d_{J_{S}}
adjacent to
t_{J_{S}}
and set
\beta(d_{J_{S}})\coloneqq V(J_{S})\cup S.

By definition of
J_{S}
we have that
G[\beta(d_{J_{S}})\setminus\beta(t_{J_{S}})]\in\mathcal{H}
and thus we are fine leaving
d_{J_{S}}
as a leaf of our decomposition.
For every other component
J
of
G^{\prime}-S
we may now apply the induction hypothesis with
X_{J}\coloneqq S\cup(X\cap V(J))
to obtain an
\mathcal{H}
-tree decomposition
(T_{J},\beta_{J})
of
G[V(J)\cup S]
of width at most
4k
such that there exists a leaf
t_{J,S}\in V(T_{J})
with
X_{J}\subseteq\beta(t_{J,S})
and
|\beta(t_{J,S})|\leq 4k.

We extend out partially defined decomposition with the tree
T_{j}
and the bags
\beta_{J}
and introduce the edge
t_{J,S}t_{J}.

With this, we are done with the first case.

Case 2.

For each component
J
of
G^{\prime}-S
introduce a vertex
t_{J}
adjacent to
t_{G^{\prime}-X}
and set
\beta(t_{J})\coloneqq(V(J)\cap X)\cup S.

This time we may observe that
|\beta(t_{J})|\leq k+\frac{2}{3}|X|\leq 3k.

For each
J,
if there exists a vertex
v\in J-X
we introduce a vertex
t^{\prime}_{J}
adjacent to
t_{J},
pick such a vertex
v
arbitrarily and set
\beta(t^{\prime}_{J})\coloneqq\beta(t_{J})\cup\{v\}.

If there does not exist such a vertex
v
we still introduce the node
t^{\prime}_{J}
but in this case we have that
V(J)\subseteq X
and thus we may simply set
\beta(t^{\prime}_{J})\coloneqq\beta(t_{J})
and declare it a leaf.
In this case, it follows that
G[\beta(t^{\prime}_{J})\setminus\beta(t_{J})]\in\mathcal{H}
and thus
t^{\prime}_{J}
may remain a leaf for the rest of the construction.

Hence, from now on we may assume that we were able to choose the vertex
v.

By calling the induction hypothesis for
G[S\cup V(J)]
and
X_{J}\coloneqq\beta(t^{\prime}_{J})
and then merging our partial decomposition with the resulting decomposition for
G[S\cup V(J)]
in the same way as we did in
Case 1
, we also complete this second case and thereby the proof by using
Lemma 11.1
.
∎

To conclude, we summarize the results of
Lemma 11.3
,
Lemma 11.4
, and
Lemma 11.5
.

Corollary 11.6
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed graph class.
Then, for every graph
G
we have

\displaystyle 2\cdot\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{link}(G)-1\leq\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{sbn}(G)-1\leq\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(H)\leq 4\cdot\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{link}(G)+4.

The above corollary implies that
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{link},

\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{sbn}
and
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{link}
are equivalent with linear gap function.

11.3
The lower bound

With the lemmas from the two previous subsections, we are now ready to obtain the first link between our walloids and the notion of
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth.
Indeed, we can prove an even stronger statement.
For this purpose, we need a special version of brambles.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a proper minor-closed class.
A strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble
\mathcal{B}
in a graph
G
is said to be a
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
-
\mathcal{H}
-bramble
if no vertex of
G
appears in more than two members of
\mathcal{B}.

Notice that, if
\mathcal{B}
is a
\frac{1}{2}
-
\mathcal{H}
-bramble of order
k
which is not of order
k+1,
then
|\mathcal{B}|\leq 2k.

Lemma 11.7
.

There exists a function
f_{\ref{lemma_half_integral_Brambles}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every graph
Z,
every embedding pair
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)}
and every positive integer
k,

\mathscr{W}_{f_{\ref{lemma_half_integral_Brambles}}(|Z|,k)}^{\mathbf{p}}
contains a
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
-
{\sf excl}(Z)
-bramble of order
k.

Moreover,
f_{\ref{lemma_half_integral_Brambles}}(|Z|,k)\in\mathcal{O}_{|Z|}(k).

Figure 31
:
A sketch for constructing a
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
-
\mathsf{excl}(Z)
-bramble in a
\mathbf{p}
-design of large order for some
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{P}^{(Z)}.

Proof.

By
Lemma 10.3
we know that for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
we have that
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{\mathbf{p}}
is a minor of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathbf{p}}_{5t}.

Moreover,
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{f_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)}
contains
Z
as a minor by
Lemma 10.4
.

Now consider the design
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{2k(f_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)+1)}.

Similar to how walloids contain half-integral packings of themselves (recall
Figure 9
), we may construct such a half-integral packing of extensions of
Z
within
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{2k(f_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)+1)}.

Indeed, by the choice of the order we can fit a half-integral packing of
2k
copies of extensions of
Z
into
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{2k(f_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)+1)}
such that any two of these extensions intersect.
See
Figure 31
for an illustration.
Let
\mathcal{B}
be the collection of these extensions.
Since the packing is half-integral and
|\mathcal{B}|=2k,
the order of the resulting bramble is at lest
k.

The explicit construction of
\mathcal{B}
is tedious, but it suffices to see that, given a fixed extension of
Z
embedded in
\mathscr{D}^{\mathbf{p}}_{f_{\ref{designs_contain_graphs}}(|Z|)}
we can take this extension and “stretch” and “shift” it slightly as depicted in
Figure 31
to achieve the half-integral packing.
∎

As an immediate consequence of
Lemma 11.7
and
Lemma 11.3
, we obtain that for every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
the parametric graphs in the family
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
act as witnesses for large
\mathcal{H}
-treewidth when found as a minor.
In particular, this immediately implies
Theorem 1.4
.

Corollary 11.8
.

For every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
there exists a function
f^{\mathcal{H}}_{\ref{cor_walloid_lower_bound}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every positive integer
k
and every graph
G,
if there exists
\mathscr{W}=\langle W_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\rangle\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
G
contains
\mathscr{W}_{f^{\mathcal{H}}_{\ref{cor_walloid_lower_bound}}(k)}
as a minor, then
\mathcal{H}\text{-}\mathsf{tw}(G)\geq k.

In particular, this means that every strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble of large enough order implies the existence of a
\frac{1}{2}
-
\mathcal{H}
-bramble of large order.

Corollary 11.9
.

For every proper minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{H}
there exists a function
f^{\mathcal{H}}_{\ref{cor_bramble_to_half_int_bramble}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every positive integer
k
and every graph
G,
if
G
contains a strict
\mathcal{H}
-bramble of order
f_{\ref{cor_bramble_to_half_int_bramble}}(k),
then
G
contains
\frac{1}{2}
-
\mathcal{H}
-bramble of order
k.

11.4
The upper bound

We are now ready to prove
Theorem 1.5
.
To do this we make use of
Theorem 9.2
to show that any graph which contains a highly
\mathcal{H}
-linked set must also contain a member of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
of large order.

In order to prove this statement we will need some additional notions from the theory of graph minors.

Of walls and well-linked sets.

Let
G
be a graph and
X\subseteq V(G)
be a vertex set.
A set
S\subseteq V(G)
is said to be a
balanced separator
for
X
if for every component
J
of
G-S
it holds that
|V(J)\cap X|\leq\frac{2}{3}|X|.

We say that
X
is
k
-well-linked
if there does not exist a balanced separator of size at most
k
for
X.

It is easy to see that well-linked sets form a special case of our
\mathcal{H}
-linked sets.
Indeed, they are a well-studied object acting as obstructions to treewidth.
Well-linked sets and walls both act as such obstructions and they relate to each other in a specific way which is usually encoded in the language of “tangles”.
Since we do not have any need for the notion of tangles in this paper we provide a slightly different definition here which can easily be seen to be equivalent by a reader familiar with these concepts.
See Section 3 of
[
77
]
for an in-depth discussion.

Let
k
and
r
be positive integers such that
k\geq r.

Let
G
be a graph
X
a
k
-well-linked set in
G
and
W
an
r
-wall in
G.

First notice that for every set
S\subseteq V(G)
of order at most
r-1
there exists a unique component
J_{W,S}
of
G-S
which contains an entire row and an entire column of
W.

Similarly, for every
S\subseteq V(G)
of order at most
k
there exists a unique component
J_{X,S}
such that
|V(J_{X,S})\cap X|>\frac{2}{3}|X|.

We say that
X
and
W

agree
if for every
S\subseteq V(G)
of order at most
r-1
we have that
J_{W,S}\subseteq J_{X,S}.

Theorem 11.10

(
[
77
]
)
.

Let
k\geq 3
be an integer.
There exists a universal positive constant
c\in\mathbb{N}
and an algorithm that, given a graph
G
and a
(36ck^{20}+3)
-well-linked set
X\subseteq V(G),
computes in time
\mathcal{O}_{k}(|G|^{2}|E(G)|\log(|G|))
a
k
-wall
W
such that
X
and
W
agree.

With this, we may now prove the main tool towards proving
Theorem 1.5
.

Lemma 11.11
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
